---
<!-- assumption-1: body_md -->
{ass:interpolation}
For almost every $u\in(0,1)$, $\pi_u$ has a density
$\rho_u\in W^{2,1}(\mathbf R)$. The derivative norms are measurable in $u$, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:averaged-M}
 M_1:=\int_0^1\left\lVert \rho_u'\right\rVert_1\,\mathrm d u<\infty,\qquad
 M_2:=\int_0^1\left\lVert \rho_u''\right\rVert_1\,\mathrm d u<\infty.
\end{equation}
<!-- end assumption-1 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
{lem:Taylor}
For $f\in C^3(\mathbf R)$ with bounded first three derivatives, set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Phi}
 \Phi_{f,x}\colon\left|\begin{aligned}
  \mathbf R&\longrightarrow \mathbf R\\
  y&\longmapsto \displaystyle\int_0^1(1-r)f''(x+ry)\,\mathrm d r
\end{aligned}\right..
\end{equation}
Then $f(x+y)-f(x)-yf'(x)=y^2\Phi_{f,x}(y)$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Phi-norm}
 \left\lVert \Phi_{f,x}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}}
 \leq\frac12\left\lVert f''\right\rVert_\infty+\frac16\left\lVert f'''\right\rVert_\infty.
\end{equation}
Consequently, the difference of the two jump terms in
\eqref{eq:terminal-operator} is bounded by the right-hand side of
\eqref{eq:Phi-norm} times $d_{\mathrm{FM}}(\mu_0,\mu_1)$.
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
For $x,y\in\mathbf R$, two applications of the fundamental theorem of calculus,
followed by Fubini's theorem, give
\begin{align*}
 f(x+y)-f(x)-yf'(x)
 &=y\int_0^1\bigl(f'(x+sy)-f'(x)\bigr)\,\mathrm d s\\
 &=y^2\int_0^1\int_0^sf''(x+ry)\,\mathrm d r\,\mathrm d s\\
 &=y^2\int_0^1(1-r)f''(x+ry)\,\mathrm d r\\
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:Phi}}{=}y^2\Phi_{f,x}(y).
\end{align*}
Differentiating in $y$ yields
\[
 \partial_y\Phi_{f,x}(y)=\int_0^1r(1-r)f'''(x+ry)\,\mathrm d r,
 \qquad
 \left\lVert \Phi_{f,x}\right\rVert_\infty\leq\frac12\left\lVert f''\right\rVert_\infty,
 \quad \operatorname{Lip}(\Phi_{f,x})\leq\frac16\left\lVert f'''\right\rVert_\infty.
\]
Thus, recalling that $\mu_i=y^2N_i$,
\begin{align*}
 \left|\int_\mathbf R y^2\Phi_{f,x}(y)(N_0-N_1)(\,\mathrm d y)\right|
 &=|\langle\Phi_{f,x},\mu_0-\mu_1\rangle|\\
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:metrics}}{\leq}\left\lVert \Phi_{f,x}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}}d_{\mathrm{FM}}(\mu_0,\mu_1)\\
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:Phi-norm}}{\leq}
 \left(\frac{\left\lVert f''\right\rVert_\infty}{2}
       +\frac{\left\lVert f'''\right\rVert_\infty}{6}\right)d_{\mathrm{FM}}(\mu_0,\mu_1).
\end{align*}
Both jump integrals converge absolutely because
$|y^2\Phi_{f,x}(y)|\leq y^2\left\lVert f''\right\rVert_\infty/2$.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
{lem:convolution}
Let $\rho\in W^{2,1}(\mathbf R)$ be a probability density with finite first moment.
For $\operatorname{Lip}(h)\leq1$, the function $q=h*\check\rho$ belongs to $C^3(\mathbf R)$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:conv-bounds}
 \left\lVert q'\right\rVert_\infty\leq1,\qquad
 \left\lVert q''\right\rVert_\infty\leq\left\lVert \rho'\right\rVert_1,\qquad
 \left\lVert q'''\right\rVert_\infty\leq\left\lVert \rho''\right\rVert_1.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Center $h$ at zero. Then $h\in W^{1,\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbf R)$,
$Dh\in L^\infty(\mathbf R)$, $\left\lVert Dh\right\rVert_\infty\leq1$, and
\[
 |q(x)|\leq\int_\mathbf R |x+y|\rho(y)\,\mathrm d y
 \leq |x|+\int_\mathbf R |y|\rho(y)\,\mathrm d y<\infty.
\]
For $k=0,1,2$, differentiation in distributions gives
\[
 D^{k+1}q=(Dh)*D^k\check\rho.
\]
Then Young's inequality, the bound $\left\lVert Dh\right\rVert_\infty\leq1$, and the change
of variables $x\mapsto-x$ yield
\[
 \left\lVert D^{k+1}q\right\rVert_\infty
 \leq\left\lVert Dh\right\rVert_\infty\left\lVert D^k\check\rho\right\rVert_1
 \leq\left\lVert D^k\check\rho\right\rVert_1=\left\lVert D^k\rho\right\rVert_1.
\]
If $a\to0$, each derivative satisfies
\[
 \sup_x|D^{k+1}q(x+a)-D^{k+1}q(x)|
 \leq\left\lVert Dh\right\rVert_\infty
       \left\lVert D^k\check\rho(\cdot+a)-D^k\check\rho\right\rVert_1\longrightarrow0.
\]
The weak derivatives are therefore continuous and agree with the
classical derivatives.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
{lem:interpolation}
Under \eqref{eq:add-int}, for every $h\in\mathcal S(\mathbf R)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:interpolation-identity}
 (Q_1-Q_0)h(x)=\int_0^1(\mathcal A_0-\mathcal A_1)Q_uh(x)\,\mathrm d u.
\end{equation}
The equality holds at every point. The operators $Q_u$ commute with translations,
spatial derivatives, and the terminal operators on these tests.
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Set $\delta\Psi=\Psi_0-\Psi_1$. Fourier transformation gives
$\widehat{Q_uh}=\widehat h e^{\psi_u}$ and
$\partial_u\widehat{Q_uh}=\delta\Psi\widehat h e^{\psi_u}$.
The identity $e^{iz}-1-iz=-z^2\int_0^1(1-r)e^{irz}\,\mathrm d r$ implies
\[
 |\Psi_i(\xi)|\leq|m_i||\xi|+\tfrac12(v_i+\mu_i(\mathbf R))|\xi|^2,
 \qquad \operatorname{Re}\psi_u\leq0.
\]
Since $\widehat h\in\mathcal S(\mathbf R)$,
$C(|\xi|+|\xi|^2)|\widehat h(\xi)|\in L^1(\mathbf R)$ dominates the
parameter derivative. Hence Fourier inversion, the fundamental theorem
of calculus in $u$, and Fubini's theorem give
\begin{align*}
 (Q_1-Q_0)h(x)
 &=\frac1{2\pi}\int_\mathbf R e^{ix\xi}\widehat h(\xi)
                  \bigl(e^{\Psi_0(\xi)}-e^{\Psi_1(\xi)}\bigr)\,\mathrm d\xi\\
 &=\int_0^1\frac1{2\pi}\int_\mathbf R
       e^{ix\xi}\widehat h(\xi)\delta\Psi(\xi)e^{\psi_u(\xi)}\,\mathrm d\xi\,\mathrm d u\\
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:terminal-operator}}{=}\int_0^1(\mathcal A_0-\mathcal A_1)Q_uh(x)\,\mathrm d u.
\end{align*}
Translations and derivatives commute with convolution. The same
quadratic bound lets us apply Fubini to each jump operator and proves
the remaining commutation identities.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
{thm:terminal}
Assume \eqref{eq:add-int} and Assumption~\ref{ass:interpolation}. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:terminal-bound}
 W_1(\lambda_0,\lambda_1)
 \leq |m_0-m_1|+\frac{M_1}{2}|v_0-v_1|
 +\left(\frac{M_1}{2}+\frac{M_2}{6}\right)d_{\mathrm{FM}}(\mu_0,\mu_1).
\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
For $h\in\mathcal S$ with $\operatorname{Lip}(h)\leq1$, put $q_u=Q_uh$.
Lemmas~\ref{lem:Taylor} and~\ref{lem:convolution} give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:calc-first-generator-bound}
 \begin{aligned}
 |(\mathcal A_0-\mathcal A_1)q_u(0)|
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:terminal-operator}}{\leq}
     |m_0-m_1|\left\lVert q_u'\right\rVert_\infty
     +\tfrac12|v_0-v_1|\left\lVert q_u''\right\rVert_\infty
     +|\langle\Phi_{q_u,0},\mu_0-\mu_1\rangle|\\
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:Phi-norm}}{\leq}|m_0-m_1|\left\lVert q_u'\right\rVert_\infty
     +\tfrac12|v_0-v_1|\left\lVert q_u''\right\rVert_\infty\\
 &\qquad+\left(\tfrac12\left\lVert q_u''\right\rVert_\infty
       +\tfrac16\left\lVert q_u'''\right\rVert_\infty\right)d_{\mathrm{FM}}(\mu_0,\mu_1)\\
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:conv-bounds}}{\leq}|m_0-m_1|
     +\tfrac12\left\lVert \rho_u'\right\rVert_1|v_0-v_1|\\
 &\qquad+\left(\tfrac12\left\lVert \rho_u'\right\rVert_1
       +\tfrac16\left\lVert \rho_u''\right\rVert_1\right)d_{\mathrm{FM}}(\mu_0,\mu_1).
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Integrating in $u$ therefore yields
\begin{align*}
 |\langle h,\lambda_0-\lambda_1\rangle|
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:interpolation-identity}}{\leq}
       \int_0^1|(\mathcal A_0-\mathcal A_1)q_u(0)|\,\mathrm d u\\
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:calc-first-generator-bound}}{\leq}|m_0-m_1|
       +\frac{|v_0-v_1|}{2}\int_0^1\left\lVert \rho_u'\right\rVert_1\,\mathrm d u\\
 &\qquad+d_{\mathrm{FM}}(\mu_0,\mu_1)\int_0^1
       \left(\frac{\left\lVert \rho_u'\right\rVert_1}{2}
             +\frac{\left\lVert \rho_u''\right\rVert_1}{6}\right)\,\mathrm d u\\
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:averaged-M}}{=}|m_0-m_1|+\frac{M_1}{2}|v_0-v_1|
       +\left(\frac{M_1}{2}+\frac{M_2}{6}\right)d_{\mathrm{FM}}(\mu_0,\mu_1).
\end{align*}
For a general centered Lipschitz test, Lemma~\ref{lem:approximation} provides
locally uniform approximations bounded by $|x|$. The finite terminal first
moments justify dominated convergence. Taking the supremum in
\eqref{eq:KR} completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
{cor:joint}
Under the assumptions of Theorem~\ref{thm:terminal}, define
$\Gamma_i:=v_i\delta_0+\mu_i$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:joint}
 W_1(\lambda_0,\lambda_1)
 \leq |m_0-m_1|+
 \left(\frac{M_1}{2}+\frac{M_2}{6}\right)d_{\mathrm{FM}}(\Gamma_0,\Gamma_1).
\end{equation}
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
The measures $\Gamma_i\in\mathcal M_f^+(\mathbf R)$ satisfy
\[
 \Phi_{f,x}(0)=\tfrac12f''(x),\qquad
 \mathcal A_i f(x)=m_i f'(x)+\langle\Phi_{f,x},\Gamma_i\rangle.
\]
In the preceding proof, replace the last two terms by this single pairing.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
{cor:Gaussian}
Under \eqref{eq:add-int}, suppose $v_0,v_1>0$.
Theorem~\ref{thm:terminal} and Corollary~\ref{cor:joint} hold with $M_1$ and $M_2$
replaced, respectively, by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:intro-constants}
 \frac{2\sqrt{2/\pi}}{\sqrt{v_0}+\sqrt{v_1}},\qquad
 \sqrt{\frac8{\pi e}}
 \begin{cases}
 (\log v_0-\log v_1)/(v_0-v_1),&v_0\ne v_1,\\
 v_0^{-1},&v_0=v_1.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
In particular, when $v_0=v_1=v>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:equal-Gaussian}
 W_1(\lambda_0,\lambda_1)
 \leq |m_0-m_1|+
 \left(\frac{\sqrt{2/\pi}}{2\sqrt v}+\frac{\sqrt{8/(\pi e)}}{6v}\right)d_{\mathrm{FM}}(\mu_0,\mu_1).
\end{equation}
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
Write $v_u=(1-u)v_1+uv_0$ and $\rho_u=g_{v_u}*\eta_u$ for a probability
law $\eta_u$. For $v_0\ne v_1$, Young's inequality, \eqref{eq:gauss-L1},
and the substitution $s=v_1+u(v_0-v_1)$ give
\begin{align*}
 M_1&\overset{\eqref{eq:averaged-M}}{=}\int_0^1\left\lVert g_{v_u}'*\eta_u\right\rVert_1\,\mathrm d u\\
 &\leq\int_0^1\left\lVert g_{v_u}'\right\rVert_1\,\mathrm d u
 \overset{\eqref{eq:gauss-L1}}{=}\sqrt{2/\pi}\int_0^1v_u^{-1/2}\,\mathrm d u\\
 &=
 \frac{2\sqrt{2/\pi}(\sqrt{v_0}-\sqrt{v_1})}{v_0-v_1}
 =\frac{2\sqrt{2/\pi}}{\sqrt{v_0}+\sqrt{v_1}},\\
 M_2&\overset{\eqref{eq:averaged-M}}{=}\int_0^1\left\lVert g_{v_u}''*\eta_u\right\rVert_1\,\mathrm d u\\
 &\leq\int_0^1\left\lVert g_{v_u}''\right\rVert_1\,\mathrm d u
 \overset{\eqref{eq:gauss-L1}}{=}\sqrt{8/(\pi e)}\int_0^1v_u^{-1}\,\mathrm d u\\
 &=
 \sqrt{\frac8{\pi e}}\,
       \frac{\log v_0-\log v_1}{v_0-v_1}.
\end{align*}
For $v_0=v_1$, the integrands are constant and the values are
$v_0^{-1/2}$ and $v_0^{-1}$.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- example-1: body_md -->
{ex:chronological}
Take $T=1$ and
$a_0(t)=2\mathbf 1_{(\frac{1}{2},1]}(t)$,
$a_1(t)=2\mathbf 1_{[0,\frac{1}{2}]}(t)$. Both terminal variances equal one.
The chronological mixture
\[
 V_t=\int_0^t a_0(s)\,\mathrm d s+\int_t^1a_1(s)\,\mathrm d s
\]
satisfies
\[
 V_t=\begin{cases}1-2t,&0\leq t\leq\tfrac12,\\
                  2t-1,&\tfrac12\leq t\leq1,
       \end{cases}
 \qquad \min_{0\leq t\leq1}V_t=0,\qquad v_0=v_1=1.
\]
Thus Corollary~\ref{cor:Gaussian} applies even though the chronological variance vanishes.
If $m_0=m_1$ and $N_0=N_1$, then $\Psi_0=\Psi_1$ and
$\lambda_0=\lambda_1$.
<!-- end example-1 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
{ex:replacement}
Compare the terminal triplets $(m,v,N+K)$ and $(m,v+\delta,N)$, where
$v>0$, $K$ is supported on $|y|\leq r$, and
$\delta=\int_\mathbf R y^2K(\,\mathrm d y)<\infty$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:replacement-third}
 W_1(\lambda_0,\lambda_1)
 \leq\frac{\sqrt{8/(\pi e)}}{6v}\int_\mathbf R |y|^3K(\,\mathrm d y)
 \leq\frac{\sqrt{8/(\pi e)}}{6v}\,r\delta.
\end{equation}
If also $\int_\mathbf R y^3K(\,\mathrm d y)=0$, in particular if $K$ is symmetric, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:replacement-fourth}
 W_1(\lambda_0,\lambda_1)
 \leq\frac{\left\lVert g_v'''\right\rVert_1}{24}\int_\mathbf R y^4K(\,\mathrm d y)
 \leq\frac{\left\lVert g_v'''\right\rVert_1}{24}\,r^2\delta.
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal D=\mathcal A_0-\mathcal A_1$. Since $\delta=\int_\mathbf R y^2K(\,\mathrm d y)$,
Taylor's formula gives
\begin{align*}
 \mathcal Df(x)
 &=
   \int_\mathbf R \left[f(x+y)-f(x)-yf'(x)-\frac{y^2}{2}f''(x)\right]K(\,\mathrm d y)\\
 &=\frac12\int_\mathbf R y^3\int_0^1(1-r)^2f'''(x+ry)\,\mathrm d r\,K(\,\mathrm d y).\\
\intertext{Then the triangle inequality and $\int_0^1(1-r)^2\,\mathrm d r=1/3$ imply}
 \left\lVert \mathcal Df\right\rVert_\infty
 &\leq\frac{\left\lVert f'''\right\rVert_\infty}{2}
          \int_\mathbf R |y|^3K(\,\mathrm d y)\int_0^1(1-r)^2\,\mathrm d r
 =\frac{\left\lVert f'''\right\rVert_\infty}{6}\int_\mathbf R |y|^3K(\,\mathrm d y).
\end{align*}
If $\int_\mathbf R y^3K(\,\mathrm d y)=0$, one more Taylor term gives
\[
 \mathcal Df(x)=\frac16\int_\mathbf R y^4\int_0^1(1-r)^3f^{(4)}(x+ry)
                  \,\mathrm d r\,K(\,\mathrm d y),\qquad
 \left\lVert \mathcal Df\right\rVert_\infty\leq\frac{\left\lVert f^{(4)}\right\rVert_\infty}{24}\int_\mathbf R y^4K(\,\mathrm d y).
\]
Every interpolated law has a Gaussian factor of variance $v$, hence
\[
 \left\lVert (Q_uh)'''\right\rVert_\infty\leq\left\lVert g_v''\right\rVert_1=\frac{\sqrt{8/(\pi e)}}{v},\qquad
 \left\lVert (Q_uh)^{(4)}\right\rVert_\infty\leq\left\lVert g_v'''\right\rVert_1
 \quad\text{when }\operatorname{Lip}(h)\leq1.
\]
Apply Lemmas~\ref{lem:interpolation} and~\ref{lem:approximation}, then use
$|y|^3\leq r y^2$ and $y^4\leq r^2y^2$ on the support of $K$.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
{thm:response}
Assume \eqref{eq:add-int} and $\min(v_0,v_1)\geq v_*>0$.
Write $\delta m=m_0-m_1$, $\kappa=\Gamma_0-\Gamma_1$ and
$d=\left\lVert \kappa\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}^*}$. For $j=1,\ldots,5$, set
$K_j=\left\lVert g_{v_*}^{(j)}\right\rVert_1$. For each one-Lipschitz test $h$, define the first-order response at the second law by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:response-definition}
 \mathcal R(h):=\delta m\,(Q_0h)'(0)
                +\int_\mathbf R \Phi_{Q_0h,0}(y)\kappa(\,\mathrm d y).
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:response-bound}
 \begin{split}
 \sup_{\operatorname{Lip}(h)\leq1}\bigl|\mathbf E\left[h(X_T^0)\right]-\mathbf E\left[h(X_T^1)\right]-\mathcal R(h)\bigr|
 \leq\frac12\bigg[&K_1|\delta m|^2
       +\left(K_2+\frac{K_3}{3}\right)|\delta m|d\\
       &+\left(\frac{K_3}{4}+\frac{K_4}{6}
                            +\frac{K_5}{36}\right)d^2\bigg].
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Put $\mathcal D=\mathcal A_0-\mathcal A_1$ and $\delta\Psi=\Psi_0-\Psi_1$.
For Schwartz tests, $\partial_u^j\widehat{Q_uh}=(\delta\Psi)^j
\widehat h e^{\psi_u}$, $j=1,2$, with integrable majorants from the polynomial bounds.
Taylor's formula in $u$ and Fourier inversion give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:second-interpolation}
 (Q_1-Q_0)h=\mathcal DQ_0h+
                   \int_0^1(1-u)\mathcal D^2Q_uh\,\mathrm d u.
\end{equation}
Define $\mathcal B_\kappa f(x)=\langle\Phi_{f,x},\kappa\rangle$.
For smooth $f$ with bounded derivatives through order six,
$\mathcal D=\delta m D+\mathcal B_\kappa$, all these operators commute, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:calc-first-insertion-bound}
 \left\lVert \mathcal B_\kappa D^jf\right\rVert_\infty
 \overset{\eqref{eq:Phi-norm}}{\leq}d\left(\tfrac12\left\lVert f^{(j+2)}\right\rVert_\infty+
                  \tfrac16\left\lVert f^{(j+3)}\right\rVert_\infty\right),\qquad0\leq j\leq3.
\end{equation}
In particular, applying this estimate twice gives
\begin{align*}
 \left\lVert \mathcal B_\kappa^2f\right\rVert_\infty
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:calc-first-insertion-bound}}{\leq}
 d\left(\tfrac12\left\lVert \mathcal B_\kappa f''\right\rVert_\infty
                     +\tfrac16\left\lVert \mathcal B_\kappa f'''\right\rVert_\infty\right)\\
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:calc-first-insertion-bound}}{\leq}
 \frac{d^2}{2}\left(\tfrac12\left\lVert f^{(4)}\right\rVert_\infty
                              +\tfrac16\left\lVert f^{(5)}\right\rVert_\infty\right)
 +\frac{d^2}{6}\left(\tfrac12\left\lVert f^{(5)}\right\rVert_\infty
                              +\tfrac16\left\lVert f^{(6)}\right\rVert_\infty\right)\\
 &=d^2\left(\tfrac14\left\lVert f^{(4)}\right\rVert_\infty
              +\tfrac16\left\lVert f^{(5)}\right\rVert_\infty
              +\tfrac1{36}\left\lVert f^{(6)}\right\rVert_\infty\right).
\end{align*}
Using $\mathcal D^2f=(\delta m)^2f''+
2\delta m\mathcal B_\kappa f'+\mathcal B_\kappa^2f$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:D2-bound}
 \begin{split}
 \left\lVert \mathcal D^2f\right\rVert_\infty\leq{}&|\delta m|^2\left\lVert f''\right\rVert_\infty
 +|\delta m|d\left(\left\lVert f'''\right\rVert_\infty+\tfrac13\left\lVert f^{(4)}\right\rVert_\infty\right)\\
 &+d^2\left(\tfrac14\left\lVert f^{(4)}\right\rVert_\infty+
              \tfrac16\left\lVert f^{(5)}\right\rVert_\infty+\tfrac1{36}\left\lVert f^{(6)}\right\rVert_\infty\right).
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
Since $\pi_u=g_{v_*}*\eta_u$ with $\eta_u\in\mathcal P_1$,
$\left\lVert D^{j+1}Q_uh\right\rVert_\infty\leq K_j$ for $1\leq j\leq5$ and
$\operatorname{Lip}(h)\leq1$. Explicitly,
\[
 K_j=v_*^{-j/2}\mathbf E\left[|\operatorname{He}_j(G)|\right]<\infty,\qquad
 \operatorname{He}_j(x)=(-1)^je^{x^2/2}\frac{\,\mathrm d^j}{\,\mathrm d x^j}e^{-x^2/2}.
\]
Consequently, using $\int_0^1(1-u)\,\mathrm d u=1/2$, the remainder of the
first response satisfies
\begin{align*}
 \big|(Q_1-Q_0)h(0)-\mathcal R(h)\big|
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:second-interpolation}}{\leq}
       \int_0^1(1-u)\left\lVert \mathcal D^2Q_uh\right\rVert_\infty\,\mathrm d u\\
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:D2-bound}}{\leq}\int_0^1(1-u)\bigg[
      K_1|\delta m|^2+
       \left(K_2+\frac{K_3}{3}\right)|\delta m|d
       +\left(\frac{K_3}{4}+\frac{K_4}{6}
                  +\frac{K_5}{36}\right)d^2\bigg]\,\mathrm d u\\
 &=
       \frac12\bigg[K_1|\delta m|^2+
       \left(K_2+\frac{K_3}{3}\right)|\delta m|d
       +\left(\frac{K_3}{4}+\frac{K_4}{6}
                  +\frac{K_5}{36}\right)d^2\bigg].
\end{align*}
For the approximants of Lemma~\ref{lem:approximation}, $Dh_n\to Dh$ weakly-$*$
in $L^\infty$: distributional convergence and the uniform derivative
bound imply convergence against all $L^1$ tests. After Gaussian convolution,
each derivative of $Q_0h_n$ therefore converges. The uniform derivative
bounds and the finiteness of $|\kappa|$ let us pass to the limit in the
response integral. The finite terminal first moments let us do the same
for the expectations.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
{cor:metric-response}
Under Theorem~\ref{thm:response}, let $p=\rho_0$ be the density of the second law
$\lambda_1$. Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:response-cdf}
 H_{\delta m,\kappa}(z)=-\delta m\,p(z)
   +\int_\mathbf R\int_0^1(1-r)p'(z-ry)\,\mathrm d r\,\kappa(\,\mathrm d y).
\end{equation}
Then $H_{\delta m,\kappa}\in L^1(\mathbf R)$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:metric-response}
 \begin{split}
 \left|W_1(\lambda_0,\lambda_1)-\left\lVert H_{\delta m,\kappa}\right\rVert_1\right|
 \overset{\eqref{eq:response-bound}}{\leq}\frac12\bigg[&K_1|\delta m|^2
 +\left(K_2+\frac{K_3}{3}\right)|\delta m|\left\lVert \kappa\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}^*}\\
 &+\left(\frac{K_3}{4}+\frac{K_4}{6}+\frac{K_5}{36}\right)
                         \left\lVert \kappa\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}^*}^2\bigg].
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
In particular, on an admissible affine path with
$(m_\varepsilon,\Gamma_\varepsilon)=(m_1,\Gamma_1)
 +\varepsilon(\dot m,\dot\Gamma)$ and Gaussian variance bounded below,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:directional-response}
 W_1(\lambda_\varepsilon,\lambda_1)
 =|\varepsilon|\left\lVert H_{\dot m,\dot\Gamma}\right\rVert_1+O(\varepsilon^2).
\end{equation}
The leading coefficient is positive whenever
$(\dot m,\dot\Gamma)\ne(0,0)$.
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Fubini gives $\left\lVert H_{\delta m,\kappa}\right\rVert_1\leq|\delta m|+
\left\lVert p'\right\rVert_1\left\lVert \kappa\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}/2<\infty$.
Integration by parts in \eqref{eq:response-definition} gives
$\mathcal R(h)=-\int_\mathbf R h'(z)H_{\delta m,\kappa}(z)\,\mathrm d z$.
Lemma~\ref{lem:approximation} extends the identity to Lipschitz tests.
Every measurable $w$ with $|w|\leq1$ is the derivative of
$h(x)=\int_0^xw(s)\,\mathrm d s$. Hence $L^1$--$L^\infty$ duality gives
\[
 \sup_{\operatorname{Lip}(h)\leq1}|\mathcal R(h)|
 =\sup_{\operatorname{Lip}(h)\leq1}\left|\int_\mathbf R h'(z)H_{\delta m,\kappa}(z)\,\mathrm d z\right|
 =\sup_{\left\lVert w\right\rVert_\infty\leq1}\left|\int_\mathbf R w(z)H_{\delta m,\kappa}(z)\,\mathrm d z\right|
 =\left\lVert H_{\delta m,\kappa}\right\rVert_1.
\]
The reverse triangle inequality and Theorem~\ref{thm:response} prove
\eqref{eq:metric-response}. Homogeneity gives \eqref{eq:directional-response}.
Positive definiteness is the case $J=0$ of Proposition~\ref{prop:tangent-quotient},
whose independent Fourier proof uses only the response formula.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
{thm:transport-response}
Under \eqref{eq:transport-lift}, define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:transport-cdf}
 \mathsf T_p(\alpha,\eta,J)(x)={}-\alpha p(x) +\int_\mathbf R \!\int_0^1(1-r)p'(x-ry)\,\mathrm d r\,\eta(\,\mathrm d y) -\int_\mathbf R \!\int_0^1r(1-r)p''(x-ry)\,\mathrm d r\,J(\,\mathrm d y).
\end{equation}
Then $\mathsf T_p\in L^1(\mathbf R)$ and, for every admissible $\varepsilon$,
\begin{align}
 \left\lVert F_{\lambda_\varepsilon}-F_\lambda
                -\varepsilon\mathsf T_p\right\rVert_1&\leq C\varepsilon^2,
                      \label{eq:transport-L1}\\
 \left|W_1(\lambda_\varepsilon,\lambda)
                  -|\varepsilon|\left\lVert \mathsf T_p\right\rVert_1\right|
                  &\leq C\varepsilon^2.\label{eq:transport-W1}
\end{align}
Here $K_j=\left\lVert g_{v_*}^{(j)}\right\rVert_1$ and one may take
\begin{equation}\label{eq:transport-constant}
 \begin{split}
 C={}&\frac12\left[K_1|\alpha|^2+
       \left(K_2+\frac{K_3}{3}\right)|\alpha|d_1+
       \left(\frac{K_3}{4}+\frac{K_4}{6}+\frac{K_5}{36}\right)d_1^2\right]
       +\frac{K_3}{24}M_2(\Pi),\\
 d_1={}&\left\lVert \eta\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}^*}+\int_{\mathbf R^2}|z|\Pi(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d z).
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
The result applies even when $\varepsilon\mapsto\Gamma_\varepsilon$
fails to be differentiable in $\mathrm{BL}^*$.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Put $\kappa_\varepsilon=\Gamma_\varepsilon-\Gamma$.
For $\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}}\leq1$, the triangle inequality gives
\begin{align*}
 |\langle f,\kappa_\varepsilon\rangle|
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:transport-lift}}{=}
   \left|\int_{\mathbf R^2}[f(y+\varepsilon z)-f(y)]\Pi(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d z)
                   +\varepsilon\langle f,\eta\rangle\right|\\
 &\leq\int_{\mathbf R^2}|f(y+\varepsilon z)-f(y)|\Pi(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d z)
       +|\varepsilon|\,|\langle f,\eta\rangle|\\
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:metrics}}{\leq}|\varepsilon|
     \left(\int_{\mathbf R^2}|z|\Pi(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d z)+\left\lVert \eta\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}^*}\right),
\end{align*}
so $\left\lVert \kappa_\varepsilon\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}^*}\leq|\varepsilon|d_1$.
For $q(x)=\int_\mathbf R h(x+w)p(w)\,\mathrm d w$ and $\operatorname{Lip}(h)\leq1$, differentiating the
Taylor field with respect to its jump variable gives
\[
 \partial_y\Phi_{q,0}(y)=\int_0^1r(1-r)q'''(ry)\,\mathrm d r,\qquad
 \left\lVert \partial_y^2\Phi_{q,0}\right\rVert_\infty
                  \leq\frac1{12}\left\lVert q^{(4)}\right\rVert_\infty\leq K_3/12.
\]
Taylor's formula gives, pointwise in $(y,z)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:calc-first-displacement-Taylor}
 \Phi_{q,0}(y+\varepsilon z)-\Phi_{q,0}(y)
 -\varepsilon z\partial_y\Phi_{q,0}(y)
 =\varepsilon^2z^2\int_0^1(1-t)
       \partial_y^2\Phi_{q,0}(y+t\varepsilon z)\,\mathrm d t.
\end{equation}
Integrating against $\Pi$, using
$\left\lVert \partial_y^2\Phi_{q,0}\right\rVert_\infty\leq K_3/12$, and then
$\int_0^1(1-t)\,\mathrm d t=1/2$, we obtain
\begin{align*}
 &\left|\langle\Phi_{q,0},\kappa_\varepsilon\rangle
 -\varepsilon\left(\int_\mathbf R \Phi_{q,0}(y)\eta(\,\mathrm d y)
                  +\int_\mathbf R \partial_y\Phi_{q,0}(y)J(\,\mathrm d y)\right)\right|\\
 &\qquad\overset{\eqref{eq:calc-first-displacement-Taylor}}{\leq}
 \varepsilon^2\int_{\mathbf R^2}z^2\int_0^1(1-t)
       \left|\partial_y^2\Phi_{q,0}(y+t\varepsilon z)\right|\,\mathrm d t\,\Pi(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d z)\\
 &\qquad\leq
       \frac{\varepsilon^2K_3}{12}\int_{\mathbf R^2}z^2\Pi(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d z)\int_0^1(1-t)\,\mathrm d t
       =\frac{\varepsilon^2K_3M_2(\Pi)}{24}.
\end{align*}
Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:response} to $\lambda_\varepsilon,\lambda$. Its remainder
is at most
\[
 \frac{\varepsilon^2}{2}\left[K_1|\alpha|^2+
 \left(K_2+\frac{K_3}{3}\right)|\alpha|d_1+
 \left(\frac{K_3}{4}+\frac{K_4}{6}+\frac{K_5}{36}\right)d_1^2\right].
\]
The resulting first-order functional is
\[
 \alpha q'(0)+\int_\mathbf R \Phi_{q,0}(y)\eta(\,\mathrm d y)+
             \int_\mathbf R \partial_y\Phi_{q,0}(y)J(\,\mathrm d y)
       \overset{\eqref{eq:transport-cdf}}{=}-\int_\mathbf R h'(x)\mathsf T_p(\alpha,\eta,J)(x)\,\mathrm d x.
\]
Indeed, for smooth $h$ and $j=1,2,3$,
\[
 q^{(j)}(t)=(-1)^{j-1}\int_\mathbf R h'(x)p^{(j-1)}(x-t)\,\mathrm d x.
\]
Substituting into $\Phi_{q,0}$ and $\partial_y\Phi_{q,0}$ gives the three
terms in \eqref{eq:transport-cdf}. Lemma~\ref{lem:approximation} extends the
identity to Lipschitz $h$. Moreover,
\[
 \left\lVert \mathsf T_p\right\rVert_1\leq|\alpha|
       +\tfrac12\left\lVert p'\right\rVert_1\left\lVert \eta\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}
       +\tfrac16\left\lVert p''\right\rVert_1\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}<\infty.
\]
Since $F_{\lambda_\varepsilon}-F_\lambda\in L^1$ and every bounded
measurable $w$, $|w|\leq1$, is the derivative of
$h(x)=\int_0^xw(t)\,\mathrm d t$, the uniform test remainder is precisely
\eqref{eq:transport-L1}. The identity $W_1=\left\lVert F_{\lambda_\varepsilon}-F_\lambda\right\rVert_1$
and the reverse triangle inequality give \eqref{eq:transport-W1}.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
{prop:tangent-quotient}
For finite signed $\eta,J$ and a Gaussian-smoothed infinitely divisible
reference density $p$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:tangent-kernel}
 \mathsf T_p(\alpha,\eta,J)=0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \alpha=0\ \text{and}\ \eta-D_yJ=0
                         \ \text{in }\mathcal D'.
\end{equation}
Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{eq:observable-norm}
 \mathfrak T=\mathbf R\times\{\eta-D_yJ:\eta,J\in\mathcal M_f(\mathbf R)\},\qquad
 \| (\alpha,\tau)\|_\lambda=\left\lVert \mathsf T_p(\alpha,\eta,J)\right\rVert_1
\end{equation}
defines a norm, independent of the representation of $\tau$.
Along \eqref{eq:transport-lift} it is the exact first-order $W_1$ speed.
If $\zeta\in\mathcal P_1$ is infinitely divisible, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:tangent-contraction}
 \| (\alpha,\tau)\|_{\lambda*\zeta}
                  \leq\| (\alpha,\tau)\|_\lambda.
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
For $(\xi,y)\in\mathbf R^2$, define
\[
 k(\xi,y)=-\xi^2\int_0^1(1-r)e^{ir\xi y}\,\mathrm d r
 =\begin{cases}(e^{i\xi y}-1-i\xi y)/y^2,&y\ne0,\\
               -\xi^2/2,&y=0,
   \end{cases}
\]
and set
\[
 \dot\Psi(\xi)=i\alpha\xi+\int_\mathbf R k(\xi,y)\eta(\,\mathrm d y)
                                  +\int_\mathbf R \partial_y k(\xi,y)J(\,\mathrm d y).
\]
The kernels satisfy $|k|\leq\xi^2/2$ and
$|\partial_y k|\leq|\xi|^3/6$. Fourier transformation of
\eqref{eq:transport-cdf} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:tangent-Fourier}
 i\xi\widehat{\mathsf T_p}(\xi) \overset{\eqref{eq:transport-cdf}}{=}\dot\Psi(-\xi)\widehat p(\xi),\quad \dot\Psi''(\xi) = -\int_\mathbf R e^{i\xi y}\eta(\,\mathrm d y)-i\xi\int_\mathbf R e^{i\xi y}J(\,\mathrm d y) \overset{\eqref{eq:transport-tangent}}{=}-\widehat\tau(-\xi).
\end{equation}
Indeed $\partial_\xi^2k=-e^{i\xi y}$ and
$\partial_\xi^2\partial_yk=-i\xi e^{i\xi y}$. These and the first
$\xi$ derivatives are uniformly bounded in $y$ on compact $\xi$ intervals.
We can therefore differentiate under the integrals because $\eta,J$ are finite.
The second expression is the negative of the Fourier transform of the
tempered distribution $\tau$, taken with a positive sign in the exponent.
Since $\widehat p(\xi)=e^{\Psi(-\xi)}\ne0$, the identity
$\mathsf T_p=0$ implies $\dot\Psi(-\xi)=0$ for all $\xi\ne0$ and hence
for all $\xi$ by continuity. Thus
\[
 \alpha=\frac{\dot\Psi'(0)}i=0,\qquad
 \widehat\tau(-\xi)=-\dot\Psi''(\xi)=0,
\]
and Fourier uniqueness in $\mathcal S'(\mathbf R)$ gives $\tau=0$.
Conversely, if $\alpha=0$ and $\tau=0$, then
\[
 \dot\Psi''=0,\qquad \dot\Psi(0)=0,\qquad \dot\Psi'(0)=i\alpha=0,
\]
so $\dot\Psi=0$. Equation~\eqref{eq:tangent-Fourier} gives
$\widehat{\mathsf T_p}(\xi)=0$ for $\xi\ne0$. Continuity gives the value
at zero, and Fourier uniqueness in $L^1$ gives $\mathsf T_p=0$.
This characterization of the kernel shows that the norm is independent
of the representation and positive definite. The other norm properties
follow from linearity.
Finally, Fubini's theorem, Young's inequality, and $\zeta(\mathbf R)=1$ give
\[
 \| (\alpha,\tau)\|_{\lambda*\zeta} \overset{\eqref{eq:observable-norm}}{=}\left\lVert \mathsf T_{p*\zeta}(\alpha,\eta,J)\right\rVert_1 =\left\lVert \mathsf T_p(\alpha,\eta,J)*\zeta\right\rVert_1 \leq\left\lVert \mathsf T_p(\alpha,\eta,J)\right\rVert_1\,\zeta(\mathbf R) \overset{\eqref{eq:observable-norm}}{=}\| (\alpha,\tau)\|_\lambda.
\]
This proves \eqref{eq:tangent-contraction}.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
{prop:reference-continuity}
Let $p,q$ be Gaussian-smoothed infinitely divisible reference densities.
For any representation $\tau=\eta-D_yJ$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reference-continuity}
 \big|\| (\alpha,\tau)\|_p-\| (\alpha,\tau)\|_q\big| \leq{}|\alpha|\left\lVert p-q\right\rVert_1 +\tfrac12\left\lVert \eta\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}\left\lVert p'-q'\right\rVert_1 +\tfrac16\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}\left\lVert p''-q''\right\rVert_1.
\end{equation}
Here the density subscript denotes its law. If $p=g_v*\rho$ and
$q=g_v*\sigma$, with $\rho,\sigma\in\mathcal P_1$ and $v>0$, then the right-hand
side is at most
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reference-W1}
 \left(|\alpha|K_1+\tfrac12\left\lVert \eta\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}K_2+
             \tfrac16\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}K_3\right)W_1(\rho,\sigma),
 \qquad K_j=\left\lVert g_v^{(j)}\right\rVert_1.
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Subtract the two formulas in \eqref{eq:transport-cdf}. Fubini,
translation invariance of $L^1$, and the reverse triangle inequality
give the first bound.
For a coupling $(U,V)$ of $\rho,\sigma$ and $j=0,1,2$, Fubini's theorem,
the fundamental theorem of calculus, and translation invariance of the
$L^1$ norm give
\begin{align*}
 \left\lVert p^{(j)}-q^{(j)}\right\rVert_1
 &\leq\mathbf E\left[\left\lVert g_v^{(j)}(\cdot-U)-g_v^{(j)}(\cdot-V)\right\rVert_1\right]\\
 &\leq\mathbf E\left[|U-V|\int_0^1
       \left\lVert g_v^{(j+1)}(\cdot-V-r(U-V))\right\rVert_1\,\mathrm d r\right]\\
 &=
       \left\lVert g_v^{(j+1)}\right\rVert_1\mathbf E\left[|U-V|\right]
 =K_{j+1}\mathbf E\left[|U-V|\right].
\end{align*}
Taking the infimum over couplings proves \eqref{eq:reference-W1}.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
{thm:atomic-geometry}
For $k\in\mathbf N$, let
\[
 \theta=(m,v,w_1,\ldots,w_k,a_1,\ldots,a_k)\in\mathbf R^{2k+2}
\]
parameterize laws with
\[
 \Gamma_\theta=v\delta_0+\sum_{j=1}^kw_j\delta_{a_j},
 \qquad v>0,\quad w_j>0,
\]
where the nonzero $a_j$ are distinct and ordered. On every compact subset
of this parameter domain there are $c,C,r>0$ such that, whenever both
parameters are in the subset and $|\theta'-\theta|\leq r$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:chart-bilip}
 c|\theta'-\theta|\leq W_1(\lambda_{\theta'},\lambda_\theta)
                         \leq C|\theta'-\theta|.
\end{equation}
For a $C^2$ curve $s\mapsto\theta_s$ in this domain, set
\[
 \eta_s=\dot v_s\delta_0+\sum_j\dot w_{j,s}\delta_{a_{j,s}},\qquad
 J_s=\sum_jw_{j,s}\dot a_{j,s}\delta_{a_{j,s}},\qquad
 \tau_s=\eta_s-D_yJ_s.
\]
Its Wasserstein metric speed and length are
\begin{equation}\label{eq:metric-length}
 |\dot\lambda_s|_{W_1}=\| (\dot m_s,\tau_s)\|_{\lambda_s},\qquad
 \operatorname{Len}_{W_1}(\lambda_{[0,1]})
        =\int_0^1\| (\dot m_s,\tau_s)\|_{\lambda_s}\,\mathrm d s.
\end{equation}
In particular the integral bounds the endpoint distance.
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Fix a compact parameter set $K$ in the stated domain. Its Gaussian
variances and masses are bounded below by positive constants, and its atom positions
are bounded, separated, and bounded away from zero. For
$u=\theta'-\theta\in\mathbf R^{2k+2}$, write
\[
 \eta_\theta(u)=u_v\delta_0+\sum_ju_{w_j}\delta_{a_j},\qquad
 J_\theta(u)=\sum_jw_ju_{a_j}\delta_{a_j},\qquad
 \mathcal T_\theta u=\mathsf T_{p_\theta}
                   (u_m,\eta_\theta(u),J_\theta(u))\in L^1(\mathbf R).
\]
The exact difference between the characteristics can be written as
\[
 \Gamma_{\theta'}-\Gamma_\theta
 =u_v\delta_0+\sum_jw_j(\delta_{a_j+u_{a_j}}-\delta_{a_j})
             +\sum_ju_{w_j}\delta_{a_j}+r_u,
 \qquad
 r_u=\sum_ju_{w_j}(\delta_{a_j+u_{a_j}}-\delta_{a_j}).
\]
Since $\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}}\leq1$ implies $\operatorname{Lip}(f)\leq1$, the elementary inequality
$2|ab|\leq a^2+b^2$ gives
\[
 \left\lVert r_u\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}^*} \overset{\eqref{eq:metrics}}{=}\sup_{\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}}\leq1} \left|\sum_ju_{w_j}[f(a_j+u_{a_j})-f(a_j)]\right| \leq\sum_j|u_{w_j}|\,|u_{a_j}| \leq \frac12\sum_j(|u_{w_j}|^2+|u_{a_j}|^2) \leq\frac{|u|^2}{2}.
\]
Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:transport-response} with $\varepsilon=|u|$, velocities
$u_{a_j}/|u|$, and its weak-remainder extension. Uniformly for
$\theta,\theta'\in K$ and $|u|\leq1$,
\[
 \left\lVert F_{\lambda_{\theta'}}-F_{\lambda_\theta}
                     -\mathcal T_\theta u\right\rVert_1\leq B_K|u|^2
\]
for a finite constant $B_K$. The case $u=0$ is immediate.
The direction $(u_m,\eta_\theta(u)-D_yJ_\theta(u))$ is zero only when
$u=0$. Indeed, smooth test functions supported near each atom can
prescribe their value and first derivative there, proving linear
independence of $\delta_0,\delta_{a_j},D_y\delta_{a_j}$. Also, $w_j>0$.
By Proposition~\ref{prop:tangent-quotient}, $\left\lVert \mathcal T_\theta u\right\rVert_1>0$ for
$u\ne0$. The map $(\theta,u)\mapsto\mathcal T_\theta u$ is continuous
in $L^1$: the jump intensities $w_j/a_j^2$ and compensation shifts
$-w_j/a_j$ vary continuously, their compound-Poisson laws vary in
$W_1$, and Gaussian derivatives and their translations vary in $L^1$.
Compactness therefore gives
\[
 a_K:=\min_{\theta\in K,\,|u|=1}\left\lVert \mathcal T_\theta u\right\rVert_1>0,
 \qquad A_K:=\max_{\theta\in K,\,|u|=1}\left\lVert \mathcal T_\theta u\right\rVert_1<\infty.
\]
Consequently
\[
 (a_K-B_K|u|)|u|\leq W_1(\lambda_{\theta'},\lambda_\theta)
                   \leq(A_K+B_K|u|)|u|.
\]
Choose $r\leq1$ with $B_Kr\leq a_K/2$ to obtain
\eqref{eq:chart-bilip}.
For a $C^2$ curve, the same expansion gives
\[
 \frac{F_{\lambda_{s+t}}-F_{\lambda_s}}{t}
 \longrightarrow\mathcal T_{\theta_s}\dot\theta_s
 =\mathsf T_{p_s}(\dot m_s,\eta_s,J_s)\quad\text{in }L^1.
\]
The derivative is continuous, so the anchored curve
$s\mapsto F_{\lambda_s}-F_{\lambda_0}$ is $C^1$ in $L^1$.
By definition,
\[
 |\dot\lambda_s|_{W_1}=\lim_{t\to0}
       \frac{W_1(\lambda_{s+t},\lambda_s)}{|t|},\qquad
 \operatorname{Len}_{W_1}(\lambda_{[0,1]})
 =\sup_{0=s_0<\cdots<s_n=1}
             \sum_{j=1}^nW_1(\lambda_{s_j},\lambda_{s_{j-1}}).
\]
The CDF isometry gives the speed. The fundamental theorem of calculus
bounds every partition sum by the integral of the derivative norm.
Riemann sums and continuity of the derivative give the reverse bound.
This proves \eqref{eq:metric-length}.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
{thm:lift-length}
Let $(E,\mathcal E,\gamma)$ be a finite positive measure space and
$w_0\in L^\infty(E,\mathcal E,\gamma;\mathbf R)$,
$y_0:E\to\mathbf R$ be measurable, and, for $s\in[0,1]$, set
\[
 w_s=w_0+\int_0^s a_t\,\mathrm d t,\qquad
 y_s=y_0+\int_0^s b_t\,\mathrm d t,
 \quad a,b\in L^1([0,1];L^\infty(\gamma)),
\]
where the integrals are Bochner integrals and $w_s\geq0$.
Let $m_s,v_s$ be absolutely continuous, $v_s\geq v_*>0$, and let
$\lambda_s$ have mean $m_s$ and joint characteristic
$\Gamma_s=v_s\delta_0+(y_s)_\#(w_s\gamma)$.
For almost every $s$, put
\[
 \eta_s=\dot v_s\delta_0+(y_s)_\#(a_s\gamma),\qquad
 J_s=(y_s)_\#(w_sb_s\gamma),\qquad \tau_s=\eta_s-D_yJ_s.
\]
Then $F_{\lambda_s}-F_{\lambda_0}$ is absolutely continuous in $L^1$, with
Bochner derivative $\mathsf T_{p_s}(\dot m_s,\eta_s,J_s)$ almost everywhere.
In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:general-length}
 |\dot\lambda_s|_{W_1}=\|(\dot m_s,\tau_s)\|_{\lambda_s}
 \quad\text{a.e.},\qquad
 \operatorname{Len}_{W_1}(\lambda_{[0,1]})
 =\int_0^1\|(\dot m_s,\tau_s)\|_{\lambda_s}\,\mathrm d s.
\end{equation}
The integral bounds $W_1(\lambda_0,\lambda_1)$.
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
Set
\[
 W=\sup_s\left\lVert w_s\right\rVert_\infty
 \leq\left\lVert w_0\right\rVert_\infty+\int_0^1\left\lVert a_t\right\rVert_\infty\,\mathrm d t<\infty,
 \qquad M_\gamma=\gamma(E).
\]
For $0\leq s<t\leq1$ and $\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}}\leq1$, decompose the integrand
and use $w_s\leq W$ together with the Bochner integral representations:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:calc-first-lift-increments}
 \begin{aligned}
 \left|\int_E\bigl(w_tf(y_t)-w_sf(y_s)\bigr)\,\mathrm d\gamma\right|
 &=
 \left|\int_E\bigl[(w_t-w_s)f(y_t)
               +w_s(f(y_t)-f(y_s))\bigr]\,\mathrm d\gamma\right|\\
 &\quad\leq
       \int_E|w_t-w_s|\,\mathrm d\gamma+\int_Ew_s|y_t-y_s|\,\mathrm d\gamma\\
 &\quad\leq
       M_\gamma\bigl(\left\lVert w_t-w_s\right\rVert_\infty
                         +W\left\lVert y_t-y_s\right\rVert_\infty\bigr)\\
 &\quad\leq
       M_\gamma\int_s^t(\left\lVert a_r\right\rVert_\infty+W\left\lVert b_r\right\rVert_\infty)\,\mathrm d r.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Thus, with $K_j=\left\lVert g_{v_*}^{(j)}\right\rVert_1$ and $L=K_1/2+K_2/6$,
absolute continuity of $m$ and $v$ yields
\begin{align*}
 W_1(\lambda_t,\lambda_s)
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:joint}}{\leq}|m_t-m_s|+L\left\lVert \Gamma_t-\Gamma_s\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}^*}\\
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:calc-first-lift-increments}}{\leq}
       |m_t-m_s|+L|v_t-v_s|
       +LM_\gamma\int_s^t
                   (\left\lVert a_r\right\rVert_\infty+W\left\lVert b_r\right\rVert_\infty)\,\mathrm d r\\
 &\leq\int_s^t\left[|\dot m_r|+L|\dot v_r|
         +LM_\gamma(\left\lVert a_r\right\rVert_\infty+W\left\lVert b_r\right\rVert_\infty)\right]\,\mathrm d r.
\end{align*}
The anchored curve $s\mapsto F_{\lambda_s}-F_{\lambda_0}$ is therefore
absolutely continuous in $L^1$.
At a common Lebesgue point $s$ of the four velocities, write
$\Delta w=w_{s+u}-w_s$ and $\Delta y=y_{s+u}-y_s$. Bochner
differentiation gives
\[
 \left\lVert \Delta w-ua_s\right\rVert_\infty=o(|u|),\qquad
 \left\lVert \Delta y-ub_s\right\rVert_\infty=o(|u|),\qquad
 m_{s+u}-m_s=u\dot m_s+o(|u|),\quad
 v_{s+u}-v_s=u\dot v_s+o(|u|).
\]
For a centered one-Lipschitz $h$, let
$q_s(x)=\int_\mathbf R h(x+z)p_s(z)\,\mathrm d z$ and $\Phi=\Phi_{q_s,0}$.
Its first two $y$ derivatives are bounded uniformly over $h$ and $s$.
The exact decomposition
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 (w_s+\Delta w)\Phi(y_s+\Delta y)-w_s\Phi(y_s)
 ={}&\Delta w\,\Phi(y_s)+w_s\Phi'(y_s)\Delta y+R,\\
 |R|\leq{}&\left\lVert \Phi'\right\rVert_\infty|\Delta w|\,|\Delta y|
       +\tfrac12\left\lVert \Phi''\right\rVert_\infty w_s|\Delta y|^2
 \end{aligned}
\]
has $\int_E|R|\,\mathrm d\gamma=O(u^2)$. Therefore, using the pushforward
definitions of $\eta_s$ and $J_s$, the affine response between the actual
endpoints satisfies, uniformly over these tests,
\begin{align*}
 (m_{s+u}-m_s)q_s'(0)+\langle\Phi,\Gamma_{s+u}-\Gamma_s\rangle
 &=
       u\left[\dot m_s q_s'(0)+\dot v_s\Phi(0)
       +\int_E\bigl(a_s\Phi(y_s)+w_sb_s\Phi'(y_s)\bigr)\,\mathrm d\gamma\right]
       +o(|u|)\\
 &\quad=
       u\left[\dot m_s q_s'(0)+\int_\mathbf R \Phi(y)\eta_s(\,\mathrm d y)
                           +\int_\mathbf R \Phi'(y)J_s(\,\mathrm d y)\right]+o(|u|)\\
 &\quad\overset{\eqref{eq:transport-cdf}}{=}
       -u\int_\mathbf R h'(x)\mathsf T_{p_s}(\dot m_s,\eta_s,J_s)(x)\,\mathrm d x
       +o(|u|).
\end{align*}
Since $|m_{s+u}-m_s|+\left\lVert \Gamma_{s+u}-\Gamma_s\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}^*}=O(|u|)$,
Theorem~\ref{thm:response} adds an $O(u^2)$ remainder. Duality gives
\[
 F_{\lambda_{s+u}}-F_{\lambda_s}
 =uV_s+o_{L^1}(|u|),\qquad
 V_s:=\mathsf T_{p_s}(\dot m_s,\eta_s,J_s).
\]
Moreover,
\[
 \left\lVert V_s\right\rVert_1\leq|\dot m_s|
 +\frac{K_1}{2}\bigl(|\dot v_s|+M_\gamma\left\lVert a_s\right\rVert_\infty\bigr)
 +\frac{K_2}{6}M_\gamma W\left\lVert b_s\right\rVert_\infty\in L^1(0,1).
\]
As an almost-everywhere limit of strongly measurable $L^1$ difference
quotients, $V$ is strongly measurable, hence Bochner integrable.
For any $\ell\in(L^1)^*=L^\infty$, the scalar absolutely continuous
function $s\mapsto\ell(F_{\lambda_s}-F_{\lambda_0})$ has derivative
$\ell(V_s)$ almost everywhere. Its fundamental theorem of calculus,
followed by separation by $L^\infty$, yields
\[
 F_{\lambda_t}-F_{\lambda_s}=\int_s^tV_r\,\mathrm d r\quad\text{in }L^1.
\]
The length of an indefinite Bochner integral equals the integral of
the derivative norm. The triangle inequality gives the upper bound.
Approximating $V$ by step functions gives the lower bound. The CDF isometry now proves \eqref{eq:general-length}.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
{thm:two-jet}
Assume $M_3<\infty$ and the uniform lower bound $v_*>0$ on the Gaussian variance in
\eqref{eq:transport-lift}. Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:two-jet}
 T_1=\mathsf T_p(\alpha,\eta,J),\qquad
 T_2=\mathsf Q_p(S)+\tfrac12\mathcal A^*T_1.
\end{equation}
Both coefficients belong to $L^1$. For every admissible
$|\varepsilon|\leq1$, we have
\begin{align}
 \left\lVert F_{\lambda_\varepsilon}-F_\lambda
                -\varepsilon T_1-\varepsilon^2T_2\right\rVert_1
                         &\leq C_3|\varepsilon|^3,
                                      \label{eq:two-jet-L1}\\
 \left|W_1(\lambda_\varepsilon,\lambda)
                -\left\lVert \varepsilon T_1+\varepsilon^2T_2\right\rVert_1\right|
                         &\leq C_3|\varepsilon|^3.
                                      \label{eq:two-jet-W1}
\end{align}
With $K_j=\left\lVert g_{v_*}^{(j)}\right\rVert_1$, we may take
\begin{equation}\label{eq:two-jet-constant}
 \begin{aligned}
 C_3={}&\frac{K_4M_3}{120}
 +M_2\left(\frac{|\alpha|K_4}{24}
          +\frac{\left\lVert \eta\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}K_5}{48}
          +\frac{M_1K_6}{144}\right)\\
 &+\frac{|\alpha|^3K_2}{6}
   +\frac{|\alpha|^2\left\lVert \eta\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}K_3}{4}
 +\left(\frac{|\alpha|^2M_1}{12}
         +\frac{|\alpha|\left\lVert \eta\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}^2}{8}\right)K_4\\
 &+\left(\frac{|\alpha|\left\lVert \eta\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}M_1}{12}
         +\frac{\left\lVert \eta\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}^3}{48}\right)K_5\\
 &+\left(\frac{|\alpha|M_1^2}{72}
         +\frac{\left\lVert \eta\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}^2M_1}{48}\right)K_6
   +\frac{\left\lVert \eta\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}M_1^2K_7}{144}
   +\frac{M_1^3K_8}{1296}.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Only the displacement velocities require a third moment.
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
Let $\Psi,\Psi_\varepsilon$ be the terminal characteristic exponents,
and let $Q_u$ have exponent $(1-u)\Psi+u\Psi_\varepsilon$, $0\leq u\leq1$.
The exact generator difference is
\[
 \mathcal D_\varepsilon f(x)=\varepsilon\alpha f'(x)
 +\varepsilon\int_\mathbf R\Phi_{f,x}(y)\eta(\,\mathrm d y)
 +\int_{\mathbf R^2}[\Phi_{f,x}(y+\varepsilon z)-\Phi_{f,x}(y)]\Pi(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d z).
\]
Set
\[
 \mathcal Bf(x)=\frac12\int_\mathbf R\partial_y^2\Phi_{f,x}(y)S(\,\mathrm d y).
\]
For $f\in C_b^{j+2}(\mathbf R)$,
\[
 \partial_y^j\Phi_{f,x}(y)
 =\int_0^1r^j(1-r)f^{(j+2)}(x+ry)\,\mathrm d r,
 \qquad \int_0^1r^j(1-r)\,\mathrm d r=\frac1{(j+1)(j+2)}.
\]
Taylor's formula in $y$ and the total variation bound for $\eta$ therefore give
\begin{align}
 \left\lVert \mathcal D_\varepsilon f\right\rVert_\infty
 &\leq|\varepsilon|\left(|\alpha|\left\lVert f'\right\rVert_\infty
       +\frac{\left\lVert \eta\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}}{2}\left\lVert f''\right\rVert_\infty
       +\frac{M_1}{6}\left\lVert f'''\right\rVert_\infty\right),\nonumber\\
 \left\lVert \mathcal Af\right\rVert_\infty
 &\leq|\alpha|\left\lVert f'\right\rVert_\infty
       +\frac{\left\lVert \eta\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}}{2}\left\lVert f''\right\rVert_\infty
       +\frac{M_1}{6}\left\lVert f'''\right\rVert_\infty,\nonumber\\
 \left\lVert (\mathcal D_\varepsilon-\varepsilon\mathcal A)f\right\rVert_\infty
 &\leq\frac{\varepsilon^2M_2}{24}\left\lVert f^{(4)}\right\rVert_\infty,
 \label{eq:calc-second-operator}\\
 \left\lVert (\mathcal D_\varepsilon-\varepsilon\mathcal A-\varepsilon^2\mathcal B)f\right\rVert_\infty
 &\leq\frac{|\varepsilon|^3M_3}{120}\left\lVert f^{(5)}\right\rVert_\infty.\nonumber
\end{align}
By translation invariance, these operators commute with spatial derivatives
and with each other. Hence
\begin{align*}
 \mathcal D_\varepsilon^2-\varepsilon^2\mathcal A^2
 &=(\mathcal D_\varepsilon-\varepsilon\mathcal A)
   (\mathcal D_\varepsilon+\varepsilon\mathcal A).\\
\intertext{Then the quadratic remainder bound and the first-order estimates yield}
 \left\lVert (\mathcal D_\varepsilon^2-\varepsilon^2\mathcal A^2)f\right\rVert_\infty
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:calc-second-operator}}{\leq}
 \frac{\varepsilon^2M_2}{24}
 \left\lVert (\mathcal D_\varepsilon+\varepsilon\mathcal A)f^{(4)}\right\rVert_\infty\\
 &\leq|\varepsilon|^3M_2\left(
 \frac{|\alpha|}{12}\left\lVert f^{(5)}\right\rVert_\infty+
 \frac{\left\lVert \eta\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}}{24}\left\lVert f^{(6)}\right\rVert_\infty+
 \frac{M_1}{72}\left\lVert f^{(7)}\right\rVert_\infty\right).
\end{align*}
Applying the first operator bound three times also gives
\begin{align*}
 \left\lVert \mathcal D_\varepsilon^3f\right\rVert_\infty
 \leq |\varepsilon|^3\bigg[&
 |\alpha|^3\left\lVert f^{(3)}\right\rVert_\infty
 +\frac{3|\alpha|^2\left\lVert \eta\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}}{2}\left\lVert f^{(4)}\right\rVert_\infty\\
 &+\left(\frac{|\alpha|^2M_1}{2}
       +\frac{3|\alpha|\left\lVert \eta\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}^2}{4}\right)
                                      \left\lVert f^{(5)}\right\rVert_\infty\\
 &+\left(\frac{|\alpha|\left\lVert \eta\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}M_1}{2}
       +\frac{\left\lVert \eta\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}^3}{8}\right)
                                      \left\lVert f^{(6)}\right\rVert_\infty\\
 &+\left(\frac{|\alpha|M_1^2}{12}
       +\frac{\left\lVert \eta\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}^2M_1}{8}\right)
                                      \left\lVert f^{(7)}\right\rVert_\infty\\
 &+\frac{\left\lVert \eta\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}M_1^2}{24}\left\lVert f^{(8)}\right\rVert_\infty
 +\frac{M_1^3}{216}\left\lVert f^{(9)}\right\rVert_\infty\bigg].
\end{align*}
For $h\in\mathcal S(\mathbf R)$, differentiating the interpolated Fourier multiplier
three times and using Taylor's integral formula gives
\[
 (Q_1-Q_0)h=\mathcal D_\varepsilon Q_0h
 +\frac12\mathcal D_\varepsilon^2Q_0h
 +\frac12\int_0^1(1-u)^2\mathcal D_\varepsilon^3Q_uh\,\mathrm d u.
\]
The multiplier derivatives are polynomially bounded, so
$\widehat h\in\mathcal S(\mathbf R)$ justifies differentiation and Fourier inversion.
Every interpolation law has a Gaussian factor of variance $v_*$.
For $\operatorname{Lip}(h)\leq1$ and $j\geq1$,
\[
 \left\lVert (Q_uh)^{(j)}\right\rVert_\infty
 \leq\left\lVert h'\right\rVert_\infty\left\lVert g_{v_*}^{(j-1)}\right\rVert_1
 \leq\left\lVert g_{v_*}^{(j-1)}\right\rVert_1.
\]
Subtracting $\varepsilon\mathcal AQ_0h+
\varepsilon^2(\mathcal B+\mathcal A^2/2)Q_0h$, applying the three
bounds above, and using $\int_0^1(1-u)^2\,\mathrm d u=1/3$ gives a
uniform error at most $C_3|\varepsilon|^3$, with the explicit constant
in \eqref{eq:two-jet-constant}. The adjoint identities, commutation with
$D_x$, and integration by parts give
\begin{align*}
 \mathcal AQ_0h(0)
 &=\int_\mathbf R h(x)(\mathcal A^*p)(x)\,\mathrm d x
  \overset{\eqref{eq:transport-cdf}}{=}\int_\mathbf R h(x)D_xT_1(x)\,\mathrm d x
  =-\int_\mathbf R h'(x)T_1(x)\,\mathrm d x,\\
 \mathcal BQ_0h(0)
 &=\int_\mathbf R h(x)(\mathcal B^*p)(x)\,\mathrm d x
  \overset{\eqref{eq:second-observable}}{=}\int_\mathbf R h(x)D_x\mathsf Q_p(S)(x)\,\mathrm d x
  =-\int_\mathbf R h'(x)\mathsf Q_p(S)(x)\,\mathrm d x,\\
 \mathcal A^2Q_0h(0)
 &=\int_\mathbf R h(x)((\mathcal A^*)^2p)(x)\,\mathrm d x
  =\int_\mathbf R h(x)D_x(\mathcal A^*T_1)(x)\,\mathrm d x
  =-\int_\mathbf R h'(x)(\mathcal A^*T_1)(x)\,\mathrm d x.
\end{align*}
These follow from $\mathcal B^*p=D_x\mathsf Q_p(S)$ and
$\mathcal A^*p=D_xT_1$. Gaussian convolution gives
$p^{(j)}\in L^1(\mathbf R)$ for every $j\geq0$, so $T_1,T_2\in L^1(\mathbf R)$.
Centered Lipschitz approximation and CDF duality, as in
Theorem~\ref{thm:transport-response}, yield \eqref{eq:two-jet-L1}.
Finally,
$|\left\lVert f\right\rVert_1-\left\lVert g\right\rVert_1|\leq\left\lVert f-g\right\rVert_1$ yields \eqref{eq:two-jet-W1}.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
{thm:scalar-second}
Under the assumptions of Theorem~\ref{thm:two-jet}, suppose $(\alpha,\tau)\ne(0,0)$.
Then $\left\lVert T_1\right\rVert_1>0$ and, along admissible parameters of either sign,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:scalar-second}
 W_1(\lambda_\varepsilon,\lambda)
 =|\varepsilon|\left\lVert T_1\right\rVert_1+
 \varepsilon|\varepsilon|\int_\mathbf R\operatorname{sgn}(T_1(x))T_2(x)\,\mathrm d x
                              +o(\varepsilon^2).
\end{equation}
The absolute error after the two displayed terms is at most
\begin{equation}\label{eq:scalar-sign-remainder}
 C_3|\varepsilon|^3+2\varepsilon^2
  \int_{\{x\in\mathbf R:0<|T_1(x)|\leq|\varepsilon T_2(x)|\}}|T_2(x)|\,\mathrm d x.
\end{equation}
If $T_1=0$, the leading term is $\varepsilon^2\left\lVert T_2\right\rVert_1$.
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
For $a,b,t\in\mathbf R$ and $a\ne0$,
\[
 0\leq |a+tb|-|a|-t\operatorname{sgn}(a)b
 \leq2|tb|\mathbf 1_{\{0<|a|\leq|tb|\}}.
\]
For real $f,g\in L^1(\mathbf R)$, set $Z=\{x\in\mathbf R:f(x)=0\}$ and
$\operatorname{sgn}(0)=0$. Integrating this inequality and treating $Z$
separately gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:L1-scalar}
 \left\lVert f+t g\right\rVert_1=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_1+t\int_{Z^c}\operatorname{sgn}(f(x))g(x)\,\mathrm d x
       +|t|\int_Z|g(x)|\,\mathrm d x+r(t),\quad
 |r(t)|\leq2|t|\int_{\{x\in\mathbf R:0<|f(x)|\leq|tg(x)|\}}|g(x)|\,\mathrm d x.
\end{equation}
The last integrand converges to zero almost everywhere and is bounded
by $|g|$, so the integral tends to zero.
Write $p=g_v*\rho$, where $v>0$ and $\rho\in\mathcal P_1(\mathbf R)$.
For $z=x+ib\in\mathbb C$ and $u\in\mathbf R$,
\[
 |g_v(z-u)|=(2\pi v)^{-1/2}
 \exp\!\left(\frac{b^2-(x-u)^2}{2v}\right).
\]
Each complex derivative is this Gaussian times a polynomial in $z-u$.
For every compact $K\subset\mathbb C$ and integer $j\geq0$,
\[
 \sup_{z\in K,\ u\in\mathbf R}|g_v^{(j)}(z-u)|<\infty.
\]
These bounds remain uniform after the real shifts $ry$ in
\eqref{eq:transport-cdf}. Integrating against $\rho$, $\eta$, and $J$
therefore extends $T_1$ to an entire function. By
Proposition~\ref{prop:tangent-quotient}, $(\alpha,\tau)\ne(0,0)$ implies
$T_1\not\equiv0$. Its real zeros are isolated, hence have Lebesgue
measure zero. In particular $\left\lVert T_1\right\rVert_1>0$.
Apply \eqref{eq:L1-scalar} with $(f,g,t)=(T_1,T_2,\varepsilon)$,
multiply by $|\varepsilon|$, and use \eqref{eq:two-jet-W1} to obtain
\eqref{eq:scalar-second} and \eqref{eq:scalar-sign-remainder}.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- theorem-8: body_md -->
{thm:cancellation}
In \eqref{eq:transport-lift}, suppose $M_2<\infty$,
$\alpha=0$, and $\eta-D_yJ=0$. Then, along admissible parameters,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cancelled-expansion}
 \left\lVert F_{\lambda_\varepsilon}-F_\lambda
              -\varepsilon^2\mathsf Q_p(S)\right\rVert_1=o(\varepsilon^2),\qquad
 W_1(\lambda_\varepsilon,\lambda)
        =\varepsilon^2\left\lVert \mathsf Q_p(S)\right\rVert_1+o(\varepsilon^2).
\end{equation}
The coefficient is strictly positive whenever $M_2>0$.
If $M_3<\infty$, the $L^1$ remainder is at most
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cancelled-third}
 \frac{K_4M_3}{120}|\varepsilon|^3+
                        \frac{K_7M_2^2}{1152}\varepsilon^4.
\end{equation}
If $\Pi$ is invariant under $(y,z)\mapsto(y,-z)$ and $M_4<\infty$, then
$J=0$, necessarily $\eta=0$, and the remainder is at most
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cancelled-fourth}
 \varepsilon^4\left(\frac{K_5M_4}{720}+
                              \frac{K_7M_2^2}{1152}\right).
\end{equation}
Here $K_j=\left\lVert g_{v_*}^{(j)}\right\rVert_1$.
<!-- end theorem-8 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
The identity $\eta-D_yJ=0$ means
$\int_\mathbf R\phi(y)\eta(\,\mathrm d y)+\int_\mathbf R\phi'(y)J(\,\mathrm d y)=0$ for $\phi\in C_c^\infty(\mathbf R)$.
For a smooth cutoff $\chi_R(y)=\chi(y/R)$, apply this identity to
$\phi(y)=\chi_R(y)\Phi_{f,x}(y)$:
\[
 \int_\mathbf R\chi_R(y)\Phi_{f,x}(y)\eta(\,\mathrm d y)
 +\int_\mathbf R\chi_R(y)\partial_y\Phi_{f,x}(y)J(\,\mathrm d y)
 =-\int_\mathbf R\chi_R'(y)\Phi_{f,x}(y)J(\,\mathrm d y)\longrightarrow0.
\]
The right-hand side is bounded by
$R^{-1}\left\lVert \chi'\right\rVert_\infty\left\lVert \Phi_{f,x}\right\rVert_\infty\left\lVert J\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}$.
Dominated convergence applies to the left-hand side. Hence $\mathcal A=0$.
Taylor expansion then gives
\[
 \left\lVert \mathcal D_\varepsilon f\right\rVert_\infty
 \leq\frac{\varepsilon^2M_2}{24}\left\lVert f^{(4)}\right\rVert_\infty,
 \qquad
 \left\lVert \mathcal D_\varepsilon^2f\right\rVert_\infty
 \leq\frac{\varepsilon^4M_2^2}{576}\left\lVert f^{(8)}\right\rVert_\infty.
\]
For $\operatorname{Lip}(h)\leq1$, the interpolation identity, the triangle inequality,
and the Gaussian derivative bound therefore give
\begin{align*}
 \left\lVert (Q_1-Q_0)h-\mathcal D_\varepsilon Q_0h\right\rVert_\infty
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:second-interpolation}}{=}
 \left\|\int_0^1(1-u)\mathcal D_\varepsilon^2Q_uh\,\mathrm d u\right\|_\infty\\
 &\leq
 \int_0^1(1-u)\left\lVert \mathcal D_\varepsilon^2Q_uh\right\rVert_\infty\,\mathrm d u
 \leq
 \frac{\varepsilon^4K_7M_2^2}{576}\int_0^1(1-u)\,\mathrm d u
 =\frac{\varepsilon^4K_7M_2^2}{1152}.
\end{align*}
For $q=Q_0h$ and $F(y)=\Phi_{q,0}(y)$,
\[
 F(y+u)-F(y)-uF'(y)-\frac{u^2}{2}F''(y)
 =u^2\int_0^1(1-t)[F''(y+tu)-F''(y)]\,\mathrm d t.
\]
Using separately
$|F''(y+tu)-F''(y)|\leq2\left\lVert F''\right\rVert_\infty$
and $|tu|\left\lVert F'''\right\rVert_\infty$, together with
$\left\lVert F''\right\rVert_\infty\leq K_3/12$ and
$\left\lVert F'''\right\rVert_\infty\leq K_4/20$, bounds the remainder in the Taylor expansion of the generator difference by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:min-remainder}
 \varepsilon^2\int_{\mathbf R^2} z^2
       \min\{K_3/12,\ |\varepsilon z|K_4/120\}\,\Pi(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d z).
\end{equation}
After dividing \eqref{eq:min-remainder} by $\varepsilon^2$, dominated convergence with respect to the
finite measure $z^2\Pi$ shows that the limit is zero. This proves
\eqref{eq:cancelled-expansion}. Using the second term in the minimum gives
\eqref{eq:cancelled-third}.
If $\Pi$ is symmetric in $z$, every integrable odd function of $z$
integrates to zero. Thus $J=0$, $\eta=0$, and the cubic Taylor term vanishes.
The fourth Taylor remainder is bounded by
\[
 \frac{\varepsilon^4M_4}{4!}
 \left\lVert \partial_y^4\Phi_{q,0}\right\rVert_\infty
 \leq\frac{\varepsilon^4M_4K_5}{720},
\]
which gives \eqref{eq:cancelled-fourth}.
Finally, Fourier transformation gives
\begin{align}
 \widehat{\mathsf Q_p(S)}(\xi)
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:second-observable}}{=}
 \frac12(i\xi)^3\widehat p(\xi)
       \int_0^1r^2(1-r)\widehat S(r\xi)\,\mathrm d r.
       \label{eq:calc-second-fourier}\\
\intertext{Then continuity at the origin and dominated convergence imply}
 \int_0^1r^2(1-r)\widehat S(r\xi)\,\mathrm d r
 &\xrightarrow[\xi\to0]{}
 M_2\int_0^1r^2(1-r)\,\mathrm d r=\frac{M_2}{12}.\nonumber
\end{align}
Since $\widehat p(0)=1$, this Fourier transform is nonzero for sufficiently
small nonzero $\xi$ when $M_2>0$. Thus $\left\lVert \mathsf Q_p(S)\right\rVert_1>0$.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- theorem-9: body_md -->
{thm:lindeberg}
Let $\mu\in\mathcal M_f^+(\mathbf R)$, $v>0$, and
$\pi_\varepsilon\in\mathcal M_f^+(\mathbf R_y\times\mathbf R_U)$, $\varepsilon\ne0$, have first
marginal $\mu$ and satisfy $\int_{\mathbf R^2} U f(y)\pi_\varepsilon(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d U)=0$ for every
bounded Borel function $f:\mathbf R\to\mathbf R$. Let $\lambda$ and $\lambda_\varepsilon$
have common mean $m\in\mathbf R$ and characteristics $v\delta_0+\mu$ and
$\Gamma_\varepsilon=v\delta_0+(y+U)_\#\pi_\varepsilon$. For Borel
$A\subset\mathbf R$, put
\[
 S_\varepsilon(A)=\varepsilon^{-2}
       \int_{A\times\mathbf R}U^2\pi_\varepsilon(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d U),\qquad
 C_{2,\varepsilon}=\int_{\mathbf R^2} U^2\pi_\varepsilon(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d U).
\]
Suppose $d_{\mathrm{FM}}(S_\varepsilon,S)\to0$ for a finite positive measure $S$, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lindeberg-condition}
 \varepsilon^{-2}\int_{\mathbf R^2} U^2\mathbf 1_{\{|U|>r\}}\pi_\varepsilon(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d U)\longrightarrow0
                      \quad\text{for every }r>0.
\end{equation}
Then \eqref{eq:cancelled-expansion} holds with this $S$. More precisely,
with $K_j=\left\lVert g_v^{(j)}\right\rVert_1$, its $L^1$ remainder is at most
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lindeberg-remainder}
 \varepsilon^2(K_3/24+K_4/40)d_{\mathrm{FM}}(S_\varepsilon,S)
 +\frac{K_7C_{2,\varepsilon}^2}{1152}
 +\int_{\mathbf R^2} U^2\min\{K_3/12,\ |U|K_4/120\}\pi_\varepsilon(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d U).
\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-9 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
Use the preceding interpolation argument with displacement $U$.
Its quadratic term is $\varepsilon^2\mathsf Q_p(S_\varepsilon)$.
For $\psi\in L^\infty(\mathbf R)$ with $\left\lVert \psi\right\rVert_\infty\leq1$, set
\[
 q_\psi(y)=\frac12\int_\mathbf R\psi(x)
       \int_0^1r^2(1-r)p'''(x-ry)\,\mathrm d r\,\mathrm d x.
\]
Fubini's theorem, differentiation under the integral, and the Gaussian
bounds $\left\lVert p^{(j)}\right\rVert_1\leq K_j$ yield
\begin{align*}
 \left\lVert q_\psi\right\rVert_\infty
 &\leq\frac{\left\lVert \psi\right\rVert_\infty}{2}
   \int_0^1r^2(1-r)\left\lVert p'''\right\rVert_1\,\mathrm d r
 \leq\frac{K_3}{2}
   \int_0^1r^2(1-r)\,\mathrm d r=\frac{K_3}{24},\\
 \operatorname{Lip}(q_\psi)
 &\leq\frac{\left\lVert \psi\right\rVert_\infty}{2}
   \int_0^1r^3(1-r)\left\lVert p^{(4)}\right\rVert_1\,\mathrm d r
 \leq\frac{K_4}{2}
   \int_0^1r^3(1-r)\,\mathrm d r=\frac{K_4}{40}.
\end{align*}
Then $L^1$ duality, Fubini's theorem, and the preceding bounds give
\begin{align*}
 \left\lVert \mathsf Q_p(S_\varepsilon)-\mathsf Q_p(S)\right\rVert_1
 &=
 \sup_{\left\lVert \psi\right\rVert_\infty\leq1}
 \left|\int_\mathbf R q_\psi(y)(S_\varepsilon-S)(\,\mathrm d y)\right|\\
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:metrics}}{\leq}
 \sup_{\left\lVert \psi\right\rVert_\infty\leq1}\left\lVert q_\psi\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}}
                  d_{\mathrm{FM}}(S_\varepsilon,S)
 \leq
 (K_3/24+K_4/40)d_{\mathrm{FM}}(S_\varepsilon,S).
\end{align*}
The other two terms come from the Taylor and interpolation remainders in
\eqref{eq:lindeberg-remainder}. Since $\left\lVert 1\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}}=1$,
\[
 \left|\varepsilon^{-2}C_{2,\varepsilon}-S(\mathbf R)\right|
 \leq d_{\mathrm{FM}}(S_\varepsilon,S)\longrightarrow0.
\]
In particular $C_{2,\varepsilon}=O(\varepsilon^2)$.
For any $r>0$, split the final remainder $R_\varepsilon$ at $|U|=r$ and use
$|U|^3\leq rU^2$ on $\{|U|\leq r\}$. Thus
\begin{align*}
 \frac{R_\varepsilon}{\varepsilon^2}
 &\leq
 \frac{K_4}{120\varepsilon^2}
       \int_{\mathbf R\times[-r,r]}|U|^3\pi_\varepsilon(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d U)
 +\frac{K_3}{12\varepsilon^2}
       \int_{\mathbf R\times(\mathbf R\setminus[-r,r])}U^2\pi_\varepsilon(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d U)\\
 &\leq
 \frac{rK_4}{120}\frac{C_{2,\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^2}
 +\frac{K_3}{12\varepsilon^2}
       \int_{\mathbf R\times(\mathbf R\setminus[-r,r])}U^2\pi_\varepsilon(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d U).
\end{align*}
Since $C_{2,\varepsilon}/\varepsilon^2\to S(\mathbf R)$, it follows that
$$\limsup_{\varepsilon\to0}\frac{R_\varepsilon}{\varepsilon^2}
 \overset{\eqref{eq:lindeberg-condition}}{\leq}
 \frac{rK_4}{120}S(\mathbf R)\xrightarrow[r\downarrow0]{}0.$$
Every term in
\eqref{eq:lindeberg-remainder} is $o(\varepsilon^2)$.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
{prop:second-matching}
Extend \eqref{eq:second-observable} linearly to $S\in\mathcal M_f(\mathbf R)$.
Then $\|S\|_{\lambda,2}:=\left\lVert \mathsf Q_p(S)\right\rVert_1$ is a norm on
$\mathcal M_f(\mathbf R)$ and contracts
under common independent infinitely divisible convolution.
For two lifted paths at the same reference law, assume their first
pairs $(\alpha,\tau)$ agree and their third displacement moments are
finite. If their dispersion measures are $S_0,S_1$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:matched-response}
 W_1(\lambda_\varepsilon^0,\lambda_\varepsilon^1)
       =\varepsilon^2\|S_0-S_1\|_{\lambda,2}+O(|\varepsilon|^3).
\end{equation}
In particular, if also $S_0=S_1$, the error is $O(|\varepsilon|^3)$.
For two balanced symmetric lifts with equal $S$ and finite fourth
velocity moments, this improves to $O(\varepsilon^4)$.
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
The norm is finite since $\left\lVert \mathsf Q_p(S)\right\rVert_1\leq
\left\lVert p'''\right\rVert_1\left\lVert S\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}/24$. Let
$k(\xi,y)=-\xi^2\int_0^1(1-r)e^{ir\xi y}\,\mathrm d r$ and
$b(\xi)=\tfrac12\int_\mathbf R\partial_y^2k(\xi,y)S(\,\mathrm d y)$.
Fourier transformation gives
$i\xi\widehat{\mathsf Q_p(S)}(\xi)=b(-\xi)\widehat p(\xi)$.
Also $b''(\xi)=\xi^2\int_\mathbf R e^{i\xi y}S(\,\mathrm d y)/2$.
Indeed, the zero initial values at $\xi=0$ give
\[
 \partial_y^2k(\xi,y)=\int_0^\xi(\xi-t)t^2e^{ity}\,\mathrm d t,
 \qquad |\partial_y^2k(\xi,y)|\leq\frac{|\xi|^4}{12}.
\]
We may differentiate this integral twice, uniformly in $y$ on
compact $\xi$ intervals, using only $|S|(\mathbf R)<\infty$.
Since $\widehat p$ never vanishes, a zero response forces $b=0$.
It follows that $\widehat S=0$ away from the origin and hence,
by continuity, everywhere.
Uniqueness of Fourier transforms gives $S=0$. Linearity proves the remaining norm axioms, and
$\mathsf Q_{p*\zeta}(S)=\mathsf Q_p(S)*\zeta$ gives contraction.
Equal first tangents give the same $\mathcal A$ and $T_1$.
Subtract \eqref{eq:two-jet-L1} for the two paths: the composed term
$\mathcal A^*T_1/2$ cancels, leaving
$\varepsilon^2\mathsf Q_p(S_0-S_1)$ and a cubic remainder.
CDF duality proves \eqref{eq:matched-response}.
For balanced symmetric lifts, we use the same subtraction with the fourth-order
remainders in \eqref{eq:cancelled-fourth}.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
{prop:raw-atom}
Fix $c,v>0$, $a\ne0$ and a mean $m$. Let the reference law have raw Lévy
measure $N=c\delta_a$ and density $p$. Keep the mean and Gaussian variance
fixed and set $N_\varepsilon=c\delta_{a+\varepsilon}$, with
$|\varepsilon|<|a|/2$. Then
\begin{align}
 T_1(x)&=c[p(x)-p(x-a)],\label{eq:raw-first}\\
 T_2(x)&=\tfrac c2p'(x-a)+\tfrac{c^2}{2}
                  [p'(x)-2p'(x-a)+p'(x-2a)].\label{eq:raw-second}
\end{align}
With $K_2=\left\lVert g_v''\right\rVert_1$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-remainder}
 \left\lVert F_{\lambda_\varepsilon}-F_\lambda
                 -\varepsilon T_1-\varepsilon^2T_2\right\rVert_1
 \leq K_2\left(\frac c6+c^2+\frac{4c^3}{3}\right)|\varepsilon|^3.
\end{equation}
In particular the first Wasserstein coefficient
$c\left\lVert p-p(\cdot-a)\right\rVert_1$ is strictly positive.
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
The generator difference and its first two coefficients are
\[
 \mathcal D_\varepsilon f
   =c[f(\cdot+a+\varepsilon)-f(\cdot+a)-\varepsilon f'],\quad
 \mathcal Af=c[f'(\cdot+a)-f'],\quad
 \mathcal Bf=\tfrac c2f''(\cdot+a).
\]
Taylor expansion gives
\begin{align*}
 \left\lVert \mathcal D_\varepsilon f\right\rVert_\infty
 &\leq 2c|\varepsilon|\left\lVert f'\right\rVert_\infty,\\
 \left\lVert (\mathcal D_\varepsilon-\varepsilon\mathcal A)f\right\rVert_\infty
 &\leq \frac{c\varepsilon^2}{2}\left\lVert f''\right\rVert_\infty,\\
 \left\lVert (\mathcal D_\varepsilon-\varepsilon\mathcal A-\varepsilon^2\mathcal B)f\right\rVert_\infty
 &\leq \frac{c|\varepsilon|^3}{6}\left\lVert f'''\right\rVert_\infty.
\end{align*}
Then, using commutation and these bounds, we obtain
\begin{align*}
 \left\lVert (\mathcal D_\varepsilon^2-\varepsilon^2\mathcal A^2)f\right\rVert_\infty
 &\leq 2c^2|\varepsilon|^3\left\lVert f'''\right\rVert_\infty,\\
 \left\lVert \mathcal D_\varepsilon^3f\right\rVert_\infty
 &\leq 8c^3|\varepsilon|^3\left\lVert f'''\right\rVert_\infty.
\end{align*}
The third-order interpolation identity and
$\left\lVert (Q_uh)'''\right\rVert_\infty\leq K_2\operatorname{Lip}(h)$ yield
$K_2|\varepsilon|^3(c/6+c^2+4c^3/3)$.
The spatial adjoint is
$\mathcal A^*u=c[u'-u'(\cdot-a)]$.
Its first CDF response is \eqref{eq:raw-first}. Adding the primitive of
$\mathcal B^*p$ and $\mathcal A^*T_1/2$ gives \eqref{eq:raw-second}.
If $p=p(\cdot-a)$ almost everywhere, the positive integral over one
period would be repeated on every translated period, contradicting
$\int_\mathbf R p(x)\,\mathrm d x=1$. Hence $T_1\ne0$.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- theorem-10: body_md -->
{thm:barycentric}
Let $\mu,\widehat\mu\in\mathcal M_f^+(\mathbf R)$ have a coupling
$\pi\in\mathcal M_f^+(\mathbf R^2)$, whose marginals are $\mu,\widehat\mu$,
with finite quadratic displacement cost
$\mathcal C_2=\int_{\mathbf R^2}|z-y|^2\pi(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d z)$. Suppose
\begin{equation}\label{eq:barycentric}
 \int_{\mathbf R^2} (z-y)f(y)\pi(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d z)=0
 \quad\text{for every bounded Borel }f:\mathbf R\to\mathbf R.
\end{equation}
For the terminal laws with common mean $m$ and joint characteristics
$v\delta_0+\mu$, $v\delta_0+\widehat\mu$, where $v>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:barycentric-bound}
 W_1(\lambda,\widehat\lambda)
          \leq\frac{\left\lVert g_v'''\right\rVert_1}{24}\,\mathcal C_2.
\end{equation}
The same bound holds if \eqref{eq:barycentric} is imposed with $f(z)$
instead of $f(y)$.
<!-- end theorem-10 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
For $f\in C_b^4(\mathbf R)$, the common mean cancels the drift difference, while
balance cancels the first derivative term in the jump coordinate. Taylor's integral
formula then gives
\begin{align*}
 \mathcal Df(x)
 &=\int_{\mathbf R^2}[\Phi_{f,x}(z)-\Phi_{f,x}(y)]\pi(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d z)\\
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:barycentric}}{=}
 \int_{\mathbf R^2}[\Phi_{f,x}(z)-\Phi_{f,x}(y)
              -(z-y)\partial_y\Phi_{f,x}(y)]\pi(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d z)\\
 &=\int_{\mathbf R^2}(z-y)^2\int_0^1(1-t)
       \partial_y^2\Phi_{f,x}(y+t(z-y))\,\mathrm d t\,\pi(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d z).
\end{align*}
Since $\left\lVert \partial_y^2\Phi_{f,x}\right\rVert_\infty
\leq\left\lVert f^{(4)}\right\rVert_\infty/12$,
\[
 \left\lVert \mathcal Df\right\rVert_\infty
 \leq\frac{\mathcal C_2}{24}\left\lVert f^{(4)}\right\rVert_\infty.
\]
Every terminal interpolation law retains Gaussian variance $v$, so
$\left\lVert (Q_uh)^{(4)}\right\rVert_\infty\leq\left\lVert g_v'''\right\rVert_1\operatorname{Lip}(h)$.
The triangle inequality and the preceding Taylor bound therefore give
\begin{align*}
 |\langle h,\widehat\lambda-\lambda\rangle|
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:interpolation-identity}}{=}
 \left|\int_0^1\mathcal DQ_uh(0)\,\mathrm d u\right|
 \\ &\leq
 \int_0^1\left\lVert \mathcal DQ_uh\right\rVert_\infty\,\mathrm d u\\
 &\leq
 \frac{\mathcal C_2}{24}\int_0^1\left\lVert (Q_uh)^{(4)}\right\rVert_\infty\,\mathrm d u
 \\ &\leq
 \frac{\mathcal C_2}{24}\left\lVert g_v'''\right\rVert_1\operatorname{Lip}(h).
\end{align*}
Lemmas~\ref{lem:interpolation} and~\ref{lem:approximation} justify the interpolation
and approximation steps. Kantorovich duality completes the proof.
Reversing the coupling proves the version with conditional balance given $z$.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- theorem-11: body_md -->
{thm:sharp-grid}
There are $h_0,c,C>0$, depending only on $v,M,R,L$, such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sharp-grid}
 c\mathcal V_h\leq
 \inf_{\pi\in\mathcal B_h}W_1(\lambda,\widehat\lambda_\pi)
 \leq C\mathcal V_h,\qquad 0<h\leq h_0.
\end{equation}
We may take $C=\left\lVert g_v'''\right\rVert_1/24$ and $c=\gamma/2$, where
\begin{equation}\label{eq:grid-lower-constant}
 \xi=(1+R)^{-1},\qquad
 \gamma=\frac{\xi^3}{24}e^{-(v+M)\xi^2/2}\cos(R\xi)>0.
\end{equation}
Consequently, $\mathcal V_h\geq\nu h^2$ along a sequence, with $\nu>0$,
implies a matching $h^2$ upper and lower rate on that sequence.
<!-- end theorem-11 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
For $z\in h\mathbb Z$, $(z-\ell_h(y))(z-r_h(y))\geq0$. Expand
\[
 (z-\ell_h)(z-r_h)
 =(z-y)^2+(2y-\ell_h-r_h)(z-y)-(y-\ell_h)(r_h-y).
\]
Since $|2y-\ell_h-r_h|\leq h$, balance removes the middle term after
integration. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:calc-second-grid-cost}
 C_2:=\int_{\mathbf R^2}(z-y)^2\pi(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d z)\geq\mathcal V_h.
\end{equation}
Splitting between the two bracketing neighbours attains this minimum
and belongs to $\mathcal B_h$. The upper bound now follows from
Theorem~\ref{thm:barycentric}.
For Borel $A\subset\mathbf R$, define $S_\pi(A)=\int_{A\times\mathbf R}(z-y)^2\pi(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d z)$.
For every positive $S$ supported on $[-R,R]$, positivity and
$R\xi<1<\pi/2$ give, for $0\leq r\leq1$,
\begin{align}
 \operatorname{Re}\widehat S(r\xi)
 &=\int_\mathbf R\cos(r\xi y)S(\,\mathrm d y)
 \geq\cos(R\xi)S(\mathbf R).
 \nonumber\\
\intertext{Moreover, the elementary inequality $\cos t\geq1-t^2/2$ gives}
 \operatorname{Re}\Psi(\xi)
 &=-\frac{v\xi^2}{2}+\int_\mathbf R\frac{\cos(\xi y)-1}{y^2}\mu(\,\mathrm d y)
 \geq
 -\frac{(v+M)\xi^2}{2}.
 \label{eq:calc-second-characteristic-bound}
\end{align}
At $y=0$, the quotient is interpreted as $-\xi^2/2$.
We now bound a Fourier transform by the $L^1$ norm, and its
modulus from below by the positive real part. This gives
\begin{align*}
 \left\lVert \mathsf Q_p(S)\right\rVert_1
 &\geq|\widehat{\mathsf Q_p(S)}(\xi)|
 \overset{\eqref{eq:calc-second-fourier}}{=}
 \frac{\xi^3|\widehat p(\xi)|}{2}
 \left|\int_0^1r^2(1-r)\widehat S(r\xi)\,\mathrm d r\right|\\
 &\geq
 \frac{\xi^3|\widehat p(\xi)|\cos(R\xi)}{24}S(\mathbf R)\\
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:calc-second-characteristic-bound}}{\geq}
 \frac{\xi^3e^{-(v+M)\xi^2/2}\cos(R\xi)}{24}S(\mathbf R)
 \overset{\eqref{eq:grid-lower-constant}}{=}\gamma S(\mathbf R).
\end{align*}
The same interpolation calculation as \eqref{eq:cancelled-third}, applied
to $U=z-y$, yields
\[
 \left\lVert F_{\widehat\lambda_\pi}-F_\lambda-\mathsf Q_p(S_\pi)\right\rVert_1
 \leq K_4C_3/120+K_7C_2^2/1152,\quad
 C_3=\int_{\mathbf R^2}|U|^3\pi(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d z),\quad K_j=\left\lVert g_v^{(j)}\right\rVert_1.
\]
Since $C_3\leq LhC_2$ and $C_2\leq ML^2h^2$, choose $h_0$ so that
$K_4Lh_0/120+K_7ML^2h_0^2/1152\leq\gamma/2$.
Using CDF duality and the reverse triangle inequality, then these
bounds and the choice $h\leq h_0$, we see that every feasible plan satisfies
\begin{align*}
 W_1(\lambda,\widehat\lambda_\pi)
 &=\left\lVert F_{\widehat\lambda_\pi}-F_\lambda\right\rVert_1\\
 &\geq
 \left\lVert \mathsf Q_p(S_\pi)\right\rVert_1
 -\left\lVert F_{\widehat\lambda_\pi}-F_\lambda-\mathsf Q_p(S_\pi)\right\rVert_1\\
 &\geq
 \left(\gamma-\frac{K_4Lh}{120}
                   -\frac{K_7ML^2h^2}{1152}\right)C_2
 \geq\frac{\gamma C_2}{2}
 \overset{\eqref{eq:calc-second-grid-cost}}{\geq}\frac{\gamma\mathcal V_h}{2}.
\end{align*}
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
{prop:split-order}
For the corresponding laws,
\begin{align}
 W_1(\lambda_\varepsilon^{\mathrm{move}},\lambda)
   &=|\varepsilon|C_1+O(\varepsilon^2),
       &\text{with}\quad C_1&=M\left\|\int_0^1r(1-r)p''(\cdot-ra)\,\mathrm d r\right\|_1>0,
           \label{eq:move-order}\\
 W_1(\lambda_\varepsilon^{\mathrm{split}},\lambda)
   &=\varepsilon^2C_2+O(\varepsilon^4),
       &\text{with}\quad C_2&=\frac M2\left\|\int_0^1r^2(1-r)p'''(\cdot-ra)\,\mathrm d r\right\|_1>0.
           \label{eq:split-order}
\end{align}
The absolute remainder in \eqref{eq:split-order} is bounded by
$\varepsilon^4(MK_5/720+M^2K_7/1152)$, where
$K_j=\left\lVert g_v^{(j)}\right\rVert_1$.
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
For the moving path, $J=M\delta_a$ and $\eta=0$.
Theorem~\ref{thm:transport-response} and Proposition~\ref{prop:tangent-quotient} show that $C_1>0$.
For the symmetric split, $J=0$ and $S=M\delta_a$. Its second and fourth
absolute displacement moments both equal $M$.
Theorem~\ref{thm:cancellation} gives $C_2>0$, with the exact fourth-order
remainder stated above.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- theorem-12: body_md -->
{thm:remeshing-oracle}
Let $M=\mu(\mathbf R)<\infty$, $v>0$, and let $\mathcal C_h$ be a nonempty
family of couplings with first marginal $\mu$, all supported on
$|z-y|\leq h$. The output law has joint characteristic
$v\delta_0+\widehat\mu$ and mean $m$. Put $K_j=\left\lVert g_v^{(j)}\right\rVert_1$.
For every $\pi\in\mathcal C_h$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:remeshing-remainder}
 \left|W_1(\lambda,\widehat\lambda_\pi)-\mathcal J_p(\pi)\right|
 \leq R_h,\qquad
 R_h=\tfrac12(K_3/4+K_4/6+K_5/36)M^2h^2.
\end{equation}
If every coupling is balanced as in \eqref{eq:barycentric}, we may instead
use
\begin{equation}\label{eq:balanced-oracle-remainder}
 R_h=K_7M^2h^4/1152.
\end{equation}
If $\pi_*$ minimizes $\mathcal J_p$ over $\mathcal C_h$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:oracle}
 W_1(\lambda,\widehat\lambda_{\pi_*})
 \leq\inf_{\pi\in\mathcal C_h}W_1(\lambda,\widehat\lambda_\pi)+2R_h.
\end{equation}
An approximate objective $\widehat{\mathcal J}$ with uniform error at most
$e_q$, minimized to tolerance $e_o$, adds $2e_q+e_o$ to the right-hand side.
<!-- end theorem-12 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
For $\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}}\leq1$,
\[
 |\langle f,\widehat\mu-\mu\rangle|
 =\left|\int_{\mathbf R^2}[f(z)-f(y)]\pi(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d z)\right|
 \leq\operatorname{Lip}(f)\int_{\mathbf R^2}|z-y|\pi(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d z)\leq Mh.
\]
Thus $\left\lVert \widehat\mu-\mu\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}^*}\leq Mh$.
The affine remainder \eqref{eq:response-bound}, with zero mean change,
and its $L^1$ representation give \eqref{eq:remeshing-remainder}.
In the balanced case the exact generator difference satisfies
\[
 \left\lVert \mathcal Df\right\rVert_\infty\leq C_2\left\lVert f^{(4)}\right\rVert_\infty/24,
 \qquad \left\lVert \mathcal D^2f\right\rVert_\infty\leq C_2^2\left\lVert f^{(8)}\right\rVert_\infty/576,
 \qquad C_2=\int_{\mathbf R^2}|z-y|^2\pi(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d z)\leq Mh^2.
\]
The second interpolation identity therefore bounds the error of the
approximation $H_\pi$ by $K_7C_2^2/1152$. This proves
\eqref{eq:balanced-oracle-remainder}.
For any feasible $\pi$, apply the remainder bound at $\pi_*$, use its minimizing
property, and then apply the remainder bound at $\pi$:
\[
 W_1(\lambda,\widehat\lambda_{\pi_*})
 \overset{\eqref{eq:remeshing-remainder}}{\leq}\mathcal J_p(\pi_*)+R_h
 \leq\mathcal J_p(\pi)+R_h
 \overset{\eqref{eq:remeshing-remainder}}{\leq}
 W_1(\lambda,\widehat\lambda_\pi)+2R_h.
\]
Taking the infimum proves \eqref{eq:oracle}.
If $|\widehat{\mathcal J}(\pi)-\mathcal J_p(\pi)|\leq e_q$ and
$\widehat{\mathcal J}(\widehat\pi)
\leq\inf_{\pi\in\mathcal C_h}\widehat{\mathcal J}(\pi)+e_o$,
use the uniform objective error bound on either side of the approximate
minimization inequality. Together with the terminal remainder, this gives
\begin{align*}
 W_1(\lambda,\widehat\lambda_{\widehat\pi})
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:remeshing-remainder}}{\leq}
 \mathcal J_p(\widehat\pi)+R_h
 \leq
 \widehat{\mathcal J}(\widehat\pi)+e_q+R_h\\
 &\leq
 \inf_{\pi\in\mathcal C_h}\widehat{\mathcal J}(\pi)
      +e_o+e_q+R_h
 \leq
 \inf_{\pi\in\mathcal C_h}\mathcal J_p(\pi)+e_o+2e_q+R_h\\
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:remeshing-remainder}}{\leq}
 \inf_{\pi\in\mathcal C_h}W_1(\lambda,\widehat\lambda_\pi)
       +2R_h+2e_q+e_o.
\end{align*}
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- proposition-7: body_md -->
{prop:LP-consistency}
Fix a finite source and grid, and a nonempty feasible polytope
\eqref{eq:LP-constraints}. Use the exact response functions and midpoint quadrature
on $[-L_n,L_n]$ of step $\Delta_n$, with $L_n\to\infty$ and
$\Delta_n\to0$. Then the discrete objectives converge uniformly to
$\mathcal J_p$ on this polytope. Their minimum values converge, and every
cluster point of minimizers minimizes the exact objective. The same
holds for feasible approximate minimizers whose tolerance tends to zero.
<!-- end proposition-7 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
Put $B_{ij}=A_p(z_j,\cdot)-A_p(y_i,\cdot)\in W^{1,1}(\mathbf R)$ and
$H_\pi=\sum_{i,j}\pi_{ij}B_{ij}$. For $H\in W^{1,1}(\mathbf R)$, use its
absolutely continuous representative. Midpoint quadrature gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:objective-quadrature}
 \left|\sum_\ell\omega_\ell|H(x_\ell)|-\left\lVert H\right\rVert_1\right|
 \leq\frac{\Delta_n}{2}\left\lVert H'\right\rVert_1+\int_{\{x\in\mathbf R:|x|>L_n\}}|H(x)|\,\mathrm d x.
\end{equation}
For a cell $I_\ell$ with midpoint $x_\ell$ and length $\Delta_n$, absolute
continuity and Fubini's theorem give the following calculation. The
weights in its two integrals are at most $\Delta_n/2$:
\begin{align*}
 \int_{I_\ell}\big||H(x)|-|H(x_\ell)|\big|\,\mathrm d x
 &\leq
 \int_{I_\ell}\left|\int_{x_\ell}^{x}|H'(t)|\,\mathrm d t\right|\,\mathrm d x\\
 &=
 \int_{x_\ell-\Delta_n/2}^{x_\ell}
      (t-x_\ell+\Delta_n/2)|H'(t)|\,\mathrm d t
 +\int_{x_\ell}^{x_\ell+\Delta_n/2}
      (x_\ell+\Delta_n/2-t)|H'(t)|\,\mathrm d t\\
 &\leq
 \frac{\Delta_n}{2}\int_{I_\ell}|H'(t)|\,\mathrm d t.
\end{align*}
Summing over the cells and adding the tail proves \eqref{eq:objective-quadrature}.
The row-sum constraints then give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:uniform-LP-error}
 e_n\leq\frac{\Delta_n}{2}\sum_i M_i\max_j\left\lVert B_{ij}'\right\rVert_1
       +\sum_iM_i\max_j\int_{\{x\in\mathbf R:|x|>L_n\}}|B_{ij}(x)|\,\mathrm d x\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
Since the family of response functions is finite, the convergence $e_n\to0$ is uniform. If $\mathcal J_n$ denotes
the discrete objective, then
\[
 |\min\mathcal J_n-\min\mathcal J_p|\leq e_n.
\]
For feasible $\pi_n$ with
$\mathcal J_n(\pi_n)\leq\min\mathcal J_n+\delta_n$ and $\delta_n\to0$,
the optimization tolerance and the uniform error imply
\[
 \mathcal J_p(\pi_n)
 \overset{\eqref{eq:uniform-LP-error}}{\leq}\mathcal J_n(\pi_n)+e_n
 \leq\min\mathcal J_n+\delta_n+e_n
 \overset{\eqref{eq:uniform-LP-error}}{\leq}\min\mathcal J_p+2e_n+\delta_n.
\]
Along any convergent subsequence, continuity shows that its limit
minimizes $\mathcal J_p$. Compactness ensures that such subsequences exist.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
{lem:atoms}
For $m\geq0$ and $x,z\in\mathbf R$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:atoms}
 d_{\mathrm{FM}}(m\delta_x,m\delta_z)=\frac{2m|x-z|}{2+|x-z|}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
Let $d=|x-z|$, $A=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_\infty$, and $B=\operatorname{Lip}(f)$.
If $A+B\leq1$, then $|f(x)-f(z)|\leq\min\{2A,Bd\}\leq2d/(2+d)$.
Equality is attained by the clipped affine function
\[
 f(y)=\max\{-A_*,\min\{B_*(y-(x+z)/2),A_*\}\},\qquad
 A_*=d/(2+d),\quad B_*=2/(2+d).
\]
Multiplying by $m$ gives the result, with the zero cases following directly.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- proposition-8: body_md -->
{prop:cubic}
Define $c_0=W_1(\operatorname{Law}(Y),\operatorname{Law}(Z))$. Then $c_0>0$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cubic}
 \theta(a):=d_{\mathrm{FM}}(a^2\delta_a,a^2\delta_{2a})
       =\frac{2a^3}{2+a},\qquad
 W_1(\operatorname{Law}(aY),\operatorname{Law}(aZ))=c_0a.
\end{equation}
No exponent greater than $\frac{1}{3}$ can hold uniformly over this family for
$0<a\leq\frac{1}{2}$, with constants depending only on common upper bounds on
its moments and stochastic-flow derivatives.
