<!-- end proposition-8 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
Lemma~\ref{lem:atoms} gives $\theta(a)$, and Wasserstein scaling gives $ac_0$.
The one-Lipschitz test $h(x)=\operatorname{dist}(x,\mathbb Z)$ has
$h(Y)=0$ and $h(Z)=1/2$ almost surely, so $c_0\geq1/2$.
If a uniform estimate held with exponent $\gamma>1/3$, it would imply
$c_0\leq C a^{3\gamma-1}(1+a/2)^{-\gamma}\to0$, a contradiction.
The intensities are fixed and the jumps bounded for $a\leq1/2$.
The additive flows have first derivative one and all higher derivatives
zero, while their moments are uniformly bounded. The regularized estimate
\eqref{eq:regularized-terminal} gives the matching upper exponent for
this class.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
{lem:moments}
Let $k\in\mathbf N$, $k\geq1$, and let
$U,V\in L^{k+1}(\Omega,\mathcal F,\mathbb P)$ satisfy
$\mathbf E\left[U^j\right]=\mathbf E\left[V^j\right]$ for $1\leq j\leq k-1$. Let $G_\sigma\sim N(0,\sigma^2)$ be independent of them,
with density $\varphi_\sigma$, where $\sigma>0$. Then, for $a>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:moment-remainder}
 \begin{aligned}
 &\Bigl|W_1\bigl(\operatorname{Law}(G_\sigma+aU),\operatorname{Law}(G_\sigma+aV)\bigr)
 -\frac{a^k}{k!}\left|\mathbf E\left[U^k\right]-\mathbf E\left[V^k\right]\right|\left\lVert \varphi_\sigma^{(k-1)}\right\rVert_1\Bigr|\\
 &\qquad\leq \frac{a^{k+1}}{(k+1)!}
 \left(\mathbf E\left[|U|^{k+1}\right]+\mathbf E\left[|V|^{k+1}\right]\right)\left\lVert \varphi_\sigma^{(k)}\right\rVert_1.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
Let $H_\sigma$ be the Gaussian distribution function. Taylor expansion gives
\[
 H_\sigma(x-au)=\sum_{j=0}^k\frac{(-au)^j}{j!}H_\sigma^{(j)}(x)
 +\frac{(-au)^{k+1}}{k!}\int_0^1(1-r)^k
                         \varphi_\sigma^{(k)}(x-rau)\,\mathrm d r.
\]
Fubini's theorem and translation invariance of the $L^1$ norm bound the
remainder $R_u$ by
\begin{align*}
 \left\lVert R_u\right\rVert_1
 &\leq\frac{a^{k+1}|u|^{k+1}}{k!}
  \int_0^1(1-r)^k\left\lVert \varphi_\sigma^{(k)}(\cdot-rau)\right\rVert_1\,\mathrm d r\\
 &=
 \frac{a^{k+1}|u|^{k+1}}{k!}
  \left\lVert \varphi_\sigma^{(k)}\right\rVert_1\int_0^1(1-r)^k\,\mathrm d r
 =\frac{a^{k+1}|u|^{k+1}}{(k+1)!}\left\lVert \varphi_\sigma^{(k)}\right\rVert_1.
\end{align*}
Fubini's theorem applies because $\mathbf E\left[|U|^{k+1}\right]+\mathbf E\left[|V|^{k+1}\right]<\infty$.
Subtracting the two expectations cancels the zeroth term and all matched
moments, leaving
\[
 F_{\operatorname{Law}(G_\sigma+aU)}-F_{\operatorname{Law}(G_\sigma+aV)}
 =\frac{(-a)^k}{k!}(\mathbf E\left[U^k\right]-\mathbf E\left[V^k\right])\varphi_\sigma^{(k-1)}+R,
 \qquad \left\lVert R\right\rVert_1\leq\mathbf E\left[\left\lVert R_U\right\rVert_1\right]+\mathbf E\left[\left\lVert R_V\right\rVert_1\right].
\]
CDF duality and the reverse triangle inequality prove the bound.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- theorem-13: body_md -->
{thm:crossover}
Let $G\sim N(0,1)$ be independent of $Y,Z$ in \eqref{eq:YZ}.
For fixed $\sigma>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sharp-limit}
 W_1\bigl(\operatorname{Law}(\sigma G+aY),\operatorname{Law}(\sigma G+aZ)\bigr)
 =\frac{\sqrt{8/(\pi e)}}{6\sigma^2}a^3+O(a^4\sigma^{-3}),\ 
 \quad\frac{a}{\sigma}\xrightarrow[a\downarrow0]{}0.
\end{equation}
More precisely, the remainder in \eqref{eq:sharp-limit} is bounded in
absolute value by $\frac{11a^4\left\lVert \varphi'''\right\rVert_1}{(24\sigma^3)}$.
There are numerical constants $0<c\leq C<\infty$, independent of $a,\sigma$,
such that for every $a>0$ and $\sigma\geq0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:crossover}
 c\,a\min\{1,\frac{a^2}{\sigma^2\}}
 \leq W_1\bigl(\operatorname{Law}(\sigma G+aY),\operatorname{Law}(\sigma G+aZ)\bigr)
 \leq C\,a\min\{1,\frac{a^2}{\sigma^2\}},
\end{equation}
where the minimum is defined as one when $\sigma=0$.
<!-- end theorem-13 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
Lemma~\ref{lem:moments} with $k=3$ and Gaussian scaling gives
\[
 \left|W_1(\operatorname{Law}(\sigma G+aY),\operatorname{Law}(\sigma G+aZ))-
                       \frac{\sqrt{8/(\pi e)}a^3}{6\sigma^2}\right|
       \leq\frac{11a^4}{24\sigma^3}\left\lVert \varphi'''\right\rVert_1.
\]
Define
\[
 D\colon\left|\begin{aligned}
  [0,\infty)&\longrightarrow [0,\infty)\\
  r&\longmapsto W_1(\operatorname{Law}(rG+Y),\operatorname{Law}(rG+Z))
\end{aligned}\right..
\]
Scaling gives
\begin{align*}
 W_1(\operatorname{Law}(\sigma G+aY),\operatorname{Law}(\sigma G+aZ))
 &=aD(\sigma/a).\\
\intertext{Then the triangle inequality and a common coupling with $G$ yield,
using $\mathbf E\left[|G|\right]=\sqrt{2/\pi}$,}
 |D(r)-D(s)|
 &\leq
 W_1(\operatorname{Law}(rG+Y),\operatorname{Law}(sG+Y))
 +W_1(\operatorname{Law}(rG+Z),\operatorname{Law}(sG+Z))\\
 &\leq2\mathbf E\left[|G|\right]\,|r-s|
 =2\sqrt{2/\pi}|r-s|.
\end{align*}
If $D(r)=0$, cancelling the nonzero Gaussian characteristic function
would give $\operatorname{Law}(Y)=\operatorname{Law}(Z)$, contradicting Proposition~\ref{prop:cubic}.
Set $A=\sqrt{8/(\pi e)}/6>0$ and $B=11\left\lVert \varphi'''\right\rVert_1/24$.
The first estimate with $a=1$ gives
\[
 |D(r)-Ar^{-2}|\leq Br^{-3}.
\]
For $R=\max\{1,2B/A\}$ and $r\geq R$,
$A/(2r^2)\leq D(r)\leq3A/(2r^2)$.
On $[0,R]$, continuity and positivity bound
$D(r)/\min\{1,r^{-2}\}$ above and away from zero, with the denominator
set to one at $r=0$. Scaling proves \eqref{eq:crossover}.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- theorem-14: body_md -->
{thm:modulus}
There are numerical constants $0<c\leq C<\infty$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:modulus}
 c\,\frac{\delta}{v+\delta^{2/3}}
 \leq\mathfrak M(v,\delta)
 \leq C\,\frac{\delta}{v+\delta^{2/3}},
 \qquad 0\leq v\leq1,\quad 0<\delta\leq1.
\end{equation}
The lower bound already holds for pairs with equal total variances.
<!-- end theorem-14 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
For $\varepsilon>0$, independent Gaussian coupling gives
$W_1(\lambda_i,\lambda_i*g_\varepsilon)\leq \sqrt{2/\pi}\sqrt\varepsilon$.
The triangle inequality and Corollary~\ref{cor:Gaussian} give
\begin{align}
 W_1(\lambda_0,\lambda_1)
 &\leq
 W_1(\lambda_0,\lambda_0*g_\varepsilon)
 +W_1(\lambda_0*g_\varepsilon,\lambda_1*g_\varepsilon)
 +W_1(\lambda_1*g_\varepsilon,\lambda_1)
 \nonumber\\
 &\leq
 2\sqrt{2/\pi}\sqrt\varepsilon
 +W_1(\lambda_0*g_\varepsilon,\lambda_1*g_\varepsilon)
 \\ &\leq
 2\sqrt{2/\pi}\sqrt\varepsilon+
 \left(\frac{\sqrt{2/\pi}}{2\sqrt{v+\varepsilon}}+
                \frac{\sqrt{8/(\pi e)}}{6(v+\varepsilon)}\right)\delta.
 \label{eq:regularized-terminal}
\end{align}
If $v\geq\delta^{2/3}$, send $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ and use $v\leq1$
to obtain $C\delta/v$. Otherwise set $\varepsilon=\delta^{2/3}$ to
obtain $C\delta^{1/3}$. Since
$\min\{\delta^{1/3},\delta/v\}$ lies between
$\delta/(v+\delta^{2/3})$ and twice this quantity, the upper bound follows.
At $v=0$, we interpret this minimum as $\delta^{1/3}$.
For the lower bound, take $a=\delta^{1/3}$ and the pair
$\sqrt vG+aY$, $\sqrt vG+aZ$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:crossover}.
Both jump variances equal $a^2\leq1$, their discrepancy is
$2a^3/(2+a)\leq\delta$, and hence this pair is admissible in
\eqref{eq:modulus-definition}. Substituting $a=\delta^{1/3}$ in the
bound covering both regimes and using
$\max\{v,\delta^{2/3}\}\leq v+\delta^{2/3}$ therefore gives
\begin{align*}
 \mathfrak M(v,\delta)
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:modulus-definition}}{\geq}
 W_1(\operatorname{Law}(\sqrt vG+aY),\operatorname{Law}(\sqrt vG+aZ))\\
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:crossover}}{\geq}
 c\,a\min\{1,a^2/v\}\\
 &=c\min\{\delta^{1/3},\delta/v\}\\
 &\geq c\,\frac{\delta}{v+\delta^{2/3}}.
\end{align*}
At $v=0$, the minima have the conventions already specified.
The total variances agree.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- theorem-15: body_md -->
{thm:multi-tangent}
Under \eqref{eq:multi-lift}, put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:multi-response}
 r_1=\mathcal A^*p,\qquad r_2=\mathcal B^*p+\tfrac12\mathcal A^*r_1.
\end{equation}
Then $r_1,r_2\in\mathcal X_1(\mathbf R^d)$, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:multi-two-jet}
 \big\|p_\varepsilon-p-\varepsilon r_1-\varepsilon^2r_2\big\|_{\mathrm{KR}}
       \leq C_0|\varepsilon|^3,\qquad
 W_1(\lambda_\varepsilon,\lambda)
       =\left\lVert \varepsilon r_1+\varepsilon^2r_2\right\rVert_{\mathrm{KR}}
          +O(|\varepsilon|^3).
\end{equation}
The constant depends only on $d,\ell$, the support and velocity bounds,
$N(\mathbf R^d),\left\lVert \eta\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}},|\alpha|,\left\lVert C\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}$.
The first response vanishes exactly when $(\alpha,C,\tau)=0$, and
$\left\lVert r_1\right\rVert_{\mathrm{KR}}$ defines a norm on these annular directions,
is invariant under simultaneous rotations, and contracts under common
independent infinitely divisible convolution.
If $\alpha=C=\eta=0$ and $J=0$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:multi-second}
 W_1(\lambda_\varepsilon,\lambda)
  =\varepsilon^2\left\lVert \mathcal B^*p\right\rVert_{\mathrm{KR}}+O(|\varepsilon|^3),
 \qquad \int_{\mathbf R^{2d}}|z|^2\Pi(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d z)>0\ \Longrightarrow\
                 \left\lVert \mathcal B^*p\right\rVert_{\mathrm{KR}}>0.
\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-15 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
{lem:approximation}
For every one-Lipschitz $h:\mathbf R^d\to\mathbf R$ with $h(0)=0$, there exist
$h_n\in C_c^\infty(\mathbf R^d)$ such that $h_n(0)=0$, $\operatorname{Lip}(h_n)\leq1$,
$|h_n(x)|\leq|x|$, and $h_n\to h$ locally uniformly.
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
Choose a smooth compactly supported probability density $\eta$ and a
smooth cutoff $0\leq\chi\leq1$ equal to one on the unit ball.
Let $h_R=\max\{-R,\min\{h,R\}\}$,
$\eta_\varepsilon(x)=\varepsilon^{-d}\eta(x/\varepsilon)$, and
$f_{R,\varepsilon}=h_R*\eta_\varepsilon-(h_R*\eta_\varepsilon)(0)$.
Then $f_{R,\varepsilon}(0)=0$, $\operatorname{Lip}(f_{R,\varepsilon})\leq1$,
$\left\lVert f_{R,\varepsilon}\right\rVert_\infty\leq2R$, and
$\left\lVert f_{R,\varepsilon}-h_R\right\rVert_\infty\leq2\varepsilon\int_{\mathbf R^d}|z|\eta(z)\,\mathrm d z$.
Set
\[
 h_n(x)=\frac{f_{n,n^{-1}}(x)\chi(x/n^3)}
                   {1+2n^{-2}\left\lVert \nabla\chi\right\rVert_\infty}.
\]
The product rule and the Lipschitz and uniform bounds above give
\begin{align*}
 \left\lVert \nabla(f_{n,n^{-1}}\chi(\cdot/n^3))\right\rVert_\infty
 &\leq
   \left\lVert \nabla f_{n,n^{-1}}\right\rVert_\infty\left\lVert \chi\right\rVert_\infty
   +n^{-3}\left\lVert f_{n,n^{-1}}\right\rVert_\infty\left\lVert \nabla\chi\right\rVert_\infty\\
 &\leq
   1+n^{-3}\left\lVert f_{n,n^{-1}}\right\rVert_\infty\left\lVert \nabla\chi\right\rVert_\infty\\
 &\leq
   1+2n^{-2}\left\lVert \nabla\chi\right\rVert_\infty.
\end{align*}
Thus $\operatorname{Lip}(h_n)\leq1$, and $h_n(0)=0$ implies $|h_n(x)|\leq|x|$.
On each fixed ball, $h_n\to h$ uniformly because the cutoff eventually
equals one, the denominator tends to one, and the mollification error
is $O(n^{-1})$. For $\lambda\in\mathcal P_1(\mathbf R^d)$, dominated convergence gives
$\int_{\mathbf R^d} h_n(x)\lambda(\,\mathrm d x)\to\int_{\mathbf R^d} h(x)\lambda(\,\mathrm d x)$.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
For a smooth test function $f$, define
\[
 R_f\colon\left|\begin{aligned}
  \mathbf R^d\times\mathbf R^d&\longrightarrow \mathbf R\\
  (x,y)&\longmapsto f(x+y)-f(x)-y\cdot\nabla f(x)
\end{aligned}\right..
\]
Let $\mathcal D_\varepsilon$ be the exact difference between the terminal
L\'evy generators. Then
\begin{align*}
 \mathcal D_\varepsilon f(x) &=\varepsilon\alpha\cdot\nabla f(x) +\frac\varepsilon2 C:\nabla^2f(x) +\varepsilon\int_{\mathbf R^d} R_f(x,y)\eta(\,\mathrm d y)\\
 &\quad+\int_{\mathbf R^{2d}}\bigl[R_f(x,y+\varepsilon z)-R_f(x,y)\bigr]\Pi(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d z).
\end{align*}
Taylor's formula in $y$ gives
\begin{align*}
 R_f(x,y+\varepsilon z)-R_f(x,y)
 &=\varepsilon z\cdot[\nabla f(x+y)-\nabla f(x)]
   +\frac{\varepsilon^2}{2}z^\top\nabla^2f(x+y)z\\
 &\quad+\frac{\varepsilon^3}{2}\int_0^1(1-r)^2
       D^3f(x+y+r\varepsilon z)[z,z,z]\,\mathrm d r.
\end{align*}
Thus $\mathcal D_\varepsilon=\varepsilon\mathcal A+
\varepsilon^2\mathcal B+\mathcal E_\varepsilon$, where
\begin{align*}
 \mathcal Af(x)&=\alpha\cdot\nabla f(x)+\tfrac12C:\nabla^2f(x)
  +\int_{\mathbf R^d} R_f(x,y)\eta(\,\mathrm d y)
  +\int_{\mathbf R^d}[\nabla f(x+y)-\nabla f(x)]\cdot J(\,\mathrm d y),\\
 \mathcal Bf(x)&=\tfrac12\sum_{j,k=1}^d\int_{\mathbf R^d}
                 \partial_{jk}f(x+y)S_{jk}(\,\mathrm d y),\\
 \left\lVert \mathcal E_\varepsilon f\right\rVert_\infty
 &\leq C|\varepsilon|^3\max_{|\beta|=3}\left\lVert D^\beta f\right\rVert_\infty.
\end{align*}
The support and velocity bounds also imply
\[
 \left\lVert \mathcal D_\varepsilon f\right\rVert_\infty
  +|\varepsilon|\left\lVert \mathcal Af\right\rVert_\infty
 \leq C|\varepsilon|\sum_{j=1}^2\left\lVert D^jf\right\rVert_\infty,\qquad \left\lVert (\mathcal D_\varepsilon-\varepsilon\mathcal A)f\right\rVert_\infty
 \leq C\varepsilon^2\left\lVert D^2f\right\rVert_\infty.
\]
All these operators commute with translations and spatial derivatives.
Consequently,
\begin{align*}
 \mathcal D_\varepsilon^2-\varepsilon^2\mathcal A^2
 &=(\mathcal D_\varepsilon-\varepsilon\mathcal A)
   (\mathcal D_\varepsilon+\varepsilon\mathcal A),\\
 \left\lVert (\mathcal D_\varepsilon^2-\varepsilon^2\mathcal A^2)f\right\rVert_\infty
 &\leq C|\varepsilon|^3\sum_{j=3}^4\left\lVert D^jf\right\rVert_\infty,\\
 \left\lVert \mathcal D_\varepsilon^3f\right\rVert_\infty
 &\leq C|\varepsilon|^3\sum_{j=3}^6\left\lVert D^jf\right\rVert_\infty.
\end{align*}
Let $\pi_{u,\varepsilon}$ have exponent
$(1-u)\Psi+u\Psi_\varepsilon$, $u\in[0,1]$, and put
$Q_{u,\varepsilon}h(x)=\int_{\mathbf R^d} h(x+y)\pi_{u,\varepsilon}(\,\mathrm d y)$, with
$Q_0=Q_{0,\varepsilon}$. For $h\in\mathcal S(\mathbf R^d)$, Fourier inversion yields
\[
 \partial_u^jQ_{u,\varepsilon}h
       =\mathcal D_\varepsilon^jQ_{u,\varepsilon}h,
 \qquad j=1,2,3.
\]
Indeed, the Fourier integrands are bounded by
$C(1+|\xi|)^6|\widehat h(\xi)|$, since the exponents have nonpositive
real parts and at most quadratic growth. Taylor's formula in $u$ gives
\[
 (Q_{1,\varepsilon}-Q_0)h
 =\mathcal D_\varepsilon Q_0h
  +\tfrac12\mathcal D_\varepsilon^2Q_0h
  +\tfrac12\int_0^1(1-u)^2
               \mathcal D_\varepsilon^3Q_{u,\varepsilon}h\,\mathrm d u.
\]
The Gaussian covariance of $\pi_{u,\varepsilon}$ is
$V+u\varepsilon C\succeq\ell I_d$. Factor its density as
$g_{\ell I_d}*\zeta_{u,\varepsilon}$ with $\zeta_{u,\varepsilon}$ a
probability law. For a multi-index $\beta$ with $1\leq|\beta|\leq6$,
choose $j$ with $\beta_j>0$. For $\operatorname{Lip}(h)\leq1$, Young's inequality
and the Lipschitz bound give
\[
 \left\lVert D^\beta Q_{u,\varepsilon}h\right\rVert_\infty =\left\lVert (\partial_jh)*(D^{\beta-e_j}g_{\ell I_d})* \check\zeta_{u,\varepsilon}\right\rVert_\infty \leq\left\lVert \partial_jh\right\rVert_\infty \left\lVert D^{\beta-e_j}g_{\ell I_d}\right\rVert_1 \leq \left\lVert D^{\beta-e_j}g_{\ell I_d}\right\rVert_1,
\]
where $e_j$ is the $j$th coordinate multi-index. Substituting these bounds gives
\[
 \sup_{\operatorname{Lip}(h)\leq1}\left|
 \int_{\mathbf R^d} h(x)(\lambda_\varepsilon-\lambda)(\,\mathrm d x)
 -\varepsilon\mathcal A Q_0h(0)
 -\varepsilon^2(\mathcal B+\tfrac12\mathcal A^2)Q_0h(0)
 \right|\leq C_0|\varepsilon|^3.
\]
We first take this supremum over Schwartz tests. The approximation below
extends it to Lipschitz tests as stated.
The spatial adjoints are
\begin{align*}
 \mathcal A^*u
 &=-\alpha\cdot\nabla u+\tfrac12 C:\nabla^2u
   +\int_{\mathbf R^d}[u(\cdot-y)-u+y\cdot\nabla u]\eta(\,\mathrm d y)\\
 &\quad+\int_{\mathbf R^d}[\nabla u-\nabla u(\cdot-y)]\cdot J(\,\mathrm d y),\\
 \mathcal B^*u&=\tfrac12\sum_{j,k=1}^d
                         \int_{\mathbf R^d}\partial_{jk}u(\cdot-y)S_{jk}(\,\mathrm d y).
\end{align*}
Writing $p=g_V*\rho$ with $\rho\in\mathcal P_1(\mathbf R^d)$, Fubini's theorem,
a change of variables, and the triangle inequality give, for every
multi-index $\beta$,
\begin{align*}
 \int_{\mathbf R^d}(1+|x|)|D^\beta p(x)|\,\mathrm d x
 &\leq\int_{\mathbf R^d}\rho(\,\mathrm d y)
                  \int_{\mathbf R^d}(1+|x|)|D^\beta g_V(x-y)|\,\mathrm d x\\
 &=\int_{\mathbf R^d}\rho(\,\mathrm d y)
                  \int_{\mathbf R^d}(1+|z+y|)|D^\beta g_V(z)|\,\mathrm d z\\
 &\leq\left(1+\int_{\mathbf R^d}|y|\rho(\,\mathrm d y)\right)
             \int_{\mathbf R^d}(1+|z|)|D^\beta g_V(z)|\,\mathrm d z<\infty.
\end{align*}
Compact translations preserve this property. Each adjoint term integrates
to zero, so
\[
 r_1=\mathcal A^*p,\qquad
 r_2=\mathcal B^*p+\tfrac12(\mathcal A^*)^2p
\]
are zero-mass densities with finite first absolute moments. Integration by
parts gives
$\mathcal A Q_0h(0)=\int_{\mathbf R^d} h(x)r_1(x)\,\mathrm d x$ and
$(\mathcal B+\mathcal A^2/2)Q_0h(0)=\int_{\mathbf R^d} h(x)r_2(x)\,\mathrm d x$.
Lemma~\ref{lem:approximation} and these weighted integrability bounds extend the estimate to
all Lipschitz tests. Hence
\[
 \left\lVert p_\varepsilon-p-\varepsilon r_1-\varepsilon^2r_2\right\rVert_{\mathrm{KR}}
 \leq C_0|\varepsilon|^3,
 \qquad
 \left|W_1(\lambda_\varepsilon,\lambda)
       -\left\lVert \varepsilon r_1+\varepsilon^2r_2\right\rVert_{\mathrm{KR}}\right|
 \leq C_0|\varepsilon|^3.
\]
For injectivity,
\[
 \widehat r_1(\xi)=\dot\Psi(-\xi)\widehat p(\xi),\qquad
 \dot\Psi(\xi)=i\alpha\cdot\xi-\tfrac12\xi^\top C\xi
  +\langle e^{i\xi\cdot y}-1-i\xi\cdot y,\tau\rangle.
\]
Since $\widehat p=e^{\Psi(-\cdot)}$ never vanishes, $r_1=0$ implies
$\dot\Psi=0$. The inverse Fourier transform of the exponential term is
$\tau$ up to reflection, whereas the polynomial terms have inverse
transforms supported at $0$. Restricting to $\mathbf R^d\setminus\{0\}$ gives
$\tau=0$, because $\operatorname{supp}\tau\subset A_{r_0,R_0}$. The remaining
identity $i\alpha\cdot\xi-\xi^\top C\xi/2=0$ gives $\alpha=C=0$ by its
first and second derivatives at $\xi=0$. The converse follows from the
same multiplier identity. Thus the linear map
$(\alpha,C,\tau)\mapsto r_1$ is injective and its KR norm is a norm.
For an orthogonal matrix $U$, $h\mapsto h\circ U$ preserves the test class,
so simultaneous rotations preserve this norm. If $\zeta\in\mathcal P_1$ is an
independent infinitely divisible convolution factor, then
the response becomes $r_1\mapsto r_1*\zeta$.
Since $\operatorname{Lip}(h*\check\zeta)\leq\operatorname{Lip}(h)$, Fubini's theorem and
Kantorovich duality give
\begin{align*}
 \left\lVert r_1*\zeta\right\rVert_{\mathrm{KR}}
 &=\sup_{\operatorname{Lip}(h)\leq1}\left|\int_{\mathbf R^d} h(x)(r_1*\zeta)(x)\,\mathrm d x\right|\\
 &=\sup_{\operatorname{Lip}(h)\leq1}\left|\int_{\mathbf R^d}(h*\check\zeta)(x)r_1(x)\,\mathrm d x\right| \leq\left\lVert r_1\right\rVert_{\mathrm{KR}},
\end{align*}
which proves contraction.
If $\alpha=C=\eta=0$ and $J=0$, then $\mathcal A=0$ and
$r_2=\mathcal B^*p$. Moreover,
\[
 \widehat{\mathcal B^*p}(\xi)
 =-\tfrac12\widehat p(\xi)\sum_{j,k=1}^d
                                     \xi_j\xi_k\widehat S_{jk}(\xi).
\]
The matrix $M=S(\mathbf R^d)=\int_{\mathbf R^{2d}} zz^\top\Pi(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d z)$ is positive semidefinite and
$\operatorname{tr}M=\int_{\mathbf R^{2d}}|z|^2\Pi(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d z)$. If this trace is positive, choose a
unit vector $e$ with $e^\top Me>0$. Continuity of the Fourier transforms
of the finite measures $S_{jk}$ yields
\[
 \lim_{t\to0}\frac{\widehat{\mathcal B^*p}(te)}{t^2}
 =-\tfrac12e^\top Me<0.
\]
Therefore $\mathcal B^*p\ne0$ and
$\left\lVert \mathcal B^*p\right\rVert_{\mathrm{KR}}>0$. This positivity argument applies
to dispersion matrices generated by the positive lift $\Pi$. It does not
establish injectivity for arbitrary signed matrix measures.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- theorem-16: body_md -->
{sup:thm:state-response}
Let $p_T^\varepsilon=P_T^{\varepsilon,*}p_0$. Define the Bochner integrals
\begin{align}
 q_1(T)&=\int_0^T P_{T-t}^*\mathcal A^*P_t^*p_0\,\mathrm d t,
                                      \label{sup:eq:state-q1}\\
 q_2(T)&=\int_0^T P_{T-t}^*\mathcal B^*P_t^*p_0\,\mathrm d t
 +\int_{0<s<t<T}P_{T-t}^*\mathcal A^*P_{t-s}^*
                         \mathcal A^*P_s^*p_0\,\mathrm d s\,\mathrm d t.
                                      \label{sup:eq:state-q2}
\end{align}
Then $q_1(T)\in X_2$, $q_2(T)\in X_1$, and, for $|\varepsilon|\leq1$,
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:state-remainder}
 \left\lVert p_T^\varepsilon-p_T^0-\varepsilon q_1(T)-\varepsilon^2q_2(T)\right\rVert_{X_0}
                  \leq C_T|\varepsilon|^3.
\end{equation}
The finite constant $C_T$ depends only on $T$, the support bound of $\Pi$,
$\left\lVert c\right\rVert_{C_b^3}$ and $\left\lVert p_0\right\rVert_{X_3}$. Both coefficients have zero
integral. Their primitives
$H_j(x)=\int_{-\infty}^xq_j(T,z)\,\mathrm d z$, $j=1,2$, belong to $L^1(\mathbf R)$ and
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:state-W1}
 \left|W_1(\operatorname{Law}(X_T^\varepsilon),\operatorname{Law}(X_T^0))
            -\left\lVert \varepsilon H_1+\varepsilon^2H_2\right\rVert_1\right|
                  \leq C_T|\varepsilon|^3.
\end{equation}
If $\int_{\mathbf R^2} zf(y)\Pi(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d z)=0$ for every bounded measurable $f:\mathbf R\to\mathbf R$, then
$\mathcal A^*=0$, $H_1=0$, and the time-ordered double integral vanishes.
Consequently,
$W_1(\operatorname{Law}(X_T^\varepsilon),\operatorname{Law}(X_T^0))
 =\varepsilon^2\left\lVert H_2\right\rVert_1+O(|\varepsilon|^3)$.
<!-- end theorem-16 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
Let $T_au=u(\cdot-a)$. A change of variables and the inequality
$1+|x+a|\leq(1+|a|)(1+|x|)$ give
\[
 \left\lVert T_au\right\rVert_{X_k}
 =
      \sum_{j=0}^k\int_{\mathbf R}(1+|x+a|)|D^ju(x)|\,\mathrm d x \leq
      (1+|a|)\sum_{j=0}^k\int_{\mathbf R}(1+|x|)|D^ju(x)|\,\mathrm d x =(1+|a|)\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{X_k}.
\]
For the heat semigroup $\mathsf H_tu=g_t*u$, with $\mathsf H_0=I$,
apply this estimate under the convolution integral. Then Tonelli's
theorem and the first absolute Gaussian moment give, for $t>0$,
\[
 \left\lVert \mathsf H_tu\right\rVert_{X_k}
 \leq\int_{\mathbf R} g_t(a)\left\lVert T_au\right\rVert_{X_k}\,\mathrm d a \leq
        \left\lVert u\right\rVert_{X_k}\int_{\mathbf R}(1+|a|)g_t(a)\,\mathrm d a =(1+\sqrt{2/\pi}\sqrt t)\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{X_k}.
\]
The same bound holds at $t=0$.
Multiplication by $c$ is bounded on $X_k$ for $k\leq3$, by
$D^j(cu)=\sum_{i=0}^j\binom ji(D^{j-i}c)D^iu$.
Hence the jump adjoint
\[
 \mathcal J_\varepsilon^*u
 =\int_{\mathbf R^2} T_{y+\varepsilon z}(cu)\Pi(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d z)-cu
\]
is bounded on each $X_k$, uniformly over $|\varepsilon|\leq1$.
The perturbation series
\[
 P_t^{\varepsilon,*}
 =\mathsf H_t+\sum_{n\geq1}\int_{0<t_1<\cdots<t_n<t}
  \mathsf H_{t-t_n}\mathcal J_\varepsilon^*
  \mathsf H_{t_n-t_{n-1}}\cdots
  \mathcal J_\varepsilon^*\mathsf H_{t_1}\,\mathrm d t_1\cdots\,\mathrm d t_n
\]
converges in operator norm on $X_k$: its $n$th summand has norm at most
$M_T^{n+1}B_k^nt^n/n!$, where $M_T=1+\sqrt{2/\pi}\sqrt T$ and
$B_k=\sup_{|\varepsilon|\leq1}
\left\lVert \mathcal J_\varepsilon^*\right\rVert_{X_k\to X_k}<\infty$.
Thus
\[
 \sup_{t\leq T,\,|\varepsilon|\leq1}
 \left\lVert P_t^{\varepsilon,*}\right\rVert_{X_k\to X_k}\leq C_T,
 \qquad k=0,1,2,3.
\]
These strongly continuous semigroups agree with the bounded-rate process
and preserve positivity and mass.
Put $\Delta_\varepsilon=\mathcal J_\varepsilon^*-\mathcal J_0^*$.
For $v=cu$ and weak derivatives through order three,
\begin{align*}
 T_{y+\varepsilon z}v-T_yv
 &=-\varepsilon z\int_0^1T_{y+r\varepsilon z}Dv\,\mathrm d r,\\
 T_{y+\varepsilon z}v-T_yv+\varepsilon zT_yDv
 &=\varepsilon^2z^2\int_0^1(1-r)
                     T_{y+r\varepsilon z}D^2v\,\mathrm d r,\\
 T_{y+\varepsilon z}v-T_yv+\varepsilon zT_yDv
                  -\tfrac12\varepsilon^2z^2T_yD^2v
 &=-\tfrac12\varepsilon^3z^3\int_0^1(1-r)^2
                     T_{y+r\varepsilon z}D^3v\,\mathrm d r.
\end{align*}
The shifts are uniformly bounded. Integrating against $\Pi$ therefore gives
\begin{align*}
 \left\lVert \Delta_\varepsilon u\right\rVert_{X_k}
       &\leq C|\varepsilon|\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{X_{k+1}} &&(k\leq2),\\
 \left\lVert (\Delta_\varepsilon-\varepsilon\mathcal A^*)u\right\rVert_{X_k}
       &\leq C\varepsilon^2\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{X_{k+2}} &&(k\leq1),\\
 \left\lVert (\Delta_\varepsilon-\varepsilon\mathcal A^*
                       -\varepsilon^2\mathcal B^*)u\right\rVert_{X_0}
       &\leq C|\varepsilon|^3\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{X_3}.
\end{align*}
Iterating Duhamel's identity
\[
 P_T^{\varepsilon,*}=P_T^*
       +\int_0^T P_{T-t}^*\Delta_\varepsilon
                                  P_t^{\varepsilon,*}\,\mathrm d t
\]
gives, on $X_3$,
\begin{align*}
 P_T^{\varepsilon,*}-P_T^*
 &=\int_0^TP_{T-t}^*\Delta_\varepsilon P_t^*\,\mathrm d t\\
 &\quad+\int_{0<s<t<T}P_{T-t}^*\Delta_\varepsilon P_{t-s}^*
                                      \Delta_\varepsilon P_s^*\,\mathrm d s\,\mathrm d t
       +R_{3,\varepsilon},\\
 R_{3,\varepsilon}
 &=\int_{0<r<s<t<T}P_{T-t}^*\Delta_\varepsilon P_{t-s}^*
       \Delta_\varepsilon P_{s-r}^*\Delta_\varepsilon
                         P_r^{\varepsilon,*}\,\mathrm d r\,\mathrm d s\,\mathrm d t.
\end{align*}
Three applications of the generator difference successively map $X_3$ to $X_2$, $X_1$, and $X_0$, so
\[
 \left\lVert R_{3,\varepsilon}\right\rVert_{X_3\to X_0}
 \leq
       C_T|\varepsilon|^3\int_0^T\int_0^t\int_0^s\,\mathrm d r\,\mathrm d s\,\mathrm d t =C_T|\varepsilon|^3\frac{T^3}{6}.
\]
For the double integral, use the exact identity
\[
 \Delta_\varepsilon P_{t-s}^*\Delta_\varepsilon -\varepsilon^2\mathcal A^*P_{t-s}^*\mathcal A^* =(\Delta_\varepsilon-\varepsilon\mathcal A^*) P_{t-s}^*\Delta_\varepsilon +\varepsilon\mathcal A^*P_{t-s}^* (\Delta_\varepsilon-\varepsilon\mathcal A^*).
\]
Both terms have norm at most $C_T|\varepsilon|^3$ from $X_3$ to $X_0$:
the derivative losses are respectively $1+2$ and $2+1$.
In the single integral, the generator difference expands as
$\varepsilon\mathcal A^*+\varepsilon^2\mathcal B^*$ with the same cubic
error. Integrating over the time simplexes proves
\eqref{sup:eq:state-remainder} and \eqref{sup:eq:state-q1}--\eqref{sup:eq:state-q2}.
For $v\in W^{1,1}(\mathbf R)$, $\int_{\mathbf R} Dv(x)\,\mathrm d x=0$. Applying this also to $Dv$ gives
$\int_{\mathbf R} D^2v(x)\,\mathrm d x=0$ when $v\in W^{2,1}$. Thus both operators in
\eqref{sup:eq:state-operators} have zero mass. Since $P_t^*$ preserves mass, we obtain $\int_{\mathbf R} q_j(T,x)\,\mathrm d x=0$.
For every zero-mass $u\in X_0$, its primitive satisfies
\[
 U(x)=\int_{-\infty}^xu(z)\,\mathrm d z =
   \int_{\mathbf R}\bigl[\mathbf 1_{\{z\leq x\}}-\mathbf 1_{\{0\leq x\}}\bigr]u(z)\,\mathrm d z.
\]
Then Tonelli's theorem and the length of the interval between zero and
$z$ give
\[
 \left\lVert U\right\rVert_1
 \leq\int_{\mathbf R}|u(z)|\int_{\mathbf R}
       |\mathbf 1_{\{z\leq x\}}-\mathbf 1_{\{0\leq x\}}|\,\mathrm d x\,\mathrm d z =\int_{\mathbf R}|z||u(z)|\,\mathrm d z \leq\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{X_0}.
\]
The one-dimensional identity $W_1(\lambda,\widetilde\lambda)
=\left\lVert F_\lambda-F_{\widetilde\lambda}\right\rVert_1$, where $F_\lambda$ is the CDF,
and the reverse triangle inequality now give \eqref{sup:eq:state-W1}.
Finally, conditional balance means that the signed measure
$B\mapsto\int_{\mathbf R^2}\mathbf 1_B(y)z\Pi(\,\mathrm d y,\,\mathrm d z)$ vanishes, so \eqref{sup:eq:state-operators}
gives $\mathcal A^*=0$.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
{sup:lem:comparison}
Let $P^1_{t,T}f(x)=\mathbf E\left[f(X_T^1)\mid X_t^1=x\right]$ and let
$v(t,x)=P^1_{t,T}f(x)$ solve
$\partial_tv+\mathcal L_1(t)v=0$, $v(T)=f$.
Suppose localized It\^o's formula applies to $v(t,X^0_t)$, with stopped
martingales of zero expectation and uniformly integrable stopped terminal
values. If
$\mathbf E\left[\int_0^T|((\mathcal L_0-\mathcal L_1)v)(t,X^0_{t-})|\,\mathrm d t\right]<\infty$, then
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:comparison}
 \mathbf E\left[f(X^0_T)\right]-\mathbf E\left[f(X^1_T)\right] =\mathbf E\left[P^1_{0,T}f(X^0_0)-P^1_{0,T}f(X^1_0)\right] +\int_0^T\mathbf E\left[((\mathcal L_0(t)-\mathcal L_1(t))v(t))(X^0_{t-})\right]\,\mathrm d t.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
Choose localizing times $\tau_n=\sigma_n\wedge T$ with
$\sigma_n\uparrow\infty$. It\^o's formula and the backward equation give
\begin{align*}
 \mathbf E\left[v(\tau_n,X^0_{\tau_n})\right]-\mathbf E\left[v(0,X^0_0)\right]
 &=\mathbf E\left[\int_0^{\tau_n}(\partial_t+\mathcal L_0(t))v(t,X^0_{t-})\,\mathrm d t\right]\\
 &=\mathbf E\left[\int_0^T\mathbf 1_{\{t\leq\tau_n\}}
                   ((\mathcal L_0-\mathcal L_1)v)(t,X^0_{t-})\,\mathrm d t\right].
\end{align*}
By uniform integrability, the left-hand side converges to
$\mathbf E\left[f(X_T^0)\right]-\mathbf E\left[P_{0,T}^1f(X_0^0)\right]$. Dominated convergence gives the
integral without stopping on the right. Subtract
$\mathbf E\left[f(X_T^1)\right]=\mathbf E\left[P_{0,T}^1f(X_0^1)\right]$ to obtain the identity.
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- proposition-9: body_md -->
{sup:prop:external-regularization}
Assume \eqref{sup:eq:comparison} for $h_\varepsilon$ and, for $k=1,2,3$,
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:derivative-transfer}
 \left\lVert D^kP^1_{t,T}f\right\rVert_\infty
 \leq C_F\sum_{j=1}^k\left\lVert D^jf\right\rVert_\infty,
 \qquad 0\leq t\leq T,
\end{equation}
for smooth $f$ of at most linear growth with bounded first three
derivatives. If $\theta_a,J_1\leq1$, then
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:external-rate}
 W_1(\operatorname{Law}(X^0_T),\operatorname{Law}(X^1_T))
 \leq CC_F\bigl(\theta_0+\theta_b+\theta_a^{1/2}+J_1^{1/3}\bigr).
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-9 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
Put $v_\varepsilon(t)=P_{t,T}^1h_\varepsilon$. For
$0<\varepsilon\leq1$, \eqref{sup:eq:derivative-transfer} and
\eqref{sup:eq:external-gaussian} give
\[
 \left\lVert v_\varepsilon'\right\rVert_\infty\leq C_F,\quad
 \left\lVert v_\varepsilon''\right\rVert_\infty\leq CC_F\varepsilon^{-1/2},\quad
 \left\lVert v_\varepsilon'''\right\rVert_\infty\leq CC_F\varepsilon^{-1}.
\]
The initial discrepancy is at most $C_F\theta_0$. Substituting the
bounds into \eqref{sup:eq:comparison}, adding
$2\sqrt{2/\pi}\sqrt\varepsilon$ for terminal regularization, and taking the
supremum over Lipschitz tests gives
\[
 W_1\overset{\eqref{sup:eq:comparison}}{\leq}
 CC_F\bigl(\theta_0+\theta_b+\sqrt\varepsilon
                     +\theta_a\varepsilon^{-1/2}+J_1\varepsilon^{-1}\bigr).
\]
If $\varepsilon=\max\{\theta_a,J_1^{2/3}\}>0$, then
\[
 \sqrt\varepsilon\leq\theta_a^{1/2}+J_1^{1/3},\qquad
 \theta_a\varepsilon^{-1/2}\leq\theta_a^{1/2},\qquad
 J_1\varepsilon^{-1}\leq J_1^{1/3}.
\]
If both discrepancies vanish, let $\varepsilon\downarrow0$.
<!-- end proof-35 -->
<!-- assumption-2: body_md -->
{sup:ass:one-sided}
For every $\operatorname{Lip}(h)\leq1$ and $0<\varepsilon\leq1$, the comparison identity
holds for $v_\varepsilon(t)=P^1_{t,T}h_\varepsilon$. There is $C_S\geq1$,
independent of $h,\varepsilon,t$, such that
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:one-sided-derivatives}
 \left\lVert Dv_\varepsilon(t)\right\rVert_\infty\leq C_S,\qquad
 \left\lVert D^2v_\varepsilon(t)\right\rVert_\infty\leq C_S(\varepsilon+T-t)^{-1/2},\qquad
 \left\lVert D^3v_\varepsilon(t)\right\rVert_\infty\leq C_S(\varepsilon+T-t)^{-1}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end assumption-2 -->
<!-- proposition-10: body_md -->
{sup:prop:additive-comparison}
Suppose model~$1$ has deterministic characteristics satisfying
\eqref{eq:add-int} and $a_1(t)\geq\lambda>0$ almost everywhere.
Then Assumption~\ref{sup:ass:one-sided} holds for the comparisons above, with
$C_S=\max\{1,\sqrt{2/\pi}\kappa^{-1/2},\sqrt{8/(\pi e)}\kappa^{-1}\}$ and
$\kappa=\min\{1,\lambda\}$. The jump-size measure need not have a density.
<!-- end proposition-10 -->
<!-- proof-36: body_md -->
Let $V_{t,T}=\int_t^Ta_1(s)\,\mathrm d s$ and let
$\eta_{t,T}\in\mathcal P_1(\mathbf R)$ be the drift-and-jump increment law.
Independence of the Gaussian and jump parts gives
\[
 v_\varepsilon(t)=h*g_{\varepsilon+V_{t,T}}*\check\eta_{t,T},
 \qquad\varepsilon+V_{t,T}\geq\kappa(\varepsilon+T-t).
\]
Convolution with a probability law does not increase the supremum norm. Applying
\eqref{eq:gauss-L1}, equivalently \eqref{sup:eq:external-gaussian} with variance
$\varepsilon+V_{t,T}$, proves the three derivative bounds.
For fixed $\varepsilon>0$, the increment Dynkin identity and translation
invariance give
\[
 v_\varepsilon(t)=h_\varepsilon+
                 \int_t^T\mathcal L_1(s)v_\varepsilon(s)\,\mathrm d s.
\]
Set $M(s)=a_1(s)+\int_{\mathbf R} y^2\nu_1(s,\,\mathrm d y)\in L^1(0,T)$.
For $k=0,1,2$, Taylor's bound for the compensated jump term gives
\begin{align*}
 \left\lVert \mathcal L_1(s)D^kv_\varepsilon(s)\right\rVert_\infty
 &\leq|b_1(s)|\left\lVert D^{k+1}v_\varepsilon(s)\right\rVert_\infty
       +\tfrac12M(s)\left\lVert D^{k+2}v_\varepsilon(s)\right\rVert_\infty\\
 &\leq|b_1(s)|\left\lVert D^{k+1}h_\varepsilon\right\rVert_\infty
       +\tfrac12M(s)\left\lVert D^{k+2}h_\varepsilon\right\rVert_\infty.
\end{align*}
All derivatives of $h_\varepsilon$ of positive order are bounded. The
right-hand sides are deterministic and integrable. They justify absolute
continuity in time through two spatial derivatives. Mollifying in time
and then using dominated convergence gives the localized It\^o formula.
Equation~\eqref{sup:eq:semimartingale-integrability} bounds the model~$0$ terms.
The comparison integrand is integrable by the profile bounds.
Finally, increment first moments are uniformly bounded, so
\[
 |v_\varepsilon(\tau_n,X^0_{\tau_n})|
 \leq C_{h,\varepsilon}\bigl(1+\sup_{t\leq T}|X_t^0|\bigr).
\]
The right-hand side is integrable, so the family is uniformly integrable.
Lemma~\ref{sup:lem:comparison} now applies.
<!-- end proof-36 -->
<!-- theorem-17: body_md -->
{sup:thm:master}
Under Assumption~\ref{sup:ass:one-sided}, for every $0<\varepsilon\leq1$,
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:master}
\begin{aligned}
 W_1(\operatorname{Law}(X^0_T),\operatorname{Law}(X^1_T))
 \leq C\biggl[&\theta_0+\theta_b+\sqrt\varepsilon
       +\int_0^T\frac{r_a(t)}{\sqrt{\varepsilon+T-t}}\,\mathrm d t\\
 &+\int_0^Tr_\nu(t)
       \bigl((\varepsilon+T-t)^{-1/2}+(\varepsilon+T-t)^{-1}\bigr)\,\mathrm d t
       \biggr],
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where $C$ depends only on $C_S$.
<!-- end theorem-17 -->
<!-- proof-37: body_md -->
Let $s=T-t$. Weighted Taylor testing and
\eqref{sup:eq:one-sided-derivatives} give
\[
 \mathbf E\left[|((\mathcal L_0-\mathcal L_1)v_\varepsilon)(t,X^0_{t-})|\right]
 \overset{\eqref{sup:eq:one-sided-derivatives}}{\leq}
 C_S\left[r_b(t)+\frac{r_a(t)}{2\sqrt{\varepsilon+s}}
 +r_\nu(t)\left(\frac1{2\sqrt{\varepsilon+s}}
                           +\frac1{6(\varepsilon+s)}\right)\right].
\]
In \eqref{sup:eq:comparison}, the initial term is at most $C_S\theta_0$.
Integrate this estimate, add the terminal smoothing error
$2\sqrt{2/\pi}\sqrt\varepsilon$, and take the supremum over $\operatorname{Lip}(h)\leq1$.
<!-- end proof-37 -->
<!-- corollary-4: body_md -->
{sup:cor:profile-rates}
Suppose Assumption~\ref{sup:ass:one-sided} holds and $\theta_a\leq1$. For
$1\leq q<\infty$ and $J_q\leq1$,
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:finite-q}
 W_1\leq C_{q,T,C_S}
   \bigl(\theta_0+\theta_b+\theta_a^{1/2}+J_q^{q/(q+2)}\bigr).
\end{equation}
For $J_\infty\leq e^{-1}$,
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:gaussian-profile-log}
 W_1\leq C_{T,C_S}
   \bigl(\theta_0+\theta_b+\theta_a^{1/2}
                +J_\infty(1+|\log J_\infty|)\bigr),
\end{equation}
where the last term is zero at $J_\infty=0$.
<!-- end corollary-4 -->
<!-- proof-38: body_md -->
For $1<q<\infty$, let $q'=q/(q-1)$. Direct integration gives
\[
 \left\lVert (\varepsilon+\cdot)^{-1}\right\rVert_{L^{q'}(0,T)}^{q'}
 =\int_0^T(\varepsilon+s)^{-q'}\,\mathrm d s =
    \frac{\varepsilon^{1-q'}-(\varepsilon+T)^{1-q'}}{q'-1} \leq\frac{\varepsilon^{1-q'}}{q'-1}.
\]
Taking the $q'$th root and using $1/q+1/q'=1$ gives
\[
 \left\lVert (\varepsilon+\cdot)^{-1}\right\rVert_{L^{q'}(0,T)}
 \leq(q'-1)^{-1/q'}\varepsilon^{-1/q}.
\]
For $q=1$, the $L^\infty$ norm equals $\varepsilon^{-1}$.
H\"older's inequality, $u^{-1/2}\leq1+u^{-1}$, and
$\varepsilon\leq1$ therefore reduce \eqref{sup:eq:master} to
\[
 W_1\overset{\eqref{sup:eq:master}}{\leq}
 C_{q,T,C_S}\bigl(\theta_0+\theta_b+\sqrt\varepsilon
       +\theta_a\varepsilon^{-1/2}+J_q\varepsilon^{-1/q}\bigr).
\]
Take $\varepsilon=\max\{\theta_a,J_q^{2q/(q+2)}\}$. Then
\[
 \sqrt\varepsilon\leq\theta_a^{1/2}+J_q^{q/(q+2)},\quad
 \theta_a\varepsilon^{-1/2}\leq\theta_a^{1/2},\quad
 J_q\varepsilon^{-1/q}\leq J_q^{q/(q+2)}.
\]
For $q=\infty$, compute instead
\[
 \int_0^T\frac{\,\mathrm d s}{\varepsilon+s}
    =\log\frac{\varepsilon+T}{\varepsilon},\qquad
 \int_0^T\frac{\,\mathrm d s}{\sqrt{\varepsilon+s}}
    =2(\sqrt{\varepsilon+T}-\sqrt\varepsilon)\leq2\sqrt T.
\]
With $\varepsilon=\max\{\theta_a,J_\infty^2\}$, the first two error
terms are bounded by $\theta_a^{1/2}+J_\infty$, and, when $J_\infty>0$,
\[
 J_\infty\log\frac{\varepsilon+T}{\varepsilon}
 \leq J_\infty\bigl[\log(1+T)+2|\log J_\infty|\bigr].
\]
If the chosen maximum vanishes, let $\varepsilon\downarrow0$.
<!-- end proof-38 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
{sup:rem:operator-singularity}
For $H_sh(x)=\mathbf E\left[h(x+\sqrt sG)\right]$, $s>0$, $G\sim N(0,1)$, and $h(x)=|x|$,
\[
 D^2|x|=2\delta_0,\qquad
 (H_sh)''(x)=2s^{-1/2}\varphi(x/\sqrt s),\qquad
 (H_sh)'''(x)=-2xs^{-3/2}\varphi(x/\sqrt s).
\]
Since $\sup_{r\geq0}r\varphi(r)=\varphi(1)$,
$\left\lVert (H_sh)'''\right\rVert_\infty=2\varphi(1)s^{-1}$.
Thus the singularity in \eqref{sup:eq:master} is sharp as a uniform
operator bound on Lipschitz tests. Sharpness of the resulting logarithm
or finite-$q$ rates for terminal laws requires lower bounds under the same
smoothing and profile hypotheses. Proposition~\ref{prop:cubic} does not provide these lower bounds.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- proposition-11: body_md -->
{sup:prop:TV-comparison}
Suppose the comparison identity and the first two bounds in
\eqref{sup:eq:one-sided-derivatives} hold. For a pure jump perturbation with
equal initial laws, let
$r_{\mathrm{TV}}(t)
 =\mathbf E\left[\left\lVert y^2(\nu_0-\nu_1)(t,X^0_{t-},\,\mathrm d y)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}\right]\leq K$
and $\theta_{\mathrm{TV}}=\int_0^Tr_{\mathrm{TV}}(t)\,\mathrm d t$.
Then $W_1\leq C\sqrt{K\theta_{\mathrm{TV}}}$.
<!-- end proposition-11 -->
<!-- proof-39: body_md -->
For the total variation pairing, we need only
$\left\lVert \Phi_{v,x}\right\rVert_\infty\leq\left\lVert v''\right\rVert_\infty/2$. Thus
\[
 W_1\leq2\sqrt{2/\pi}\sqrt\varepsilon+
             \tfrac12C_S\int_0^T
                 \frac{r_{\mathrm{TV}}(t)}{\sqrt{\varepsilon+T-t}}\,\mathrm d t.
\]
Let $r(s)=r_{\mathrm{TV}}(T-s)$ and
$m(u)=|\{s\in(0,T):r(s)>u\}|$, where $|E|$ denotes Lebesgue measure.
For any measurable $E\subset(0,T)$, decreasing rearrangement of
$s^{-1/2}$ gives
$\int_Es^{-1/2}\,\mathrm d s\leq\int_0^{|E|}s^{-1/2}\,\mathrm d s=2\sqrt{|E|}$.
Writing $r(s)$ as an integral over its level sets and applying Tonelli's theorem, we obtain
\begin{align*}
 \int_0^T r(s)s^{-1/2}\,\mathrm d s
 &=\int_0^K\int_{\{s\in(0,T):r(s)>u\}}s^{-1/2}\,\mathrm d s\,\mathrm d u\\
 &\leq 2\int_0^K\sqrt{m(u)}\,\mathrm d u\\
 &\leq 2\sqrt{K\int_0^K m(u)\,\mathrm d u}\\
 &=2\sqrt{K\theta_{\mathrm{TV}}}.
\end{align*}
This is finite, so letting $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ proves the bound.
<!-- end proof-39 -->
<!-- theorem-18: body_md -->
{sup:thm:matrix}
Under these assumptions,
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:matrix-stability}
 W_1(\lambda_0,\lambda_1) \leq|m_0-m_1|+\frac{M_{1,d}}2\left\lVert V_0-V_1\right\rVert_* +\left(\frac{M_{1,d}}2+\frac{M_{2,d}}6\right) d_{\mathrm{mat}}(\mu_0,\mu_1).
\end{equation}
We also have the joint bound
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:matrix-joint}
 W_1(\lambda_0,\lambda_1)
 \leq|m_0-m_1|+\left(\frac{M_{1,d}}2+\frac{M_{2,d}}6\right)
                    d_{\mathrm{mat}}(\Gamma_0,\Gamma_1).
\end{equation}
If $V_0,V_1$ are positive definite, put $V_u=(1-u)V_1+uV_0$.
Then
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:matrix-gaussian}
 M_{1,d}\leq \sqrt{2/\pi}\int_0^1\lambda_{\min}(V_u)^{-1/2}\,\mathrm d u,
 \qquad
 M_{2,d}\leq \sqrt{8/(\pi e)}\int_0^1\lambda_{\min}(V_u)^{-1}\,\mathrm d u,
\end{equation}
where $\lambda_{\min}$ denotes the smallest eigenvalue. A common lower bound
$V_0,V_1\succeq\ell I_d$, $\ell>0$, therefore gives
$M_{1,d}\leq \sqrt{2/\pi}\ell^{-1/2}$ and $M_{2,d}\leq \sqrt{8/(\pi e)}\ell^{-1}$,
with no explicit dimension factor.
<!-- end theorem-18 -->
<!-- proof-40: body_md -->
For $h\in\mathcal S(\mathbf R^d)$ with $\operatorname{Lip}(h)\leq1$, set
$q_u=h*\check\rho_u$ and
$A_j(u)=\sup_{|e|=1}\left\lVert D_e^j\rho_u\right\rVert_1$, $j=1,2$.
For unit vectors $e,z$, differentiation gives
\[
 D_e^2q_u=(D_eh)*D_e\check\rho_u,\qquad
 D_zD_e^2q_u=(D_zh)*D_e^2\check\rho_u.
\]
Translation is continuous for the $L^1$ factors, so these distributional
derivatives are continuous and hence classical. Since
$\left\lVert D_eh\right\rVert_\infty\leq1$ and
$\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=\sup_{|e|=1}|e^\top Ae|$ for $A\in\mathrm{Sym}_d$,
\begin{align*}
 \left\lVert \nabla q_u\right\rVert_\infty&\leq1,\qquad
 \left\lVert \nabla^2q_u\right\rVert_{\infty,\mathrm{op}}\leq A_1(u),\\
 |e^\top[\nabla^2q_u(x)-\nabla^2q_u(x')]e|
 &\leq|x-x'|\sup_{|z|=1}\left\lVert D_zD_e^2q_u\right\rVert_\infty
 \leq A_2(u)|x-x'|.
\end{align*}
Taking the supremum in $e$ gives
$\operatorname{Lip}_{\mathrm{op}}(\nabla^2q_u)\leq A_2(u)$.
The matrix Taylor field
\[
 F_{u,x}(y)=\int_0^1(1-r)\nabla^2q_u(x+ry)\,\mathrm d r
\]
satisfies
\begin{align*}
 \sup_y\left\lVert F_{u,x}(y)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}&\leq\tfrac12A_1(u),\\
 \left\lVert F_{u,x}(y)-F_{u,x}(y')\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}
 &\leq A_2(u)|y-y'|\int_0^1r(1-r)\,\mathrm d r
   =\tfrac16A_2(u)|y-y'|,\\
 q_u(x+y)-q_u(x)-y\cdot\nabla q_u(x)
 &=y^\top F_{u,x}(y)y,\qquad
 F_{u,x}(0)=\tfrac12\nabla^2q_u(x).
\end{align*}
Thus $\left\lVert F_{u,x}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL},\mathrm{op}}
\leq A_1(u)/2+A_2(u)/6$.
Let $\mathcal D$ be the difference of the terminal generators and write
$\delta m=m_0-m_1$, $\delta V=V_0-V_1$, and
$\delta\mu=\mu_0-\mu_1$. Fourier differentiation as in Lemma~\ref{lem:interpolation} gives
\[
 \int_{\mathbf R^d} h(x)(\lambda_0-\lambda_1)(\,\mathrm d x)=\int_0^1\mathcal Dq_u(0)\,\mathrm d u.
\]
Indeed, $|e^{ia}-1-ia|\leq a^2/2$ gives
$|\Psi_i(\xi)|\leq|m_i||\xi|+\tfrac12(\left\lVert V_i\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}
+\int_{\mathbf R^d}|y|^2N_i(\,\mathrm d y))|\xi|^2$, so
$C(|\xi|+|\xi|^2)|\widehat h(\xi)|$ is an integrable majorant.
Using $y^\top Fy=\operatorname{tr}(Fyy^\top)$, we have
\[
 \mathcal Dq_u(0)=\delta m\cdot\nabla q_u(0)
 +\tfrac12\operatorname{tr}(\delta V\nabla^2q_u(0))
 +\int_{\mathbf R^d}\operatorname{tr}(F_{u,0}(y)\delta\mu(\,\mathrm d y)).
\]
The inequality $|\operatorname{tr}(AB)|\leq\left\lVert A\right\rVert_*\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}$,
together with the matrix discrepancy and the derivative bounds above,
therefore gives
\begin{align*}
 |\mathcal Dq_u(0)|
 &\overset{\eqref{sup:eq:matrix-discrepancy}}{\leq}
   |\delta m|\left\lVert \nabla q_u\right\rVert_\infty
   +\tfrac12\left\lVert \delta V\right\rVert_*\left\lVert \nabla^2q_u\right\rVert_{\infty,\mathrm{op}} +\left\lVert F_{u,0}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL},\mathrm{op}}
                                  d_{\mathrm{mat}}(\mu_0,\mu_1)\\
 &\leq
   |\delta m|+\tfrac12A_1(u)\left\lVert \delta V\right\rVert_* +\left(\tfrac12A_1(u)+\tfrac16A_2(u)\right)
                                  d_{\mathrm{mat}}(\mu_0,\mu_1).
\end{align*}
Integrating in $u$ proves \eqref{sup:eq:matrix-stability}.
For the joint bound, write instead
\[
 \mathcal Dq_u(0)=\delta m\cdot\nabla q_u(0)
             +\int_{\mathbf R^d}\operatorname{tr}\bigl(F_{u,0}(y)(\Gamma_0-\Gamma_1)(\,\mathrm d y)\bigr),
\]
using $F_{u,0}(0)=\nabla^2q_u(0)/2$.
Lemma~\ref{lem:approximation} extends the two estimates from Schwartz tests to all Lipschitz
tests. The first moments justify dominated convergence.
For $V\succ0$, $|e|=1$, and $a=e^\top V^{-1}e$, direct differentiation gives
\[
 D_eg_V(x)=-(e^\top V^{-1}x)g_V(x),\qquad
 D_e^2g_V(x)=\bigl[(e^\top V^{-1}x)^2-a\bigr]g_V(x).
\]
If $Z\sim N(0,V)$, then $e^\top V^{-1}Z\overset{\mathrm{law}}=\sqrt aG$
for $G\sim N(0,1)$. Hence
\begin{align*}
 \left\lVert D_eg_V\right\rVert_1
 &=\mathbf E\left[|e^\top V^{-1}Z|\right] =\sqrt a\,\mathbf E\left[|G|\right] =\sqrt{2/\pi}\sqrt a,\\
 \left\lVert D_e^2g_V\right\rVert_1
 &=\mathbf E\left[\left|(e^\top V^{-1}Z)^2-a\right|\right] =a\mathbf E\left[|G^2-1|\right] =4a\varphi(1)=\sqrt{8/(\pi e)}a.
\end{align*}
The second identity follows from
$\int_1^\infty(x^2-1)\varphi(x)\,\mathrm d x=
[-x\varphi(x)]_1^\infty=\varphi(1)$ and $\mathbf E\left[G^2-1\right]=0$.
Since $\rho_u=g_{V_u}*\zeta_u$ for a probability law $\zeta_u$,
Young's inequality gives
$\left\lVert D_e^j\rho_u\right\rVert_1\leq\left\lVert D_e^jg_{V_u}\right\rVert_1$, $j=1,2$.
Finally,
$\sup_{|e|=1}e^\top V_u^{-1}e=\lambda_{\min}(V_u)^{-1}$.
Taking suprema and then integrating proves \eqref{sup:eq:matrix-gaussian}.
<!-- end proof-40 -->
<!-- assumption-3: body_md -->
{sup:ass:factor-regularity}
For every $h\in C_b^\infty(\mathbf R)$ with $\operatorname{Lip}(h)\leq1$, the comparison
identity \eqref{sup:eq:comparison} holds for $v(t,\cdot)=P^1_{t,T}h$,
with an integrable scalar integrand. For $t<T$, $v(t,\cdot)\in C_b^3(\mathbf R)$,
and
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:factor-backward}
 \sup_{0\leq t\leq T/2}
   \bigl(\left\lVert v''(t)\right\rVert_\infty+\left\lVert v'''(t)\right\rVert_\infty\bigr)
 +\sup_{0\leq t<T}\left\lVert v'(t)\right\rVert_\infty\leq B.
\end{equation}
For $T/2\leq t\leq T$, $\operatorname{Law}(X_t^0)$ has a density
$p_t\in W^{2,1}(\mathbf R)$ satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:factor-forward}
 \sup_{T/2\leq t\leq T}
   \bigl(\left\lVert p_t'\right\rVert_1+\left\lVert p_t''\right\rVert_1\bigr)\leq F.
\end{equation}
The constants $B\geq1$ and $F\geq0$ are finite and independent of $h$.
<!-- end assumption-3 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
{sup:lem:factor-transfer}
If $q\in W^{2,1}(\mathbf R)$, $w\in L^\infty(\mathbf R)$, and $r\in[0,1]$, then
the map
\[
 A_r\colon\left|\begin{aligned}
  \mathbf R&\longrightarrow \mathbf R\\
  y&\longmapsto -\displaystyle\int_\mathbf R q'(z-ry)w(z)\,\mathrm d z
\end{aligned}\right.
\]
satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:factor-transfer}
 \left\lVert A_r\right\rVert_\infty\leq\left\lVert q'\right\rVert_1\left\lVert w\right\rVert_\infty,
 \qquad
 \operatorname{Lip}(A_r)\leq r\left\lVert q''\right\rVert_1\left\lVert w\right\rVert_\infty.
\end{equation}
If $w=v'$ with bounded $v',v''$, then
$A_r(y)=\int_\mathbf R q(x)v''(x+ry)\,\mathrm d x$.
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-41: body_md -->
For $f\in W^{1,1}(\mathbf R)$ and $a\in\mathbf R$, the absolutely continuous
representative satisfies
\[
 f(z-a)-f(z)=-a\int_0^1f'(z-\theta a)\,\mathrm d\theta,
 \qquad
 \left\lVert f(\cdot-a)-f\right\rVert_1\leq|a|\left\lVert f'\right\rVert_1.
\]
First, H\"older's inequality and translation invariance of the $L^1$ norm
give, for $y,\widetilde y\in\mathbf R$,
\[
 |A_r(y)|
 \leq\left\lVert q'(\cdot-ry)\right\rVert_1\left\lVert w\right\rVert_\infty =\left\lVert q'\right\rVert_1\left\lVert w\right\rVert_\infty.
\]
Then H\"older's inequality and the translation estimate above give
\[
 |A_r(y)-A_r(\widetilde y)|
 \leq\left\lVert w\right\rVert_\infty
       \left\lVert q'(\cdot-ry)-q'(\cdot-r\widetilde y)\right\rVert_1 \leq
       r|y-\widetilde y|\left\lVert q''\right\rVert_1\left\lVert w\right\rVert_\infty.
\]
For $w=v'$, weak integration by parts and $z=x+ry$ yield
\[
 \int_\mathbf R q(x)v''(x+ry)\,\mathrm d x
 =-\int_\mathbf R q'(x)v'(x+ry)\,\mathrm d x =-\int_\mathbf R q'(z-ry)v'(z)\,\mathrm d z =A_r(y).
\]
All products are integrable. A compactly supported cutoff justifies
integration by parts against the bounded, noncompact test $v'$.
<!-- end proof-41 -->
<!-- theorem-19: body_md -->
{sup:thm:factor-bound}
Under \eqref{sup:eq:semimartingale-integrability},
\eqref{sup:eq:factor-decomposition}--\eqref{sup:eq:factor-cost}, and
assumption~\ref{sup:ass:factor-regularity},
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:factor-bound}
 W_1\bigl(\operatorname{Law}(X_T^0),\operatorname{Law}(X_T^1)\bigr)
 \leq B\theta_0+C_{B,F}\mathcal D,
 \qquad
 C_{B,F}=B\max\left\{\frac12,\frac{2F}{3},
                       \frac12+\frac F3,\frac16\right\}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-19 -->
<!-- proof-42: body_md -->
Fix $h\in C_b^\infty(\mathbf R)$ with $\operatorname{Lip}(h)\leq1$ and put
$v(t)=P^1_{t,T}h$. Taylor's integral remainder gives
\[
 v(t,x+y)-v(t,x)-yv'(t,x)
 =y^2\Phi_{v(t),x}(y),\qquad
 \Phi_{v(t),x}(y)=\int_0^1(1-r)v''(t,x+ry)\,\mathrm d r.
\]
The jump term in \eqref{sup:eq:comparison} is therefore
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:factor-pairing}
 \int_0^T\sum_j\langle\Psi_{j,t},\kappa_{j,t}\rangle\,\mathrm d t,
 \qquad
 \Psi_{j,t}(y)=\mathbf E\left[
 r_j(t,X_t^0)\int_0^1(1-r)v''(t,X_t^0+ry)\,\mathrm d r\right].
\end{equation}
For $t\leq T/2$, Lemma~\ref{lem:Taylor} and
$\int_0^1(1-r)\,\mathrm d r=1/2$, $\int_0^1r(1-r)\,\mathrm d r=1/6$ give
\begin{align*}
 \left\lVert \Psi_{j,t}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}}
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:Phi-norm}}{\leq}\left\lVert r_j(t)\right\rVert_\infty
       \left(\frac12\left\lVert v''(t)\right\rVert_\infty
                    +\frac16\left\lVert v'''(t)\right\rVert_\infty\right)\\
 &\leq\frac{\left\lVert r_j(t)\right\rVert_\infty}{2}
               \bigl(\left\lVert v''(t)\right\rVert_\infty+\left\lVert v'''(t)\right\rVert_\infty\bigr) \overset{\eqref{sup:eq:factor-backward}}{\leq}\frac B2\left\lVert r_j(t)\right\rVert_\infty.
\end{align*}
For $T/2\leq t<T$, abbreviate $r_j=r_j(t,\cdot)$ and set
$q=p_tr_j\in W^{2,1}(\mathbf R)$. Since $\left\lVert p_t\right\rVert_1=1$,
\begin{align*}
 q'&=p_t'r_j+p_tr_j',&
 \left\lVert q'\right\rVert_1&\leq F\left\lVert r_j\right\rVert_\infty+\left\lVert r_j'\right\rVert_\infty,\\
 q''&=p_t''r_j+2p_t'r_j'+p_tr_j'',&
 \left\lVert q''\right\rVert_1&\leq F\left\lVert r_j\right\rVert_\infty
                  +2F\left\lVert r_j'\right\rVert_\infty+\left\lVert r_j''\right\rVert_\infty.
\end{align*}
Apply lemma~\ref{sup:lem:factor-transfer} with $w=v'(t)$ and integrate in $r$:
\[
 \left\lVert \Psi_{j,t}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}}
 \overset{\eqref{sup:eq:factor-transfer}}{\leq}\left\lVert v'(t)\right\rVert_\infty
        \int_0^1(1-r)\bigl(\left\lVert q'\right\rVert_1+r\left\lVert q''\right\rVert_1\bigr)\,\mathrm d r \overset{\eqref{sup:eq:factor-backward}}{\leq}
 B\left[\frac{2F}{3}\left\lVert r_j\right\rVert_\infty
       +\left(\frac12+\frac F3\right)\left\lVert r_j'\right\rVert_\infty
       +\frac16\left\lVert r_j''\right\rVert_\infty\right].
\]
On both time intervals,
$\left\lVert \Psi_{j,t}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}}\leq
 C_{B,F}\left\lVert r_j(t)\right\rVert_{W^{2,\infty}}$. Hence
\[
 \left|\int_0^T\sum_j\langle\Psi_{j,t},\kappa_{j,t}\rangle\,\mathrm d t\right|
 \leq C_{B,F}\int_0^T\sum_j
      \left\lVert r_j(t)\right\rVert_{W^{2,\infty}}
      \left\lVert \kappa_{j,t}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}^*}\,\mathrm d t=C_{B,F}\mathcal D.
\]
The initial term is bounded by
$\operatorname{Lip}(v(0))W_1(\operatorname{Law}(X_0^0),\operatorname{Law}(X_0^1))\leq B\theta_0$.
Lemma~\ref{lem:approximation} and the finite terminal first moments extend the estimate to
all one-Lipschitz tests. Kantorovich duality completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-42 -->
<!-- corollary-5: body_md -->
{sup:cor:factor-countable}
The same bound holds with $m=\infty$ if the series in
\eqref{sup:eq:factor-decomposition} converges in $\mathrm{BL}^*$ for almost every
$t$ and every $x$, and the series version of \eqref{sup:eq:factor-cost}
is integrable.
<!-- end corollary-5 -->
<!-- proof-43: body_md -->
For almost every fixed $t<T$, define the finite signed kernels
$K_{n,t}(x)=\sum_{j=1}^nr_j(t,x)\kappa_{j,t}$ and the summable majorant
\[
 S(t)=\sum_{j\geq1}\left\lVert r_j(t)\right\rVert_{W^{2,\infty}}
                       \left\lVert \kappa_{j,t}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}^*},
 \qquad S\in L^1(0,T).
\]
Since $\Phi_{v(t),x}\in\mathrm{BL}(\mathbf R)$, convergence in $\mathrm{BL}^*$ gives
$\langle\Phi_{v(t),x},K_{n,t}(x)\rangle
\to\langle\Phi_{v(t),x},K_t(x)\rangle$. Moreover,
\[
 |\langle\Phi_{v(t),x},K_{n,t}(x)\rangle|
 \leq\left(\frac12\left\lVert v''(t)\right\rVert_\infty
           +\frac16\left\lVert v'''(t)\right\rVert_\infty\right)S(t),
\]
so dominated convergence applies under $\operatorname{Law}(X_t^0)$. Writing the
resulting expectations as $I_n(t)\to I(t)$,
theorem~\ref{sup:thm:factor-bound} gives $|I_n(t)|\leq C_{B,F}S(t)$.
Applying dominated convergence once more gives
\[
 \int_0^TI(t)\,\mathrm d t=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_0^TI_n(t)\,\mathrm d t,
 \qquad
 \left|\int_0^TI(t)\,\mathrm d t\right|\leq C_{B,F}\mathcal D.
\]
The initial term is unchanged. We do not need convergence in total variation.
<!-- end proof-43 -->
<!-- theorem-20: body_md -->
{sup:thm:factor-intensity}
Let $X^i$ have generator \eqref{sup:eq:factor-generator} and initial law
in $\mathcal P_1(\mathbf R)$. For every $T>0$, a finite constant $C_T$, depending only
on $T$, the $C_b^3$ bounds of $c_i$, and $\int_{\mathbf R}|y|F_i(\,\mathrm d y)$, satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:factor-intensity-bound}
 W_1\bigl(\operatorname{Law}(X_T^0),\operatorname{Law}(X_T^1)\bigr) \leq C_T\bigl[\theta_0 +T\left\lVert c_0-c_1\right\rVert_{W^{2,\infty}}s_0 +T\left\lVert c_1\right\rVert_{W^{2,\infty}}d_{\mathrm{FM}}(\mu_0,\mu_1)\bigr].
\end{equation}
Discrete jump laws and nonnegative $C_b^3$ intensities are allowed. No
bound on $\left\lVert F_0-F_1\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}$ is required.
<!-- end theorem-20 -->
<!-- proof-44: body_md -->
We verify assumption~\ref{sup:ass:factor-regularity}. Omit the index $i$, let
$\mathrm{BUC}(\mathbf R)$ denote bounded uniformly continuous functions with the
supremum norm, and define
\[
 H_tf=f*g_t,\quad
 Rf=\int_\mathbf R[f(\cdot+y)-f]F(\,\mathrm d y),\quad Bf=cRf.
\]
On $\mathrm{BUC}(\mathbf R)$, $\left\lVert B\right\rVert\leq2\left\lVert c\right\rVert_\infty$. The absolutely
convergent heat perturbation series is
\[
 P_t=\sum_{n=0}^\infty P_t^{(n)},\quad P_t^{(0)}=H_t,\quad
 P_t^{(n+1)}=\int_0^tH_{t-s}BP_s^{(n)}\,\mathrm d s,\quad
 \left\lVert P_t^{(n)}\right\rVert\leq\frac{(2t\left\lVert c\right\rVert_\infty)^n}{n!}.
\]
It solves $P_t=H_t+\int_0^tH_{t-s}BP_s\,\mathrm d s$ and gives the semigroup
of the bounded-rate process.
For $h\in C_b^\infty(\mathbf R)$, put $w_s=DP_sh$,
$m_1=\int_\mathbf R|y|F(\,\mathrm d y)$, and
\[
 Jw=\int_\mathbf R\int_0^yw(\cdot+z)\,\mathrm d zF(\,\mathrm d y),\qquad
 \left\lVert Jw\right\rVert_\infty\leq m_1\left\lVert w\right\rVert_\infty.
\]
The inner integral is oriented when $y<0$. Since $Rf=J(Df)$,
$D(Rf)=R(Df)$, and $D(Jw)=J(Dw)$, differentiating the backward equation
gives
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:factor-gradient-equation}
 \partial_sw_s=\frac12D^2w_s+\mathcal Cw_s,
 \qquad \mathcal Cw=cRw+c'Jw.
\end{equation}
In particular,
\[
 D\mathcal Cw=c'Rw+cR(Dw)+c''Jw+c'J(Dw).
\]
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:factor-gradient-bounds}
 \left\lVert \mathcal Cw\right\rVert_\infty\leq K\left\lVert w\right\rVert_\infty,
 \qquad
 \left\lVert D\mathcal Cw\right\rVert_\infty
 \leq K'\bigl(\left\lVert w\right\rVert_\infty+\left\lVert Dw\right\rVert_\infty\bigr),
\end{equation}
where
\[
 K=2\left\lVert c\right\rVert_\infty+m_1\left\lVert c'\right\rVert_\infty,\qquad
 K'=\max\{K,2\left\lVert c'\right\rVert_\infty+m_1\left\lVert c''\right\rVert_\infty\}.
\]
Apply heat contraction to the mild form
$w_s=H_sh'+\int_0^sH_{s-r}\mathcal Cw_r\,\mathrm d r$.
Then the operator bound and Gronwall's inequality give
\[
 \left\lVert w_s\right\rVert_\infty
 \leq
     \left\lVert h'\right\rVert_\infty+\int_0^s\left\lVert \mathcal Cw_r\right\rVert_\infty\,\mathrm d r \overset{\eqref{sup:eq:factor-gradient-bounds}}{\leq}
     \left\lVert h'\right\rVert_\infty+K\int_0^s\left\lVert w_r\right\rVert_\infty\,\mathrm d r \leq e^{Ks}\left\lVert h'\right\rVert_\infty.
\]
Using $\left\lVert Dg_s\right\rVert_1=\sqrt{2/\pi}s^{-1/2}$ and
$\left\lVert D^2g_s\right\rVert_1=\sqrt{8/(\pi e)}s^{-1}$, distribute the derivatives as
\[
 Dw_s=(Dg_s)*h'+\int_0^s(Dg_{s-r})*\mathcal Cw_r\,\mathrm d r,\qquad D^2w_s=(D^2g_s)*h'
             +\int_0^s(Dg_{s-r})*D\mathcal Cw_r\,\mathrm d r.
\]
The corresponding bounds are
\begin{align*}
 \left\lVert Dw_s\right\rVert_\infty
 &\leq \sqrt{2/\pi}s^{-1/2}\left\lVert h'\right\rVert_\infty
      +\sqrt{2/\pi}K\int_0^s(s-r)^{-1/2}\left\lVert w_r\right\rVert_\infty\,\mathrm d r,\\
 \left\lVert D^2w_s\right\rVert_\infty
 &\leq \sqrt{8/(\pi e)}s^{-1}\left\lVert h'\right\rVert_\infty
      +\sqrt{2/\pi}K'\int_0^s(s-r)^{-1/2}
                  (\left\lVert w_r\right\rVert_\infty+\left\lVert Dw_r\right\rVert_\infty)\,\mathrm d r.
\end{align*}
For $0<s\leq T$, the first inequality gives
\[
 \left\lVert Dw_s\right\rVert_\infty
 \leq \sqrt{2/\pi}\bigl(s^{-1/2}+2Ke^{KT}\sqrt s\bigr)\left\lVert h'\right\rVert_\infty
 \leq A_Ts^{-1/2}\left\lVert h'\right\rVert_\infty,
 \quad A_T=\sqrt{2/\pi}(1+2KTe^{KT}).
\]
Substituting into the second inequality gives
\[
 \left\lVert D^2w_s\right\rVert_\infty
 \leq\bigl[\sqrt{8/(\pi e)}s^{-1}
       +\sqrt{2/\pi}K'(2e^{KT}\sqrt s+\pi A_T)\bigr]\left\lVert h'\right\rVert_\infty,
\]
because
\[
 \int_0^s(s-r)^{-1/2}\,\mathrm d r=2\sqrt s,\qquad
 \int_0^s(s-r)^{-1/2}r^{-1/2}\,\mathrm d r=\pi.
\]
Thus, with constants depending only on the stated parameters,
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:factor-semigroup-derivatives}
 \left\lVert DP_sh\right\rVert_\infty\leq C_T\operatorname{Lip}(h),\qquad
 \left\lVert D^2P_sh\right\rVert_\infty\leq C_Ts^{-1/2}\operatorname{Lip}(h),\qquad
 \left\lVert D^3P_sh\right\rVert_\infty\leq C_Ts^{-1}\operatorname{Lip}(h).
\end{equation}
These integrable bounds justify differentiating the series in the
distributional sense. Continuity of the heat convolutions gives classical derivatives.
For $v(t)=P_{T-t}h$, $T-t\geq T/2$ on the first half of the interval,
which verifies \eqref{sup:eq:factor-backward}.
For an arbitrary initial law $\zeta\in\mathcal P(\mathbf R)$, the forward series has
a density $p_t\geq0$ for $t>0$, with $\left\lVert p_t\right\rVert_1=1$ and
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:factor-forward-mild}
 p_t=H_t\zeta+\int_0^tH_{t-s}B^*p_s\,\mathrm d s,
 \qquad B^*p=(cp)*F-cp.
\end{equation}
Here $(u*F)(x)=\int_\mathbf R u(x-y)F(\,\mathrm d y)$ and
$H_t\zeta(x)=\int_\mathbf R g_t(x-z)\zeta(\,\mathrm d z)$. For $p\in W^{1,1}(\mathbf R)$,
\[
 D(B^*p)=(c'p+cp')*F-(c'p+cp'),
\]
so Young's inequality gives
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:factor-adjoint-bounds}
 \left\lVert B^*p\right\rVert_1\leq2\left\lVert c\right\rVert_\infty\left\lVert p\right\rVert_1,
 \qquad
 \left\lVert D(B^*p)\right\rVert_1
 \leq2\left\lVert c'\right\rVert_\infty\left\lVert p\right\rVert_1+2\left\lVert c\right\rVert_\infty\left\lVert p'\right\rVert_1.
\end{equation}
Differentiate \eqref{sup:eq:factor-forward-mild} as above. Young's
inequality, followed by \eqref{sup:eq:factor-adjoint-bounds}, yields
\begin{align*}
 \left\lVert p_t'\right\rVert_1
 &\leq\left\lVert Dg_t\right\rVert_1
       +\int_0^t\left\lVert Dg_{t-s}\right\rVert_1\left\lVert B^*p_s\right\rVert_1\,\mathrm d s\\
 &\overset{\eqref{sup:eq:factor-adjoint-bounds}}{\leq}
       \sqrt{2/\pi}t^{-1/2}
       +2\sqrt{2/\pi}\left\lVert c\right\rVert_\infty\int_0^t(t-s)^{-1/2}\,\mathrm d s\\
 &=\sqrt{2/\pi}t^{-1/2}
                     +4\sqrt{2/\pi}\left\lVert c\right\rVert_\infty\sqrt t \leq A_T^*t^{-1/2},
 \qquad A_T^*=\sqrt{2/\pi}(1+4T\left\lVert c\right\rVert_\infty).
\end{align*}
Using this estimate for the second derivative gives
\begin{align*}
 \left\lVert p_t''\right\rVert_1
 &\leq\left\lVert D^2g_t\right\rVert_1
       +\int_0^t\left\lVert Dg_{t-s}\right\rVert_1\left\lVert D(B^*p_s)\right\rVert_1\,\mathrm d s\\
 &\overset{\eqref{sup:eq:factor-adjoint-bounds}}{\leq}
       \sqrt{8/(\pi e)}t^{-1}
       +2\sqrt{2/\pi}\int_0^t(t-s)^{-1/2}
       (\left\lVert c'\right\rVert_\infty+\left\lVert c\right\rVert_\infty A_T^*s^{-1/2})\,\mathrm d s\\
 &=\sqrt{8/(\pi e)}t^{-1}
       +4\sqrt{2/\pi}\left\lVert c'\right\rVert_\infty\sqrt t
       +2\pi\sqrt{2/\pi}\left\lVert c\right\rVert_\infty A_T^* \leq C_Tt^{-1}.
\end{align*}
The integrable bounds justify the weak derivatives and imply
$p_t\in W^{2,1}(\mathbf R)$, even for singular $F$. On $[T/2,T]$ these bounds
give \eqref{sup:eq:factor-forward}.
Bounded intensities and $s_i<\infty$ give
\eqref{sup:eq:semimartingale-integrability}. In particular,
\[
 \mathbf E\left[\int_0^T\int_{\mathbf R} y^2\nu_i(X_t^i,\,\mathrm d y)\,\mathrm d t\right]
 \leq T\left\lVert c_i\right\rVert_\infty s_i<\infty.
\]
The backward equation and localized It\^o formula give the comparison
identity for smooth bounded tests. The path-supremum bound gives uniform
integrability of the stopped variables. Finally,
\[
 c_0\mu_0-c_1\mu_1=(c_0-c_1)\mu_0+c_1(\mu_0-\mu_1),
 \qquad \left\lVert \mu_0\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}^*}=\mu_0(\mathbf R)=s_0,
\]
and hence
\[
 \mathcal D\leq T\left\lVert c_0-c_1\right\rVert_{W^{2,\infty}}s_0
       +T\left\lVert c_1\right\rVert_{W^{2,\infty}}d_{\mathrm{FM}}(\mu_0,\mu_1).
\]
Apply theorem~\ref{sup:thm:factor-bound}.
<!-- end proof-44 -->
<!-- proposition-12: body_md -->
{sup:prop:factor-atom}
Let $X^{i,a}$ start at zero and have generator
$\mathcal L_i^af=\frac12f''+c\int_{\mathbf R}[f(\cdot+y)-f]F_i^a(\,\mathrm d y)$.
For every $T>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:factor-atom-response}
 \mathbf E\left[\sin X_T^{0,a}\right]-\mathbf E\left[\sin X_T^{1,a}\right]
    =\frac T4e^{-T/2}a^3+O_T(a^4).
\end{equation}
Consequently, there are $a_T>0$ and $0<c_T\leq C_T<\infty$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:factor-atom-order}
 c_Ta^3\leq W_1\bigl(\operatorname{Law}(X_T^{0,a}),\operatorname{Law}(X_T^{1,a})\bigr)
       \leq C_Ta^3,\qquad 0<a\leq a_T.
\end{equation}
The linear power of the weak jump-law discrepancy is therefore sharp
for this state-dependent family.
<!-- end proposition-12 -->
<!-- proof-45: body_md -->
The upper bound follows uniformly in $a$ from
theorem~\ref{sup:thm:factor-intensity} and
\eqref{sup:eq:factor-atom-discrepancies}. Put
$B_i^af=c\int_{\mathbf R}[f(\cdot+y)-f]F_i^a(\,\mathrm d y)$. Centering gives
\[
 B_i^af(x)=c(x)\int_{\mathbf R} y^2\int_0^1(1-r)f''(x+ry)\,\mathrm d rF_i^a(\,\mathrm d y).
\]
Leibniz's rule and $\int_{\mathbf R} y^2F_i^a(\,\mathrm d y)=a^2$ imply
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:factor-atom-operator}
 \left\lVert B_i^af\right\rVert_{C_b^k}\leq Ca^2\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C_b^{k+2}},
 \qquad k=0,2.
\end{equation}
On $\mathrm{BUC}^4(\mathbf R)$, the space of functions with bounded uniformly
continuous derivatives through order four, $\left\lVert B_i^a\right\rVert\leq C$
uniformly in $i,a$. Heat contraction and the perturbation series give
$\sup_{s\leq T,i,a}\left\lVert P_s^{i,a}\sin\right\rVert_{C_b^4}\leq C_T$.
Applying Duhamel's formula twice gives
\[
 P_T^{i,a}h
 =H_Th+\int_0^TH_tB_i^aH_{T-t}h\,\mathrm d t+\mathcal E_i^a(h),\qquad \mathcal E_i^a(h)
 =\int_0^T\int_0^{T-t}
       H_tB_i^aH_sB_i^aP_{T-t-s}^{i,a}h\,\mathrm d s\,\mathrm d t.
\]
For $h=\sin$, \eqref{sup:eq:factor-atom-operator} gives
\[
 \left\lVert H_tB_i^aH_sB_i^aP_{T-t-s}^{i,a}\sin\right\rVert_\infty
 \overset{\eqref{sup:eq:factor-atom-operator}}{\leq}
 Ca^2\left\lVert B_i^aP_{T-t-s}^{i,a}\sin\right\rVert_{C_b^2}
 \leq C_Ta^4,
\]
so $\left\lVert \mathcal E_i^a(\sin)\right\rVert_\infty\leq C_Ta^4$.
The third-order Taylor formula gives
\[
 B_i^af=\frac{a^2}{2}cf''
        +\frac16\left(\int_{\mathbf R} y^3F_i^a(\,\mathrm d y)\right)cf'''
        +\mathcal R_i^a(f),\quad
 \left\lVert \mathcal R_i^a(f)\right\rVert_\infty
 \leq\frac{\left\lVert c\right\rVert_\infty}{24}\left\lVert f^{(4)}\right\rVert_\infty
                       \int_{\mathbf R} y^4F_i^a(\,\mathrm d y).
\]
Since the fourth moments are $a^4$ and $13a^4/4$, respectively,
\[
 (B_0^a-B_1^a)f=-\frac14a^3cf'''+R_a(f),\qquad
 \left\lVert R_a(f)\right\rVert_\infty\leq Ca^4\left\lVert f^{(4)}\right\rVert_\infty.
\]
Now $H_{T-t}\sin=e^{-(T-t)/2}\sin$, $D^3\sin=-\cos$, and
\[
 H_t(c\cos)(0)=H_t\cos(0)+\frac12H_t(\sin\cos)(0)=e^{-t/2},
\]
because $g_t$ is even and $\sin\cos$ is odd. Subtracting the Duhamel
expansions and evaluating at zero gives
\[
 \mathbf E\left[\sin X_T^{0,a}\right]-\mathbf E\left[\sin X_T^{1,a}\right]
 =\frac{a^3}{4}\int_0^Te^{-(T-t)/2}e^{-t/2}\,\mathrm d t+O_T(a^4)
 =\frac T4e^{-T/2}a^3+O_T(a^4).
\]
Since $\operatorname{Lip}(\sin)=1$ and $Te^{-T/2}/4>0$, duality gives the lower
bound for sufficiently small $a$.
<!-- end proof-45 -->
<!-- theorem-21: body_md -->
{sup:thm:finite-split}
Interpolate the canonical terminal exponents
\[
 \Psi_i(\xi)=i\ell_i\xi-\frac12v_i\xi^2
           +\int_\mathbf R(e^{i\xi y}-1-i\xi\chi(y))N_i(\,\mathrm d y),
 \qquad \xi\in\mathbf R.
\]
Under \eqref{sup:eq:finite-integrability} and
Assumption~\ref{ass:interpolation} for these interpolation laws,
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:finite-split-bound}
 W_1\bigl(\operatorname{Law}(X_T^0),\operatorname{Law}(X_T^1)\bigr)
 \leq|\ell_0-\ell_1|+\frac{M_1}{2}|v_0-v_1|
         +\left(\frac{M_1}{2}+\frac{M_2}{6}\right)D_S+3D_L.
\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-21 -->
<!-- proof-46: body_md -->
The inequalities $|e^{iz}-1|\leq|z|$ and
$|e^{iz}-1-iz|\leq z^2/2$ give
\[
 |\Psi_i(\xi)|
 \leq\left(|\ell_i|+\int_{\{y\in\mathbf R:|y|>1\}}|y|N_i(\,\mathrm d y)\right)|\xi|
    +\frac12\left(v_i+\int_{\{y\in\mathbf R:|y|\leq1\}}y^2N_i(\,\mathrm d y)\right)|\xi|^2.
\]
Lemma~\ref{lem:interpolation} therefore applies to
$\Psi_u=(1-u)\Psi_1+u\Psi_0$, $0\leq u\leq1$.
Let $\rho_u\in W^{2,1}(\mathbf R)$ be the interpolation density,
$Q_uh(x)=\int_{\mathbf R} h(x+z)\rho_u(z)\,\mathrm d z$, and
$M_k=\int_0^1\left\lVert D^k\rho_u\right\rVert_1\,\mathrm d u$, $k=1,2$.
For $\operatorname{Lip}(h)\leq1$ and $q=Q_uh$, derivative transfer gives
\[
 \left\lVert q'\right\rVert_\infty\leq1,\quad
 \left\lVert q''\right\rVert_\infty\leq\left\lVert \rho_u'\right\rVert_1,\quad
 \left\lVert q'''\right\rVert_\infty\leq\left\lVert \rho_u''\right\rVert_1.
\]
The drift, Gaussian, and small-jump terms are therefore bounded as in
Theorem~\ref{thm:terminal} by
\[
 |\ell_0-\ell_1|+\frac12\left\lVert \rho_u'\right\rVert_1|v_0-v_1|
 +\left(\frac12\left\lVert \rho_u'\right\rVert_1
              +\frac16\left\lVert \rho_u''\right\rVert_1\right)D_S.
\]
For the large-jump term, define
$\Xi(y)=[q(x+y)-q(x)]/|y|$ on $|y|>1$. Then $|\Xi|\leq1$.
For $y,z$ in the same outer interval,
\[
 |\Xi(y)-\Xi(z)|
 \leq\frac{|y-z|+\bigl||z|-|y|\bigr|}{\min\{|y|,|z|\}}
 \leq2|y-z|.
\]
For opposite signs, $|\Xi(y)-\Xi(z)|\leq2\leq|y-z|$.
Extend $\Xi$ linearly from its boundary values at $-1$ and $1$.
The slope on $[-1,1]$ has absolute value at most one. The resulting continuous
extension satisfies $\left\lVert \Xi\right\rVert_\infty\leq1$ and $\operatorname{Lip}(\Xi)\leq2$,
so
\[
 \left|\int_{\{y\in\mathbf R:|y|>1\}}[q(x+y)-q(x)](N_0-N_1)(\,\mathrm d y)\right|
 =|\langle\Xi,\mu_0^L-\mu_1^L\rangle|\leq3D_L.
\]
Integrate in $u$. Finite terminal first moments allow us to approximate
all Lipschitz tests.
<!-- end proof-46 -->
<!-- theorem-22: body_md -->
{sup:thm:finite-continuous}
Assume \eqref{sup:eq:finite-integrability} and
Assumption~\ref{ass:interpolation} for the terminal laws
$\lambda_i=\operatorname{Law}(X_T^i)\in\mathcal P_1(\mathbf R)$.
With $C_w=10+\frac32M_1+\frac13M_2$,
\begin{align}
 W_1(\lambda_0,\lambda_1)
 &\leq|m_0-m_1|+\frac12M_1|v_0-v_1|
        +C_wd_{\mathrm{FM}}(\mu_0^w,\mu_1^w),
                      \label{sup:eq:finite-continuous-bound}\\
 W_1(\lambda_0,\lambda_1)
 &\leq|m_0-m_1|+C_wd_{\mathrm{FM}}(\Gamma_0^w,\Gamma_1^w).
                      \label{sup:eq:finite-continuous-joint}
\end{align}
The mean and fixed continuous weight make both bounds independent of the
drift truncation convention.
<!-- end theorem-22 -->
<!-- proof-47: body_md -->
For $f\in C^3(\mathbf R)$ with bounded first three derivatives and $x\in\mathbf R$,
define
\[
 \Theta_{f,x}\colon\left|\begin{aligned}
  \mathbf R&\longrightarrow \mathbf R\\
  y&\longmapsto \begin{cases}
   \dfrac{(1+|y|)[f(x+y)-f(x)-yf'(x)]}{y^2},&y\ne0,\\[4pt]
   \dfrac12f''(x),&y=0.
  \end{cases}
\end{aligned}\right.
\]
Put $L=\left\lVert f'\right\rVert_\infty$, $H_2=\left\lVert f''\right\rVert_\infty$, and
$H_3=\left\lVert f'''\right\rVert_\infty$. For $|y|\leq1$,
\[
 \Theta_{f,x}(y)=(1+|y|)\Phi_{f,x}(y),\qquad
 \Phi_{f,x}(y)=\int_0^1(1-r)f''(x+ry)\,\mathrm d r,
\]
so
\[
 \left\lVert \Theta\right\rVert_{L^\infty([-1,1])}\leq H_2,\qquad
 \operatorname{Lip}(\Theta|_{[-1,1]})
 \leq\left\lVert \Phi\right\rVert_\infty+2\operatorname{Lip}(\Phi)\leq\frac12H_2+\frac13H_3.
\]
For $|y|\geq1$, set $A(y)=[f(x+y)-f(x)]/y-f'(x)$. Then
\[
 |A(y)|\leq2L,\qquad
 A'(y)=\frac{yf'(x+y)-[f(x+y)-f(x)]}{y^2},\qquad
 |A'(y)|\leq\frac{2L}{|y|}.
\]
Since $\Theta=(1/y+\operatorname{sgn}y)A$,
\[
 |\Theta(y)|\leq4L,\qquad
 |\Theta'(y)|\leq\frac{2L}{y^2}
                +\left(1+\frac1{|y|}\right)\frac{2L}{|y|}
                \leq6L.
\]
The formulas agree at $\pm1$ and are continuous at zero. Splitting any
interval at $-1,0,1$ therefore gives
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:finite-continuous-test}
 \left\lVert \Theta_{f,x}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}}
 \leq\max\{H_2,4L\}+\max\{H_2/2+H_3/3,6L\}
 \leq10L+\frac32H_2+\frac13H_3.
\end{equation}
The full-compensation symbols satisfy
$|\Psi_i(\xi)|\leq C_i(|\xi|+|\xi|^2)$: use
$|e^{iz}-1-iz|\leq z^2/2$ for small jumps and
$|e^{iz}-1-iz|\leq2|z|$ for large jumps. Thus the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:interpolation}
applies. For $f=Q_uh$, Lemma~\ref{lem:convolution} gives
\[
 L\leq1,\quad H_2\leq\left\lVert \rho_u'\right\rVert_1,\quad
 H_3\leq\left\lVert \rho_u''\right\rVert_1.
\]
The operator difference pairs $\Theta_{Q_uh,x}$ with
$\mu_0^w-\mu_1^w$. Equation~\eqref{sup:eq:finite-continuous-test},
weak duality, and integration in $u$ give
\eqref{sup:eq:finite-continuous-bound}. Because
$\Theta_{f,x}(0)=f''(x)/2$, the identity
\[
 \frac12(v_0-v_1)f''(x)
  +\langle\Theta_{f,x},\mu_0^w-\mu_1^w\rangle
 =\langle\Theta_{f,x},\Gamma_0^w-\Gamma_1^w\rangle
\]
gives \eqref{sup:eq:finite-continuous-joint}. Finite terminal first
moments justify approximating Lipschitz tests in the last step.
<!-- end proof-47 -->
<!-- corollary-6: body_md -->
{sup:cor:stable-terminal}
Suppose the $i$th terminal exponent has an independent symmetric
$\beta$-stable factor with parameter $s_i>0$: subtracting $-s_i|\xi|^\beta$
leaves a L\'evy--Khintchine exponent satisfying the finite-first-moment
condition. Then theorems~\ref{sup:thm:finite-split} and~\ref{sup:thm:finite-continuous} hold
with
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:stable-terminal-constants}
 M_k\leq B_{k,\beta}\int_0^1
       \bigl((1-u)s_1+us_0\bigr)^{-k/\beta}\,\mathrm d u
 \leq B_{k,\beta}\min(s_0,s_1)^{-k/\beta},\qquad k=1,2.
\end{equation}
If $s_i=\int_0^Tc_i(t)\,\mathrm d t$, no pointwise positive lower bound on
$c_i(t)$ is needed.
<!-- end corollary-6 -->
<!-- proof-48: body_md -->
Set $s_u=(1-u)s_1+us_0$. The interpolated residual exponent defines
$\eta_u\in\mathcal P_1(\mathbf R)$. The convolution representation
$\rho_u=p_{s_u}^{(\beta)}*\eta_u$ implies
$D^k\rho_u=(D^kp_{s_u}^{(\beta)})*\eta_u$.
Young's inequality and the stable scaling identity then give
\[
 M_k=\int_0^1\left\lVert (D^kp_{s_u}^{(\beta)})*\eta_u\right\rVert_1\,\mathrm d u \leq\int_0^1\left\lVert D^kp_{s_u}^{(\beta)}\right\rVert_1\,\mathrm d u \overset{\eqref{sup:eq:stable-scaling}}{=}B_{k,\beta}\int_0^1s_u^{-k/\beta}\,\mathrm d u \leq B_{k,\beta}\min(s_0,s_1)^{-k/\beta},
\]
because $s_u\geq\min(s_0,s_1)>0$. Explicitly, for
$\alpha=k/\beta\ne1$,
\[
 \int_0^1s_u^{-\alpha}\,\mathrm d u=
 \begin{cases}
 \displaystyle\frac{s_0^{1-\alpha}-s_1^{1-\alpha}}
                    {(1-\alpha)(s_0-s_1)},&s_0\ne s_1,\\[6pt]
 s_0^{-\alpha},&s_0=s_1.
 \end{cases}
\]
<!-- end proof-48 -->
<!-- theorem-23: body_md -->
{sup:thm:stable-onesided}
Assume \eqref{sup:eq:finite-integrability},
\eqref{sup:eq:stable-onesided-smoothing}, and the corresponding
comparison identity. Suppose the initial points, canonical drifts, and
Gaussian coefficients agree. For $1\leq q\leq\infty$ and $J_{S,q}\leq1$,
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:stable-onesided-bound}
 W_1\bigl(\operatorname{Law}(X_T^0),\operatorname{Law}(X_T^1)\bigr)
 \leq C_{\beta,q,T}J_{S,q}^{\,1/(3-\beta+\beta/q)}+3\Theta_L,
\end{equation}
where $\beta/\infty=0$. The constant also depends on the smoothing
constant in \eqref{sup:eq:stable-onesided-smoothing}.
<!-- end theorem-23 -->
<!-- proof-49: body_md -->
Let $q'\in[1,\infty]$ satisfy $1/q+1/q'=1$, and set
\[
 \kappa=2-\beta/q'=2-\beta+\beta/q>0,\qquad
 K_3(r,s)=\min\{r^{-2},s^{-2/\beta}\},\quad K_2(r,s)=\sqrt{K_3(r,s)}.
\]
For $q'<\infty$, extending the integration interval to $(0,\infty)$
and splitting at $s=r^\beta$ gives
\begin{align*}
 \int_0^TK_3(r,s)^{q'}\,\mathrm d s
 &\leq\int_0^\infty\min\{r^{-2q'},s^{-2q'/\beta}\}\,\mathrm d s\\
 &=\int_0^{r^\beta}r^{-2q'}\,\mathrm d s
           +\int_{r^\beta}^\infty s^{-2q'/\beta}\,\mathrm d s \\&=
       r^{\beta-2q'}\left(1+\frac1{2q'/\beta-1}\right).
\end{align*}
Here $2q'/\beta>1$. Thus
$\left\lVert K_3(r,\cdot)\right\rVert_{L^{q'}(0,T)}\leq C_{\beta,q}r^{-\kappa}$.
For $q'=\infty$, the norm is $r^{-2}$ and $\kappa=2$.
Since $K_2\leq1+K_3$ and $r\leq1$, both kernels satisfy
\[
 \left\lVert K_j(r,\cdot)\right\rVert_{L^{q'}(0,T)}
 \leq C_{\beta,q,T}r^{-\kappa},\qquad j=2,3.
\]
The weighted Taylor test for small jumps, H\"older's inequality, and
$\left\lVert h-h_r\right\rVert_\infty\leq Cr$ yield
\begin{align*}
 |\mathbf E\left[h(X_T^0)\right]-\mathbf E\left[h(X_T^1)\right]|
 &\leq2Cr+C\int_0^Tr_S(t)
                 [K_2(r,T-t)+K_3(r,T-t)]\,\mathrm d t+3\Theta_L\\
 &\overset{\eqref{sup:eq:stable-onesided-smoothing}}{\leq}
 C_{\beta,q,T}(r+J_{S,q}r^{-\kappa})+3\Theta_L.
\end{align*}
As in theorem~\ref{sup:thm:finite-split}, the large-jump bound uses only
Lipschitz contraction. Take the supremum over $h$. For $J_{S,q}>0$,
choose $r=J_{S,q}^{1/(1+\kappa)}\leq1$. Then
$r=J_{S,q}r^{-\kappa}$ and
$1+\kappa=3-\beta+\beta/q$. If $J_{S,q}=0$, let $r\downarrow0$.
<!-- end proof-49 -->
<!-- proposition-13: body_md -->
{sup:prop:quantization}
Let $\lambda,\widehat\lambda\in\mathcal P_1(\mathbf R)$ be the laws of these
triplets. Then
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:quantization-certificate}
 W_1(\lambda,\widehat\lambda)
 \leq\left(\frac{\sqrt{2/\pi}}{2\sqrt v}+\frac{\sqrt{8/(\pi e)}}{6v}\right)
       \int_\mathbf R\frac{2|q(y)-y|}{2+|q(y)-y|}\mu(\,\mathrm d y).
\end{equation}
If $|q(y)-y|\leq\eta$ on $[-R,R]$, the integral is at most
\begin{equation}\label{sup:eq:quantization-tail}
 \eta\mu([-R,R])+2\mu(\{|y|>R\}).
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-13 -->
<!-- proof-50: body_md -->
The joint Gaussian and jump measures are
\[
 \Gamma=v\delta_0+\mu,\qquad
 \widehat\Gamma=\widehat v\delta_0+z^2\widehat N
                =v\delta_0+q_\#\mu.
\]
By Lemma~\ref{lem:atoms}, if $\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}}\leq1$ and $d=|q(y)-y|$, then
$|f(q(y))-f(y)|\leq2d/(2+d)$. Indeed, write
$A=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_\infty$, $L=\operatorname{Lip}(f)$ and $A+L\leq1$.
Using both bounds on the increments of $f$ and maximizing the resulting
piecewise linear expression gives
\[
 |f(q(y))-f(y)|
 \leq\min\{2A,Ld\} \leq\min\{2(1-L),Ld\} \leq\max_{0\leq\ell\leq1}\min\{2(1-\ell),\ell d\} =\frac{2d}{2+d}.
\]
Consequently, duality and the triangle inequality yield
\begin{align*}
 d_{\mathrm{FM}}(\Gamma,\widehat\Gamma)
 &\overset{\eqref{eq:metrics}}{=}
    \sup_{\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}}\leq1}
       \left|\int_\mathbf R[f(q(y))-f(y)]\mu(\,\mathrm d y)\right|\\
 &\leq\sup_{\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{BL}}\leq1}
       \int_\mathbf R|f(q(y))-f(y)|\mu(\,\mathrm d y) \\&\leq
       \int_\mathbf R\frac{2|q(y)-y|}{2+|q(y)-y|}\mu(\,\mathrm d y).
\end{align*}
The Gaussian variance stays at least $v$ throughout the interpolation. Corollaries~\ref{cor:joint} and~\ref{cor:Gaussian}
therefore give \eqref{sup:eq:quantization-certificate}. Splitting the
integral over $[-R,R]$ and its complement and using
$2d/(2+d)\leq\min\{d,2\}$ gives \eqref{sup:eq:quantization-tail}.
<!-- end proof-50 -->
